Nonlinear dynamic fracture problems with polynomial and strain-limiting constitutive relations Thanks: V. Patel is supported by the UK Engineering and Physical Sciences Research Council [EP/L015811/1].
Victoria Patel

Abstract
. We extend the framework of dynamic fracture problems with a phase-field approximation to the case of a nonlinear constitutive relation between the Cauchy stress tensor 𝐓 \mathbf{T} , linearised strain 𝜺 ⁡ ( 𝒖 ) \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}) and strain rate 𝜺 ⁡ ( 𝒖 t ) \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}) . The relationship takes the form 𝜺 ⁡ ( 𝒖 t ) + α ​ 𝜺 ​ ( 𝒖 ) = F ⁡ ( 𝐓 ) \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t})+\alpha\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u})=F(\mathbf{T}) where F F satisfies certain p p -growth conditions. We take particular care to study the case p = 1 p=1 of a ‘strain-limiting’ solid, that is, one in which the strain is bounded a priori . We prove the existence of long-time, large-data weak solutions of a balance law coupled with a minimisation problem for the phase-field function and an energy-dissipation inequality, in any number d d of spatial dimensions. In the case of Dirichlet boundary conditions, we also prove the satisfaction of an energy-dissipation equality.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
35M13, 35K99, 74D10, 74H20, 74R99.

Mathematical Institute, University of Oxford

Andrew Wiles Building, Woodstock Road

Oxford OX2 6GG, UK

1.
Introduction

In this work, we are interested in combining the theory of dynamic fracture with the theory of nonlinear, implicit constitutive relations. At present, mathematical analysis of dynamic fracture problems has only been performed in the setting of linear elasticity to the author’s knowledge. In this work, we aim to extend such fracture studies and examine time-dependent damage problems with a constitutive relation from nonlinear viscoelasticity. Of particular interest is the strain-limiting case, by which we mean that the linearised strain
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u})=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}+(\nabla\boldsymbol{u})^{\mathrm{T}})
is bounded
a priori
. A key motivation for studying such models is that such a defining relation ensures that no singularity is experienced in the strain. Indeed, we cannot contradict the small strain assumption, which is essential to the construction of the model here.
This contrasts the known results for damage models with a linear constitutive relation where a singularity occurs at the crack tip
[
37
]
.
In this section, we discuss the theory of implicit constitutive relations that was established by Rajagopal in his pioneering work
[
34
]
and a series of later papers, followed by an introduction to the theory of dynamic fracture based on the principles in
[
29
,
27
]
. Then we combine the two theories to obtain a fracture problem with a nonlinear constitutive relation between the Cauchy stress tensor, linearised strain tensor and strain rate.
We particularly emphasise the work in Section
3
where strain-limiting models are studied in the context of dynamic fracture problems, the state of the art in this work.

A framework for nonlinear, implicit constitutive relations in continuum mechanics was introduced by Rajagopal in his pioneering work
[
34
]
and a series of later papers. Although such relations had been studied previously, this was only in an
ad hoc
way. The novelty of
[
34
]
is the systematic and thermodynamically consistent structure that is available for the family of constitutive relations. These ideas are extended in
[
36
]
where nonlinear relationships between the Cauchy stress tensor
\mathbf{T}
, strain tensor
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
and strain rate
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
are justified in the small strain setting. Namely, we may consider the defining relation

(1.1)

\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u})=F(\mathbf{T}),

where
F
is a nonlinear, monotonic function from
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{sym}
to itself and
\alpha
is a positive constant. We let
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{2}((\nabla\cdot)+(\nabla\cdot)^{\mathrm{T}})
denote the symmetric gradient operator,
\boldsymbol{u}
is the displacement and
\mathbf{T}
is the Cauchy stress tensor. The relation (
1.1
) is a generalisation of the classical linear Kelvin–Voigt model for viscoelastic bodies.

A subclass of these models that we take particular care to study here is that of a strain-limiting solid. A strain-limiting constitutive relation ensures that the strain is bounded
a priori
, irrespective of the magnitude of the stress tensor.
The motivation to study such models is as follows.
It is well known that brittle materials can fracture in the small strain range under large stresses.
Furthermore, under the assumption that the strain is small, the mathematical model that results from the balance laws of momentum and mass for the deformation of the body under investigation can be linearised. As we are considering the motion of solids, it is sensible to assume that we are working in the small strain setting. Indeed, solids damage and break under sufficiently large strains.
Moreover, the linearisation procedure as a result of the small strain assumption simplifies the problem and makes it more amenable to mathematical analysis.
Hence, from now on, it is assumed that the small displacement gradient assumption holds, that is,

(1.2)

\sup_{(t,x)\in Q}|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(t,x)|=O(\delta),

for a
\delta\ll 1
, where
Q
is the space-time domain. It follows from (
1.2
) that the linearised strain
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u})
as defined above must be at most of order
\delta
.

Now, consider a body with a crack which is possibly growing due to an external force acting on the material.
In the case of a linear constitutive relation, the strain has magnitude of order
r^{-\frac{1}{2}}
where
r
is the distance to the crack tip
[
37
]
. The strain experiences a singularity at the crack tip, contradicting the standing assumption that the strain is small. A more reliable model could be one in which the magnitude of the strain is bounded
a priori
so such contradictions cannot arise.

This motivates studying the constitutive relation

(1.3)

\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u})=F(\mathbf{T}):=\frac{\mathbf{T}}{(1+|\mathbf{T}|^{a})^{\frac{1}{a}}},

for fixed parameter
a>0
.
Other choices for the function
F
are available, but for simplicity of the presentation, we make this specific choice as suggested in
[
10
,
35
]
. For further details of the possible generalisations, we refer to the remark in Section
3
.

The defining relation (
1.3
) is coupled with the balance law

(1.4)

\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}=\mathrm{div}(\mathbf{T})+\boldsymbol{f},

where
\boldsymbol{f}
represents the external force, alongside suitable boundary and initial conditions.
The balance law comes from considering the balance of linear momentum under (
1.2
). The balance of angular momentum reduces to
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}^{\mathrm{T}}
, which is naturally included in (
1.3
). Due to the assumption (
1.2
), we assume that the density is constant in time and so we do not include a balance of mass.
For further details on the relevant kinematics and the derivation of this problem, we refer to
[
9
]
and the references therein. We note that the strain-limiting problem (
1.3
), (
1.4
) has been recently studied on domains without a crack.
For the analysis of the spatially periodic problem, we refer to
[
9
]
and for a general domain with Dirichlet boundary conditions, we mention
[
8
]
.

In this paper, we consider (
1.3
), (
1.4
) in the context of dynamic fracture.
Hence, before proceeding further, we give a brief introduction to the modern theory of fracture.
The principles that drive the mathematical analysis of fracture in brittle bodies can be traced back to the seminal work of Griffith
[
24
]
. In the quasi-static setting
1
1

1

By quasi-static setting, we mean that the intertial term
\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}
is negligible in the balance of momentum equation (
1.4
).
, he stipulated that a crack can only grow if the increase in surface energy is exactly equal to the resulting decrease in elastic potential energy.
In other words, the newly created area is proportional to the loss of stored energy.
In the anti-plane setting
2
2

2

By anti-plane setting, we mean that, in three spatial dimensions, the displacement
\boldsymbol{u}
takes the particular form
\boldsymbol{u}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=(0,0,u(x_{1},x_{2}))
.
with a linear relationship between the stress and strain, we write this mathematically as

(1.5)

\frac{\mu}{2}\int_{\Omega\setminus C(t)}|\nabla u(t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+G_{c}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(C(t))=\frac{\mu}{2}\int_{\Omega\setminus C(0)}|\nabla u(0)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+G_{c}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(C(0)),

where
C(t)\subset\Omega
is the crack set,
\mu
is the elasticity constant and
G_{c}
is the fracture toughness.
We use
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
to denote the
(d-1)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure.
The first term in (
1.5
) corresponds to the elastic energy and the second term to the surface energy. This formulation was first introduced by Francfort and Marigo
[
22
]
, based on the Mumford–Shah functional that is used in image segmentation
[
30
]
.

Alongside this energy balance, the balance of linear momentum in the quasi-static setting must hold. We formulate this as a variational problem. Indeed, the couple
(u(t),C(t))
must satisfy

(1.6)

\mathcal{A}(u(t),C(t)):=\frac{\mu}{2}\int_{\Omega\setminus C(t)}|\nabla u(t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+G_{c}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(C(t))\leq\frac{\mu}{2}\int_{\Omega\setminus\tilde{C}}|\nabla\tilde{u}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+G_{c}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\tilde{C}),

for every suitable comparison couple
(\tilde{u},\tilde{C})
with
C(t)\subset\tilde{C}
.
The principle of irreversibility is also required to hold, that is, the crack is non-decreasing in time, so
C(s)\subset C(t)
for every
s<t
. Problems of the form (
1.5
), (
1.6
), where we work directly with the crack set, are called sharp interface problems.
Mathematical analysis of this anti-plane shear problem took place in
[
22
]
, with higher dimensional settings treated in
[
17
]
and
[
21
]
. Both the crack set and displacement are treated as unknowns in these works. This causes significant analytical difficulties. Indeed, the analysis takes places on the set
\cup_{t\geq 0}\{t\}\times(\Omega\setminus C(t))
, which is not only a domain that changes in time, but is
a priori
unknown because the crack set
(C(t))_{t\geq 0}
is unknown.

An approach to avoid working on time dependent cracking domains is to approximate the crack set by a phase-field function. The Ambrosio–Tortorelli functional
[
1
,
2
]
takes the following form in the context of quasi-static fracture for linear elasticity:

(1.7)

\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon}(u,v)=\frac{\mu}{2}\int_{\Omega}(v^{2}+\eta_{\epsilon})|\nabla u|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+G_{c}\int_{\Omega}\epsilon|\nabla v|^{2}+\frac{1}{4\epsilon}(1-v)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x,

where
\epsilon>0
is an approximation parameter,
\eta_{\epsilon}
is a numerical stability parameter such that
0<\eta_{\epsilon}\ll\epsilon
, and
v
is the unknown phase-field function, also referred to in the literature as the material parameter. The function
v
approximates the crack set in the sense that
v
takes values close to
1
away from the crack and values close to
0
near the crack.
Although
\epsilon
acts as a length scale for the approximation,
we must be careful not to assign a physical meaning to this parameter
[
5
]
. For the mathematical analysis of this approximation in the quasi-static setting, we refer to
[
26
]
and
[
23
]
, for example.

The approximation (
1.7
) is a sensible choice because
\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon}
converges to the sharp-interface functional
\mathcal{A}
in the sense of
\Gamma
-convergence. We refer to
[
6
]
for the details but the essence is as follows. Let
((u^{\epsilon},v^{\epsilon}))_{\epsilon}
be a sequence such that
v^{\epsilon}
minimises
v\mapsto\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon}(u^{\epsilon},v)
over suitable test functions
v\leq v_{\epsilon}
at every time. Then there exists a subsequence in
\epsilon
and a solution couple
(u,C)
to (
1.5
), (
1.6
) such that, for a.e. time
t
, we have

\displaystyle v^{\epsilon}\nabla u^{\epsilon}(t)\rightarrow\nabla u(t)\quad\text{ strongly in }L^{2}(\Omega)^{d},

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\int_{\Omega}((v^{\epsilon})^{2}+\eta_{\epsilon})|\nabla u^{\epsilon}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\int_{\Omega}\epsilon|\nabla v|^{2}+\frac{1}{4\epsilon}(1-v)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(C(t)).

Hence, the sequence of solutions for the approximate problem represents an approximation to the solution of the sharp-interface functional.

We remark that there are other choices of approximation available. For example, the fourth order functional

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}(u,v)=\frac{\mu}{2}\int_{\Omega}(v^{2}+\eta_{\epsilon})|\nabla u|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{G_{c}}{4}\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{\epsilon}(1-v)^{2}+2\epsilon|\nabla v|^{2}+\epsilon^{3}|\Delta v|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x,

is investigated in
[
4
]
and
[
31
]
. The functional
\Gamma
-converges to
\mathcal{A}
. An advantage of this choice is that the higher order spatial derivatives improve the convergence rate of numerical solutions. In particular, when studying these fracture problems from the point of view of computational analysis, we note that the Ambrosio–Tortorelli functional is not necessarily the best choice of approximation for the sharp-interface functional.

The aforementioned literature all concern the quasi-static problem. Here, we are interested in the adaptation of Griffith’s theory to the dynamic problem, that is, we do not assume that the term
\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}
is negligible. An extension was first introduced by Mott
[
29
]
and relies on the following three principles:

(i)

elastodynamics, the balance of linear momentum should hold away from the crack set;

(ii)

energy balance, an energy-dissipation balance that includes the kinetic energy should hold;

(iii)

irreversibility, the crack cannot heal itself in time.

However, this is not sufficient for a well-posed problem. The case of a stationary crack should be ruled out. The following additional principle was introduced by Larsen
[
27
]
:

(iv)

maximal dissipation, that is, if the crack can grow while satisfying (i)–(iii), then it should.

As with the quasi-static problem, the study of dynamic fracture problems broadly follows one of two approaches: working directly in the sharp-interface setting, or an approximation by means of a phase-field function, generally based on the Ambrosio–Tortorelli functional. The focus in this paper is on the use of a phase-field approximation, thus avoiding the technicalities of working in a time dependent domain. However, for completeness, we first discuss the known results in the sharp-interface case.

For sharp-interface problems, the analysis is significantly more complicated compared to the quasi-static model due to the presence of the intertial term. Hence, a first step in the analysis is to solve the elastodynamic equation for an unknown displacement when the crack set
(C(t))_{t>0}
is known
a priori
. We look for a function
\boldsymbol{u}
such that

(1.8)

\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}=\mathrm{div}\big(\mathbb{E}(t,x)\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\big)+\boldsymbol{f}(t,x)\quad\text{ in }\Omega\setminus C(t),\quad t\in[0,T],

where
\mathbb{E}
is the elasticity tensor, given a sequence of subsets
(C(t))_{t\geq 0}
of
\Omega
satisfying some regularity requirements. In an attempt to better understand (
1.8
), in
[
14
]
for the scalar-valued case and
[
38
]
for the vector-valued case, the wave equation

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}-\Delta\boldsymbol{u}-\gamma\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{t}=\boldsymbol{f}

is studied, where
\gamma\in\{0,1\}
. The system is damped if
\gamma=1
and undamped if
\gamma=0
. The viscous term from the damping provides a certain smoothing effect. A result of this is that, if
\gamma=1
, uniqueness and an energy-dissipation balance can be shown. However, uniqueness and satisfaction of an appropriate energy-dissipation balance is an open problem in the undamped setting.
We note that in both
[
14
]
and
[
38
]
, the existence proofs use a discretisation in the time variable. This is the standard approach to tackle such problems, and we use a similar approach here.

However, different approaches to prove the existence of solutions are available under stronger regularity requirements on the crack set. In
[
32
]
,
[
16
]
and, for the vector-valued case,
[
11
]
, a co-ordinate change is used to transform the problem from one on
\cup_{t\geq 0}\{t\}\times(\Omega\setminus C(t))
, a cracking domain, to one on the reference domain
[0,\infty)\times(\Omega\setminus C(0))
. A solution is found on this reference domain and it is shown that the existence of such a solution to the transformed problem is equivalent to the existence of a solution to the original problem on the cracking domain.

The next step in the analysis is to deal with the case where both the crack set and displacement are
a priori
unknown. As far as the author is aware, the only known result in the multi-dimensional setting is in
[
15
]
. Under some assumptions on the regularity and geometry of the crack set, the authors show that among all possible couples
(u(t),C(t))_{t\geq 0}
, there exists one which satisfies the maximal dissipation condition. There is no damping term present, which is an advantage of the analysis compared to the aforementioned works. Although a significant amount remains unknown, this work suggests that the maximal dissipation condition is the correct principle to impose on the dynamic problem.

An important aspect of all of the aforementioned literature is that only linear elasticity is considered, that is, a linear relationship between the Cauchy stress tensor and linearised strain. This leads to a significant gap in the literature. Indeed, to the author’s knowledge, there has been no study of dynamic fracture problems in the context of nonlinear constitutive relations.
In this work, we aim to extend some of the known results to such models, considering both a general case and that of a strain-limiting solid. To avoid the technicalities that come with the sharp-interface model, we consider a dynamic problem with a phase-field approximation based on the Ambrosio–Tortorelli functional. A regularised model of the type that we study in this work was first introduced in
[
5
,
27
,
28
]
. The formulation is based on the principles of elastodynamics, energy-balance and irreversibility, but not maximal dissipation as in
[
15
]
.
This is due to the fact that maximal dissipation is naturally guaranteed by the formulation. Indeed, the minimisation problem (
1.10
) introduced below ensures that the ‘crack’ grows whenever it is possible for it to do so.

In
[
28
]
, the authors solve the equation

(1.9)

\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}=\mathrm{div}\big((v^{2}+\eta_{\epsilon})\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{u}_{t})\big)+\boldsymbol{f}\quad\text{ in }(0,T)\times\Omega,

subject to the couple
(\boldsymbol{u},v)
solving the minimisation problem

(1.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}(t),v(t))

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}(v(t)^{2}+\eta_{\epsilon})\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u})\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{4\epsilon}(1-v(t))^{2}+\epsilon|\nabla v(t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\inf_{v\leq v(t)}\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}(t),v).

The first integral on the right-hand side of (
1.10
) represents an approximation of the elastic energy and the second is an approximation of the surface energy, with fixed approximation parameter
\epsilon
.
The minimisation problem is identical to that for the quasi-static model. In the literature, it is sometimes referred to as unilateral minimality.

We note that an issue occurs as a result of the minimisation problem when taking the limit in the approximation parameter
\epsilon
.
As stated in
[
28
]
, “the unilateral minimality of the
v
variable has no obvious counterpart in a sharp-interface model.”
This means that there is nothing ensuring maximal dissipation in the limiting model.
Another deficiency with the model (
1.9
), (
1.10
) is the presence of the viscous term
\boldsymbol{u}_{t}
. When taking the limit in the approximation parameter, the viscoelastic paradox can occur
[
13
]
, that is, the only possible solution of the corresponding sharp-interface problem is constant in time. However, in such dynamic fracture models, our interest lies in non-stationary solutions. Hence this paradox is a potential deficiency of the damping term in (
1.9
). In the problems that we study here, we include a damping term. It is out of the scope of the paper to remove this term but will be the focus of future work.

Such a shortcoming is overcome in
[
12
]
where the author considers a variation of (
1.9
), (
1.10
) that has no such damping term in (
1.9
).
Rather than include a dissipative term in (
1.9
), a rate-dependent term is included in the minimisation problem (
1.10
). The author of
[
12
]
considers the balance law

(1.11)

\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}=\mathrm{div}\big((v^{2}+\eta_{\epsilon})\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u})\big)+\boldsymbol{f}

coupled with the rate-dependent minimisation problem

(1.12)

\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}(t),v(t))+(v_{t}(t),v(t))_{k,2}=\inf_{v\leq v(t)}\Big\{\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}(t),v)+(v_{t}(t),v)_{k,2}\Big\},

where
\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon}
is as in (
1.10
) and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{k,2}
is the standard inner product in the Sobolev space
H^{k}(\Omega)
.
For every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, it is shown that a weak solution to (
1.11
), (
1.12
) exists such that it satisfies an energy-dissipation inequality. However, if
k>\frac{d}{2}
, it is possible to prove that the energy-dissipation inequality is in fact an equality at every point in time. This is essentially a consequence of the Sobolev embedding theorem, from which we know that
H^{k}(\Omega)
is continuously embedded into
C(\overline{\Omega})
for
k>\frac{d}{2}
.

The proof of the existence of solutions to the dynamic problem in both
[
28
]
and
[
12
]
involves introducing a discretisation in the time variable. The time-discrete formulation involves an alternating procedure. The elastodynamic equation is solved for the displacement given the phase-field function from the previous time step. The minimisation problem for an unknown phase-field function is then solved, given the displacement function from the aforementioned step. A similar procedure is used here.

A key novelty of the presentation in Section
2
is the formulation of the problem and the extension of the analysis of the linear case to a model of nonlinear viscoelasticity with a Kelvin–Voigt rheology, based on the implicit constitutive framework discussed previously.
In particular, we note the derivation of bounds on the sequence of solutions to the time discrete problem. This demonstrates the highly nonlinear nature of the problem.
However, the case of the strain-limiting material is the true highlight of this work. We make a significant step towards the use of strain-limiting constitutive relations in sharp-interface fracture models, which is the ultimate goal.

The nonlinear nature of the problem adds significant analytical challenges to problem, compared with the equivalent problem in the linear setting. Not only does the problem require careful formulation, but we work hard to obtain various
a priori
bounds and to improve the regularity of approximate solution sequences. In the strain-limiting case, we have the added complication that the stress tensor
\mathbf{T}
is only bounded
a priori
in
L^{1}(Q)^{d\times d}
. It is well known that this space has poor compactness properties. Indeed, at best we can obtain weak-* convergence in the space of Radon measures on
\overline{Q}
. However, we want to stay out of the space of measure-valued solutions. We use careful manipulations to improve the regularity of the approximations of the stress which allow the deduction of a pointwise convergence result, despite the poor integrability. This is later improved to strong convergence in
L^{1}(0,T;L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
and we prove that the limit is exactly the solution that we are looking for.

There are two main results that we present in this work. The first, presented in Section
2
, concerns a constitutive relation of the form

(1.13)

\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u})=F(\mathbf{T}):=|\mathbf{T}|^{p-2}\mathbf{T},

where
p>1
. When
p\in(1,2]
, we prove the existence of a weak solution
(\boldsymbol{u},v)
to the elastodynamic equation (
1.4
) coupled with a minimisation problem analogous to (
1.10
) and an energy-dissipation balance at every point in time. For
p>2
, we also obtain an existence result. However, the energy-dissipation balance only holds at almost every point in time. This results from a lack of regularity of the phase-field function.

The second result, presented in Section
3
, concerns strain-limiting solids with the constitutive relation (
1.3
). The elastodynamic equation (
1.4
) is coupled with the defining relation (
1.3
), a rate-dependent minimisation problem of the type (
1.12
) and an energy-dissipation balance.
We supplement this with mixed Dirichlet–Neumann boundary conditions. We restrict ourselves to
k>\frac{d}{2}+1
to ensure good regularity of the phase-field function. This allows the derivation of higher regularity estimates on approximations of the displacement and stress tensor.

If the Neumann part of the boundary is non-empty, due to a lack of global estimates on the stress tensor, the elastodynamic equation is shown to hold weakly up to a penalisation term on the Neumann part of the boundary.
Indeed, the only global convergence result for the approximations of the stress tensor is in the set of measures on
\overline{Q}
due to the lack of weak compactness in the Lebesgue space
L^{1}(Q)
.
An important consequence of this is that we are only able to show that an energy-dissipation inequality holds. However, if we have a fully Dirichlet boundary condition, we have no such penalisation term and are able to obtain a full existence result. Indeed, a weak form of (
1.4
) holds, the constitutive relation is satisfied pointwise a.e., the minimisation problem holds at every point in time and we have an energy-dissipation equality. If additionally
a\in(0,\frac{2}{d})
, we also have
\mathbf{T}\in L^{1+\delta}(0,T;L^{1+\delta}_{loc}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
for every positive
\delta
such that
a+\delta<\frac{2}{d}
.

The structure of the remainder of the paper is as follows. In Section
1.1
, we state the relevant notation and background results. In Section
2
, we study the nonlinear fracture problem for a constitutive relation of the form (
1.13
). The case
p\in(1,2]
is dealt with first. The existence of a solution is shown by means of a discretisation in time and semi-discretisation in space. In particular, we consider a Galerkin approximation in space with respect to the displacement variable
\boldsymbol{u}
. Next, we adapt this work to the case
p\in(2,\infty)
. We must use the monotonicity of the approximations of the phase-field function to overcome the lack of uniform bounds on the time derivatives of these functions. In Section
3
, we study the strain-limiting problem with constitutive relation (
1.3
). The proof uses a discretisation in time with an elliptic regularisation. We obtain suitable weighted regularity estimates on the sequence of approximations
(\mathbf{T}^{n})_{n}
of the stress tensor
(\mathbf{T}^{n})_{n}
to overcome the poor integrability properties of the sequence. These estimates are used to prove the pointwise convergence of this sequence to a limit
\mathbf{T}
, which we show to be a part of the required solution triple
(\boldsymbol{u},\mathbf{T},v)
. The climax of this work in the proof of a full existence result under Dirichlet boundary conditions and a partial existence result when the Neumann part of the boundary is non-empty.

1.1.
Notation and auxiliary results

Throughout this paper,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes a bounded domain with Lipschitz boundary
\partial\Omega
. The boundary has a Dirichlet part
\Gamma_{D}
and a Neumann part
\Gamma_{N}
. These are relatively open but disjoint subsets of
\partial\Omega
such that
\overline{\Gamma_{D}\cup\Gamma_{N}}=\partial\Omega
. We always assume that
\Gamma_{D}\neq\emptyset
.
We let
\mathbf{n}
denote the outward unit normal to a point on the boundary
\partial\Omega
.
We fix the spatial dimension
d\geq 2
and a final time horizon
T\in(0,\infty)
. We denote the space-time domain by
Q=(0,T)\times\Omega
.

We use
W^{k,p}(\Omega)
to denote the standard Sobolev space for
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
p\in[1,\infty]
with the usual norm
\|\cdot\|_{k,p}
.
When considering
W^{k,p}(\Omega_{0})
for a subdomain
\Omega_{0}\subset\Omega
, we write
\|\cdot\|_{W^{k,p}(\Omega_{0})}
.
We write
C^{\infty}_{D}(\overline{\Omega})
for the set of functions
h\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
such that
h=0
on
\Gamma_{D}
and let
W^{k,p}_{D}(\Omega)
denote the closure of
C^{\infty}_{D}(\overline{\Omega})
with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{k,p}
for
p\in[1,\infty)
. We define
W^{k,\infty}_{D}(\Omega):=W^{k,\infty}(\Omega)\cap W^{k,1}_{D}(\Omega)
.

We define
C^{1}(\partial\Omega)^{d}
to be the set of continuously differentiable functions on
\partial\Omega
such that there exists an extension to the space
C^{1}(\overline{\Omega})^{d}
. The norm on
C^{1}(\partial\Omega)^{d}
is defined by

\|\boldsymbol{w}\|_{C^{1}(\partial\Omega)}=\inf\big\{\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}\|_{1,\infty}\,:\,\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}\in C^{1}(\overline{\Omega})^{d},\,\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}=\boldsymbol{w}\text{ on }\partial\Omega\big\}.

We define
C^{1}_{0}(\Gamma_{N})^{d}
to be the subset of those functions
\boldsymbol{w}\in C^{1}(\partial\Omega)^{d}
such that
\boldsymbol{w}
vanishes on
\overline{\Gamma_{D}}
. By Theorem 1 of
[
19
]
, we know that there exists a linear map
E:C^{1}(\partial\Omega)\rightarrow C^{1}(\overline{\Omega})
such that
T\boldsymbol{w}=\boldsymbol{w}
on
\partial\Omega
for every
\boldsymbol{w}\in C^{1}(\partial\Omega)^{d}
. Furthermore, there exists a constant
C
, depending only on
\Omega
, such that

(1.14)

\|T\boldsymbol{w}\|_{1,\infty}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{w}\|_{C^{1}(\partial\Omega)}.

The restriction of
T
to the space
C^{1}_{D}(\overline{\Omega})^{d}
maps into
C^{1}_{0}(\Gamma_{N})^{d}
by definition of the latter function space. Moreover, for every
\boldsymbol{w}\in C^{1}_{0}(\Gamma_{N})^{d}
, there exists an extension
\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}\in C^{1}_{D}(\overline{\Omega})^{d}
such that (
1.14
) holds. This property is vital when dealing with the penalisation term in Section
3
on the Neumann part of the boundary.

If
\mathcal{F}
is a function space of real-valued functions with norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{F}}
, we let
\mathcal{F}^{d}
denote the set of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued functions such that each co-ordinate is an element of
\mathcal{F}
. The norm is given by
\||\cdot|\|_{\mathcal{F}}
, where
|\cdot|
is the Euclidean norm, but is denoted by
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{F}}
with an abuse of notation. The meaning is to be understood from the context. We define
\mathcal{F}^{d\times d}
analogously, but with
|\cdot|
meaning the Frobenius norm.

For a Banach space
X
, we use
L^{q}(0,T;X)
to denote the standard Bochner space with norm denoted
\|\cdot\|_{L^{q}(0,T;X)}
. Furthermore,
L^{\infty}_{w^{*}}(0,T;X^{*})
denotes the set of functions
h:[0,T]\rightarrow X^{*}
such that

t\mapsto\langle h(t),x\rangle_{(X^{*},X)}
is measurable on
[0,T]
for every element
x\in X
and there exists a constant
c
, independent of
x
, such that

|\langle h(t),x\rangle_{(X^{*},X)}|\leq c\|x\|_{X}\quad\text{ for a.e. }t\in[0,T].

By Theorem 10.1.16 of
[
33
]
, for example,
L^{\infty}_{w^{*}}(0,T;X^{*})
is the dual space of
L^{1}(0,T;X)
. In particular, bounded subsets of
L^{\infty}_{w^{*}}(0,T;X^{*})
are weakly-* compact by the Banach–Alaoglu theorem.

Let
H^{k}_{D}(\Omega)
be the set of functions
h\in W^{k,2}(\Omega)
such that
h=0
on
\Gamma_{D}
.
We use
H^{k}_{D+1}(\Omega)
to denote the set of
h\in W^{k,2}(\Omega)
such that
h=1
on
\Gamma_{D}
.
The duals of
H^{1}_{D}(\Omega)
and
W^{1,p}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
are denoted by
H^{-1}_{D}(\Omega)
and
W^{-1,p^{\prime}}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
, respectively, where
p^{\prime}=\frac{p}{p-1}
is the Hölder conjugate of
p
.

We use lower case letters to denote scalar-valued functions, bold lower case letters for vector-valued functions and bold upper case letters for tensor-valued functions. The differential operator
\nabla
denotes the gradient operator so, if
\boldsymbol{u}
is an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued function and
\mathbf{T}
is an
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
-valued function, we have

\nabla\boldsymbol{u}=(\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{j})_{i,j=1}^{d},\quad\nabla\mathbf{T}=(\partial_{k}T_{ij})_{i,j,k=1}^{d}.

The divergence operator
\mathrm{div}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}(\boldsymbol{u})=\frac{\partial u_{i}}{\partial x_{i}},\quad\mathrm{div}(\mathbf{T})=\Big(\frac{\partial T_{ij}}{\partial x_{j}}\Big)_{j=1}^{d},

assuming Einstein’s summation convention.
Throughout this paper, we fix positive problem parameters
\alpha
,
\epsilon
and
\eta
, where
\alpha
is the inverse of the viscosity coefficient,
\epsilon
is the approximation level for the phase-field function and
\eta
is a stability coefficient. In practice,
\eta\ll\epsilon\ll 1
but this is not required for the results here.

We require certain properties of convex conjugate functions. In this paper, we define the function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
by

(1.15)

\varphi(\mathbf{T})=\frac{|\mathbf{T}|^{p}}{p},\quad\text{ and }\quad\varphi(\mathbf{T})=\int_{0}^{|\mathbf{T}|}\frac{t}{(1+t^{a})^{\frac{1}{a}}}\,\mathrm{d}t,

in Section
2
and Section
3
, respectively.
Given one of these choices, we further define

\displaystyle F(\mathbf{T})=\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial\mathbf{T}}(\mathbf{T})=\Big(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial T_{ij}}(\mathbf{T})\Big)_{i,j=1}^{d},\quad\varphi^{*}(\mathbf{T})=\sup_{\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}}\Big\{\mathbf{T}:\mathbf{S}-\varphi(\mathbf{S})\Big\},

the derivative and convex conjugate, respectively. On the image of
F
over
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, we can show that

\frac{\partial\varphi^{*}}{\partial\mathbf{T}}=F^{-1}
. For every
\mathbf{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
such that the statements make sense, we have

(1.16)

\mathbf{T}:F(\mathbf{T})=\varphi(\mathbf{T})+\varphi^{*}(F(\mathbf{T})),\quad\mathbf{T}:F^{-1}(\mathbf{T})=\varphi(F^{-1}(\mathbf{T}))+\varphi^{*}(\mathbf{T}).

Given the choices in (
1.15
), we notice that
F(\mathbf{T})=|\mathbf{T}|^{p-2}\mathbf{T}
and
F(\mathbf{T})=\frac{\mathbf{T}}{(1+|\mathbf{T}|^{a})^{\frac{1}{a}}}
, respectively.

We require the following result in the analysis of the strain-limiting problem. The proof can be found in
[
7
]
.

Lemma 1.1
.

For every
y\geq 0
and
a>0
, we have

\displaystyle\min\{1,2^{-1+\frac{1}{a}}\}(1+y)\leq(1+y^{a})^{\frac{1}{a}}\leq\max\{1,2^{-1+\frac{1}{a}}\}(1+y).

A key tool in the deduction of various
a priori
estimates and uniform bounds on the sequences of approximate solutions is the following variation of the Korn–Poincaré inequality for a partly homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition. The result follows from Theorem 4 in
[
25
]
, combined with an application of the Poincaré inequality.

Theorem 1.1

(Korn–Poincaré inequality)
.

Let
\Omega
,
\Gamma_{D}
and
\Gamma_{N}
be as above with
\Gamma_{D}\neq\emptyset
. For every
p\in(1,\infty)
, there exists a constant
C
depending only on
\Omega
,
\Gamma_{D}
and
p
such that, for every
\boldsymbol{u}\in W^{1,p}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
,

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{1,p}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u})\|_{p}.

2.
Nonlinear fracture problems with growth

In this section, we study nonlinear dynamic fracture problems with growth in the constitutive relation, by which we mean that the strain is not
a priori
bounded.
This is the first step in the analysis of strain-limiting fracture problems because we want to understand nonlinear dynamic fracture problems. However, we start with this growth setting so that we gain some understanding of the problem formulation without the additional difficulty of the lack of integrability of the stress tensor that we have in the case that
F
is bounded.

We consider the following problem: find a triple
(\boldsymbol{u},\mathbf{T},v):Q\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\times\mathbb{R}
such that

(2.1)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{div}\big(b(v)\mathbf{T}\big)+\boldsymbol{f}

\displaystyle\text{ in }Q,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u})

\displaystyle=F(\mathbf{T}):=|\mathbf{T}|^{p-2}\mathbf{T}

\displaystyle\text{ in }Q,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}\big|_{\Gamma_{D}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{0},\,v\big|_{\Gamma_{D}}=1

\displaystyle\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Gamma_{D},

\displaystyle b(v)\mathbf{T}\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{g}

\displaystyle\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Gamma_{N},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{t}(0,\cdot)=\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\,v(0,\cdot)=v_{0}

\displaystyle\text{ in }\Omega,

subject to the minimisation problem

(2.2)

\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}(t),v(t))+\mathcal{H}(v(t))=\inf\Big\{\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}(t),v)+\mathcal{H}(v)\,:\,v\in H^{1}_{D+1}(\Omega),\,v\leq v(t)\Big\},

and the energy-dissipation equality

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(t;\boldsymbol{u}(t),\boldsymbol{u}_{t}(t),v(t))+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}b(v)\big[F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}))-F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}))\big]:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t})+\boldsymbol{f}_{t}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma_{N}}\boldsymbol{g}_{t}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(0;\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{1},v_{0}),

for every
t\in[0,T]
, alongside the crack non-healing property
v_{t}\leq 0
. The functionals
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{H}
denote the total, elastic and surface energy, respectively, and are defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(t;\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{w},v)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{K}(\boldsymbol{w})+\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u},v)+\mathcal{H}(v)-\langle l(t),\boldsymbol{u}\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u},v)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\frac{b(v)}{\alpha}\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}))\,\mathrm{d}x,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(v)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\epsilon}\|1-v\|_{2}^{2}+\epsilon\|\nabla v\|_{2}^{2}.

We define the functionals
\mathcal{K}
and
l
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(\boldsymbol{w})

\displaystyle=\frac{\|\boldsymbol{w}\|_{2}^{2}}{2},

\displaystyle\langle l(t),\boldsymbol{u}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{f}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma_{N}}\boldsymbol{g}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\,\mathrm{d}S,

representing the kinetic energy and external force, respectively.
To ensure that the minimisation problem holds at the initial time
t=0
, we demand the compatibility condition

(2.4)

\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0},v_{0})+\mathcal{H}(v_{0})=\inf\Big\{\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0},v)+\mathcal{H}(v)\,:\,v\in H^{1}_{D+1}(\Omega),\,v\leq v_{0}\Big\}.

The function
b:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined by

(2.5)

b(v)=v^{2}+\eta.

The stability parameter
\eta
is required so that we do not experience degeneracy in the elastodynamic equation (
2.1
)
1
at points where the phase-field function
v
vanishes.

We remark that
the functional
\mathcal{A}(v):=\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u},v)+\mathcal{H}(v)
is well-defined on
H^{1}_{D+1}(\Omega)
, provided that we have
\boldsymbol{u}\in W^{1,p^{\prime}}(\Omega)^{d}
, which we later show to be the case for solutions of (
2.1
)–(
2.3
). However, the functional
\mathcal{A}
may take the value positive infinity when testing against functions that are not also elements of
L^{\infty}(\Omega)
.

In this section we consider mixed Dirichlet–Neumann boundary conditions. We assume a homogeneous boundary condition on the Dirichlet part for simplicity of the presentation. However, it is not too difficult to adapt the arguments to allow for inhomogeneous boundary conditions. We refer to
[
8
]
, for example, for details on how to do this.

Now we state the main results for this section and have a brief discussion regarding the methods used in the proofs. There are two main results in this section concerning the existence of weak energy solutions to (
2.1
)–(
2.3
), that is, a weak solution satisfying an energy-dissipation balance. We differentiate between the cases
p\in(1,2]
and
p\in(2,\infty)
. This is due to a technical argument that allows good time regularity of the phase-field function in the former case, but not necessarily the latter. We remark that uniqueness remains an open problem for dynamic fracture models and is out of the scope of this paper. Our focus is on the existence of solutions. The two main results are as follows.

Theorem 2.1
.

Suppose that
p\in(1,2]
,
l\in C^{1}([0,T];W^{-1,p}_{D}(\Omega)^{d})
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in W^{1,p^{\prime}}(\Omega)^{d}
, and
v_{0}\in H^{1}_{D+1}(\Omega)
such that
v_{0}\in[0,1]
a.e. in
\Omega
and the compatibility condition (
2.4
) holds.
There exists a weak energy solution
(\boldsymbol{u},\mathbf{T},v)
of problem (
2.1
)–(
2.3
) in the following sense with

•

\boldsymbol{u}\in W^{2,2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)^{d})\cap W^{1,\infty}(0,T;W^{1,p^{\prime}}_{D}(\Omega)^{d})
,

•

\mathbf{T}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
,

•

v\in W^{1,\infty}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega))
with
v(t)\in H^{1}_{D+1}(\Omega)
for every
t\in[0,T]
.

The weak elastodynamic equation holds,

(2.6)

\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{w}+b(v(t))\mathbf{T}(t):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{w})\,\mathrm{d}x=\langle l(t),\boldsymbol{w}\rangle,

for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
and every
\boldsymbol{w}\in W^{1,p^{\prime}}_{D}(\Omega)
, the constitutive relation
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u})=F(\mathbf{T})
is satisfied pointwise a.e. in
Q
, the minimisation problem (
2.2
) holds for every
t\in[0,T]
and the energy-dissipation equality (
2.3
) is satisfied for every
t\in[0,T]
. We have the non-healing property
v_{t}\leq 0
pointwise a.e. in
Q
. The initial conditions are satisfied in the sense that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 0+}\big[\|\boldsymbol{u}(t)-\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{1,p^{\prime}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{t}(t)-\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\|_{2}+\|v(t)-v_{0}\|_{1,2}\big]=0.

Theorem 2.2
.

Suppose that
p\in(2,\infty)
,
l\in C^{1}([0,T];W^{-1,p}_{D}(\Omega)^{d})
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in W^{1,p^{\prime}}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}\cap L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
, and
v_{0}\in H^{1}_{D+1}(\Omega)
such that
v_{0}\in[0,1]
a.e. in
\Omega
and the compatibility condition (
2.4
) holds. There exists a weak energy solution
(\boldsymbol{u},\mathbf{T},v)
of (
2.1
)–(
2.3
) with regularity

•

\boldsymbol{u}\in W^{2,2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)^{d})\cap W^{1,\infty}(0,T;W^{1,p^{\prime}}_{D}(\Omega)^{d})
,

•

\mathbf{T}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
,

•

v:[0,T]\rightarrow H^{1}_{D+1}(\Omega)
with
v\in L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega))
and
v
is continuous a.e. as a map from
[0,T]
to
L^{2}(\Omega)
,

solving the problem (
2.1
)–(
2.3
) in the following sense.
The weak elastodynamic equation (
2.6
) holds for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
and every test function
\boldsymbol{w}\in W^{1,p^{\prime}}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}\cap L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
, we have the constitutive relation
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u})=F(\mathbf{T})
pointwise a.e. in
Q
, the minimisation problem (
2.2
) is satisfied for every
t\in[0,T]
, and the energy-dissipation equality (
2.3
) holds for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
. Furthermore,
v(t)\leq v(s)
a.e. in
\Omega
, for every
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
. The initial conditions hold in the sense that
v(0)=v_{0}
and

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 0+}\big[\|\boldsymbol{u}(t)-\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{1,p^{\prime}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{t}(t)-\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\|_{2}\big]=0.

Both proofs follow the same steps initially. A two-level approximation is introduced with parameters
(N,M)
, where
N
refers to a discretisation in space for the displacement variable and
M
is a time discretisation parameter.
For notational simplicity, we denote
\beta=(N,M)
.
We let
M\rightarrow\infty
, followed by the limit as
N\rightarrow\infty
. It is in the latter limit where we need to differentiate between the cases
p\in(1,2]
and
p\in(2,\infty)
.
Hence, we focus first on proving Theorem
2.1
. In Section
2.5
, we present the required changes for the proof of Theorem
2.2
.

The two-level approximation suggests how the analysis could be adapted when considering the problem in a numerical setting and using, for example, finite-element approximations. However, we choose Galerkin approximations with very high regularity to simplify the analysis when taking the limit as
M\rightarrow\infty
.
In particular, we are able to avoid technical arguments such as those in Section 3.6 of
[
28
]
.

We structure the proof of Theorem
2.1
in the following way. First, we introduce the two-level approximation and in Theorem
2.3
prove the existence of unique solutions to the time-discrete Galerkin approximation. In Propositions
2.2
and
2.3
, we show that the solution sequence satisfies discrete counterparts of the minimisation problem (
2.2
) and the energy-dissipation equality (
2.3
). Then we derive various
M
-independent bounds, which allow us to take the limit as
M\rightarrow\infty
. We obtain a weak-energy solution of a time-continuous Galerkin approximation of (
2.1
)–(
2.3
). We derive various
N
-independent bounds on this solution. Due to the growth of the function
F
, the solution of the time-continuous Galerkin approximation experiences good integrability and regularity. Hence, we can take the limit as
N\rightarrow\infty
and use standard compactness arguments to obtain a weak energy solution of (
2.1
)–(
2.3
) in the sense of Theorem
2.1
. We remark that, out of the time-discrete setting, we are unable to make any claims regarding the uniqueness of solutions.

For the discretisation in space, let
(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
be an orthogonal basis of
W^{k,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
such that it is orthonormal in
L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
and
k>\frac{d}{2}+1
. We make this choice so that
W^{k,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
embeds continuously into
C^{1}(\overline{\Omega})^{d}
, by the Sobolev embedding theorem. For details of how to obtain such a basis, we refer to
[
20
]
and the references therein. We define
V_{N}=\mathrm{span}\{\boldsymbol{\phi}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\phi}_{N}\}
and let
P_{N}:L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}\rightarrow V_{N}
denote the orthogonal projection operator. By construction, there exists a constant
C>0
, independent of
N
, such that

\|P_{N}\boldsymbol{u}\|_{k,2}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{k,2}\quad\text{ for every }\boldsymbol{u}\in W^{k,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}.

In this finite-dimensional setting, for every
q\in[1,\infty]
,
\|\cdot\|_{q}
and
\|\cdot\|_{1,q}
are norms on
V_{N}
that are equivalent to the usual norm
\|\cdot\|_{V_{N}}:=\|\cdot\|_{k,2}
, with the constants of equivalence depending on
N
.

Now we define the time discrete, finite-dimensional problem. Let
\beta=(N,M)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
with
V_{N}
defined as above. We define the time step
h=\frac{T}{M}
and, for every
0\leq m\leq M
, we set
t^{\beta}_{m}=mh
. For problem data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}
,
v_{0}
,
\boldsymbol{f}
and
\boldsymbol{g}
satisfying the assumptions of Theorem
2.1
, we define an initialisation for the
\beta
-approximation of (
2.1
)–(
2.3
) by

(2.7)

\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{0}=\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N},\quad\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{-1}=\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N}-h\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N},\quad v^{\beta}_{0}=v^{N}_{0},

where
v^{N}_{0}\in H^{1}_{D+1}(\Omega)
is the solution of the minimisation problem

(2.8)

\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N},v^{N}_{0})+\mathcal{H}(v^{N}_{0})=\inf\Big\{\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N},v)+\mathcal{H}(v):v\leq v_{0},v\in H^{1}_{D+1}(\Omega)\Big\}.

We impose (
2.8
) so that, at the level of the approximate problem, the minimisation problem is satisfied at
t=0
. The function
v^{N}_{0}
exists and is unique, applying the direct method in the calculus of variations and strict convexity.
The functions
\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N}\in W^{k,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
are approximations of the initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}
, respectively, and are chosen depending on the value of
p
.

In the case that
p\in(1,2]
, we choose the approximations such that
\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N}\in V_{N}
and, for every
N
,

\displaystyle 0=\lim_{\tilde{N}\rightarrow\infty}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\tilde{N}}\|_{1,p^{\prime}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{1,\tilde{N}}\|_{1,p^{\prime}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N}\|_{1,p^{\prime}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N}\|_{1,p^{\prime}}\leq C,

where
C=C(\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{1})
depends only on the initial data.
Existence of such a construction is deduced as follows. The space
\cup_{N\geq 1}V_{N}
is dense in
W^{k,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
so, by the Sobolev embedding theorem, it must be dense in
W^{1,p^{\prime}}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
for any
p^{\prime}\in(1,\infty)
.
Furthermore,
V_{N}
is a closed subset of
W^{1,p^{\prime}}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
. Hence for every
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}\in W^{1,p^{\prime}}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
, there exists a
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}\in V_{N}
such that

\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{N}\|_{1,p^{\prime}}=\inf_{\boldsymbol{v}_{N}\in V_{N}}\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}-\boldsymbol{v}_{N}\|_{1,p^{\prime}}\leq\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}\|_{1,p^{\prime}}.

By density, we must have

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\inf_{\boldsymbol{v}_{N}\in V_{N}}\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}-\boldsymbol{v}_{N}\|_{1,p^{\prime}}=0.

Thus we conclude the existence of such approximation sequences for
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}
as above. Furthermore, we note that because
p^{\prime}\geq 2
, it follows that the sequences
(\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N})_{N}
and
(\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N})_{N}
are bounded in
L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
and converge strongly in this space to
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}
, respectively, whenever
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in W^{1,p^{\prime}}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
.

In the case that
p>2
, we must choose the sequences in such a way so that they approximate the data in both
L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
and
W^{1,p^{\prime}}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
.
This is due to the fact that
W^{1,p^{\prime}}_{D}(\Omega)
is not necessarily embedded in
L^{2}(\Omega)
, unlike the case that
p\leq 2
.
We replace
\|\cdot\|_{1,p^{\prime}}
in the above by
\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}\|_{*}:=\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}\|_{2}+\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}\|_{1,p^{\prime}}
and repeat the same arguments. Hence, given
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}\cap W^{1,p^{\prime}}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
, we can find
\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N}\in V_{N}
such that

\displaystyle 0=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{i}-\boldsymbol{u}_{i,N}\|_{2}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{i}-\boldsymbol{u}_{i,N}\|_{1,p^{\prime}}\ \leq\|\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\|_{2}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\|_{1,p^{\prime}}\quad\text{ for }i\in\{1,2\}.

The
\beta
-approximation problem is defined recursively as follows. For every
1\leq m\leq M
, we look for a function
\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}\in V_{N}
such that

(2.9)

\int_{\Omega}\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}+b(v^{\beta}_{m-1})F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{w})\,\mathrm{d}x=\langle l^{\beta}_{m},\boldsymbol{w}\rangle,

for every
\boldsymbol{w}\in V_{N}
, where
l^{\beta}_{m}:=l(t^{\beta}_{m})
. Next we look for a function
v^{\beta}_{m}\in H^{1}_{D+1}(\Omega)
that solves the minimisation problem

(2.10)

\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m},v^{\beta}_{m})+\mathcal{H}(v^{\beta}_{m})=\inf\Big\{\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m},v)+\mathcal{H}(v)\,:\,v\in H^{1}_{D+1}(\Omega),\,v\leq v^{\beta}_{m-1}\Big\}.

We use
\delta
and
\delta^{2}
to denote the first and second order difference quotients, respectively, by which we mean

(2.11)

\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}:=\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}-\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1}}{h},\quad\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}:=\frac{\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}-\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1}}{h}=\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}-2\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1}+\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-2}}{h^{2}}.

We define
\delta v^{\beta}_{m}
and
\delta l^{\beta}_{m}
analogously.

We remark that the function
F
is a bijection from
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
to itself so we can formulate the problem in terms of only the displacement and the phase-field function. We reintroduce the stress function later when taking the limit as
N\rightarrow\infty
, to simplify the notation.

Theorem 2.3
.

Suppose that
l\in C^{1}([0,T];W^{-1,p}_{D}(\Omega)^{d})
with
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in W^{1,p^{\prime}}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}\cap L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
and
v_{0}\in H^{1}_{D+1}(\Omega)
such that
v_{0}\in[0,1]
a.e. in
\Omega
.
For every
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, defining the initial data by (
2.7
), there exists a unique sequence of solutions
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m},v^{\beta}_{m})_{m=1}^{M}
solving the time discrete problem (
2.10
), (
2.11
).

Existence follows in a standard way from the Browder–Minty theorem for (
2.10
) and the direct method in the calculus of variations for (
2.11
) so we do not include the details. Uniqueness is a result of strict monotonicity and strict convexity, respectively.

Before proceeding any further, we note the following convergence result for the sequence
(v^{N}_{0})_{N}
. This is vital knowledge when we take the limit as
N\rightarrow\infty
. It enables us to prove the satisfaction of the energy-dissipation equality when taking the limit in
N
.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
v_{0}\in H^{1}_{D+1}(\Omega)
with
v_{0}\in[0,1]
a.e. in
\Omega
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in W^{1,p^{\prime}}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}\cap L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
such that the compatibility condition (
2.4
) holds. Let
v^{N}_{0}\in H^{1}_{D+1}(\Omega)
be the solution of the minimisation problem (
2.8
).
Then
v^{N}_{0}\in[0,1]
a.e. in
\Omega
and we have
v^{N}_{0}\rightarrow v_{0}
strongly in
H^{1}(\Omega)
as
N\rightarrow\infty
.

Proof.

First, we notice that, comparing
v
with
\max\{v,0\}
, any minimiser of (
2.8
) must be non-negative. By construction, we have
v_{0}^{N}\leq v_{0}\leq 1
. Hence
(v^{N}_{0})_{N}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}(\Omega)
. Next, by construction, we have

\displaystyle C(\epsilon)\|v^{N}_{0}\|_{1,2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N},v_{0}^{N})+\mathcal{H}(v^{N}_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N},v_{0})+\mathcal{H}(v_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{b(1)}{\alpha}\int_{\Omega}\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N}))\,\mathrm{d}x+C(\epsilon)\|v_{0}\|_{1,2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\big[\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N})\|_{p^{\prime}}^{p^{\prime}}+\|v_{0}\|_{1,2}^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\leq C\big[\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{2}^{p^{\prime}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{1,p^{\prime}}^{p^{\prime}}+\|v_{0}\|_{1,2}^{2}\big].

It follows that
(v^{N}_{0})_{N}
converges weakly in
H^{1}(\Omega)
and strongly in
L^{2}(\Omega)
to a limit
\tilde{v}_{0}\in H^{1}_{D}(\Omega)
with
\tilde{v}_{0}\leq v_{0}
, up to a subsequence that we do not relabel. It follows from Fatou’s lemma and weak lower semi-continuity that

(2.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\tilde{v}_{0})+\mathcal{H}(\tilde{v}_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\Big[\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N},v_{0}^{N})+\mathcal{H}(v^{N}_{0})\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\Big[\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N},v_{0})+\mathcal{H}(v_{0})\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0},v_{0})+\mathcal{H}(v_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\tilde{v}_{0})+\mathcal{H}(\tilde{v_{0}}).

The final inequality follows from the compatibility condition (
2.4
). Hence
\tilde{v}_{0}
is also a minimiser of the problem (
2.4
). By strict convexity, we deduce that
v_{0}=\tilde{v}_{0}
.

Next, we notice that
(\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N},v^{N}_{0}))_{N}
converges to
\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0},v_{0})
as a result of the dominated convergence theorem and the uniform boundedness of
(b(v^{N}_{0}))_{N}
. Inserting this into (
2.12
), it follows that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{H}(v^{N}_{0})=\mathcal{H}(v_{0}).

The functional
\mathcal{H}
is equivalent to the usual norm on
H^{1}(\Omega)
. Thus
(v^{N}_{0})_{N}
converges weakly and in norm to
v_{0}
and so we have strong convergence.
∎

Now we prove that the sequence of solutions satisfies properties related to the minimisation problem, and an inequality analogous to the energy-dissipation equality for the time continuous solution. The first result is an immediate consequence of the minimisation problem (
2.11
). However, the formulation presented in Proposition
2.2
is more amenable when it comes to taking the limit as
M\rightarrow\infty
. Corollary
2.1
follows from Proposition
2.2
, which is vital in proving uniform bounds on the sequence
(\delta v^{\beta}_{m})_{m=1}^{M}
. In Proposition
2.3
, we prove an energy-dissipation inequality in the spirit of (
2.3
). We are not able to obtain an equality in this setting due to the presence of numerical dissipation terms resulting from the time discretisation. We later use this inequality to obtain one direction of an energy-dissipation equality for the semi-discrete couple that is constructed by taking the limit in
M
.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
2.3
hold. Then
v^{\beta}_{m}\in[0,1]
and
\delta v^{\beta}_{m}\leq 0
a.e. in
\Omega
, for every
1\leq m\leq M
. Furthermore, for every
\tilde{\chi}\in H^{1}_{D+1}(\Omega)
such that
\tilde{\chi}\leq v^{\beta}_{m-1}
, we have

(2.13)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\big[\partial_{v}\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m},v^{\beta}_{m})+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v^{\beta}_{m})\big](\tilde{\chi}-v^{\beta}_{m})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\frac{b^{\prime}(v^{\beta}_{m})}{\alpha}(\tilde{\chi}-v^{\beta}_{m})\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}))+\frac{1}{2\epsilon}(v^{\beta}_{m}-1)(\tilde{\chi}-v^{\beta}_{m})+2\epsilon\nabla v^{\beta}_{m}\cdot\nabla(\tilde{\chi}-v^{\beta}_{m})\,\mathrm{d}x.

In particular, for every
\chi\in H^{1}_{D}(\Omega)
such that
\chi\leq 0
,

(2.14)

0\leq\big[\partial_{v}\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m},v^{\beta}_{m})+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v^{\beta}_{m})\big](\chi).

Corollary 2.1
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
2.3
hold. For every
1\leq m\leq M
, we have

\displaystyle 0=\big[\partial_{v}\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m},v^{\beta}_{m})+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v^{\beta}_{m})\big](\delta v^{\beta}_{m}).

Proof.

Using (
2.8
), we see that (
2.14
) also holds for
m=0
.
Reasoning as in the proof of Lemma 3.2 of
[
28
]
, we immediately obtain the following. Indeed, we test in (
2.13
) against
\tilde{\chi}=v^{\beta}_{m-1}
for “
\geq
” and against
\tilde{\chi}=2v^{\beta}_{m}-v^{\beta}_{m-1}
for the opposite direction “
\leq
”.
∎

Proposition 2.3

(Discrete energy-dissipation inequality)
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
2.3
hold. The sequence of solutions to (
2.10
), (
2.11
) satisfies, for every
1\leq m\leq M
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(t^{\beta}_{m};\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m},\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m},v^{\beta}_{m})+h\sum_{j=1}^{m}\langle\delta l^{\beta}_{j},\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{j}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+h\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int_{\Omega}b(v^{\beta}_{j-1})\big[F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{j}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{j}))-F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}))\big]:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{F}(0;\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N},\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N},v^{N}_{0}).

Proof.

We test in the elastodynamic equation (
2.10
) against
h\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}
. The interial term, that is, the one involving
\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}
, can be rewritten as

(2.15)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}\cdot h\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\big(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}-\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1}\big)\cdot\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}-\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}-\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1}\|_{2}^{2}\Big),

as a result of the well-known relation

(2.16)

\mathbf{a}\cdot(\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{b})=\frac{1}{2}\big(|\mathbf{a}|^{2}-|\mathbf{b}|^{2}+|\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{b}|^{2}\big)\quad\text{ for all }\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

For the term involving the external force, we write

(2.17)

\langle l^{\beta}_{m},h\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}\rangle=\langle l^{\beta}_{m},\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}\rangle-\langle l^{\beta}_{m-1},\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1}\rangle+h\langle\delta l^{\beta}_{m},\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1}\rangle.

For the term involving the nonlinearity, we must be more careful. First, we write

(2.18)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}b(v^{\beta}_{m-1})F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(h\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=h\int_{\Omega}b(v^{\beta}_{m-1})\big[F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}))-F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}))\big]:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle+\int_{\Omega}b(v^{\beta}_{m-1})F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(h\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m})\,\mathrm{d}x.

The first term on the right-hand side of (
2.18
) is in the form that we want.
For the second term, using that
F^{-1}
is the derivative of
\varphi^{*}
, we get

(2.19)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}b(v^{\beta}_{m-1})F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(h\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}b(v^{\beta}_{m-1})F^{-1}(\alpha\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(s\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}-\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1})\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle+\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}b(v^{\beta}_{m-1})\big[F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}))-F^{-1}(\alpha\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(s\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1}))\big]:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}-\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1})\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}b(v^{\beta}_{m-1})F^{-1}(\alpha\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(s\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}-\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1})\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\frac{b(v^{\beta}_{m})}{\alpha}\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}))-\frac{b(v^{\beta}_{m-1})}{\alpha}\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1}))+\frac{b(v^{\beta}_{m-1})-b(v^{\beta}_{m})}{\alpha}\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}))\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m},v^{\beta}_{m})-\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1},v^{\beta}_{m-1})+\mathcal{H}(v^{\beta}_{m})-\mathcal{H}(v^{\beta}_{m-1}),

where the transition to the final line follows from the minimisation problem (
2.10
).
Summing (
2.15
), (
2.17
), (
2.18
) and (
2.19
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(t^{\beta}_{m};\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m},\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m},v^{\beta}_{m})+h\langle\delta l^{\beta}_{m},\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+h\int_{\Omega}b(v^{\beta}_{m-1})\big[F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}))-F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}))\big]:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{F}(t^{\beta}_{m-1};\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1},\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1},v^{\beta}_{m-1}).

Using this inequality recursively, we obtain the required result.
∎

2.1.
M
-independent
a priori
bounds

Now we focus on finding estimates on the sequence of solutions to the time discrete problem that are independent of
M
. This is so that we can take the limit as
M\rightarrow\infty
and obtain sufficiently strong convergence results. All constants may depend on the data and parameters of the original problem, but we specify the dependence on
N
if it is present. The first bound is an immediate corollary of the energy-dissipation inequality.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
2.3
hold and suppose that (
2.4
) holds. There exists a constant
C=C(N)
, independent of
M
, such that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq m\leq M}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}\|_{V_{N}}+\max_{1\leq m\leq M}\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}\|_{V_{N}}+\max_{1\leq m\leq M}\|v^{\beta}_{m}\|_{1,2}\leq C.

Proof.

Rearranging the energy dissipation inequality and using monotonicity of
F
to eliminate terms, we see that

(2.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m})+\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m},v^{\beta}_{m})+\mathcal{H}(v^{\beta}_{m})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{K}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{0})+\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{0},v^{\beta}_{0})+\mathcal{H}(v^{\beta}_{0})-\langle l^{\beta}_{0},\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{0}\rangle-h\sum_{j=1}^{m}\langle\delta l^{\beta}_{j},\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{j-1}\rangle+\langle l^{\beta}_{m},\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N}\|_{2}^{2}}{2}+\frac{b(1)}{\alpha}\int_{\Omega}\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N}))\,\mathrm{d}x+\mathcal{H}(v^{N}_{0})

\displaystyle+C_{T}\|l(0)\|_{-1,2}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N}\|_{1,2}+\frac{h}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|\delta l^{\beta}_{j}\|_{-1,2}^{2}+\frac{C_{T}h}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{j}\|_{1,2}^{2}

\displaystyle+C_{T}\|l\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{-1,2}_{D}(\Omega))}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}\|_{1,2}.

Using that
\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}=\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{j}
and adding
\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}
to both sides of (
2.20
), we apply Young’s inequality and the equivalence of norms in
V_{N}
to deduce that

\displaystyle{\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}}+{\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}}+\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m},v^{\beta}_{m})+\mathcal{H}(v^{\beta}_{m})

\displaystyle\leq C(N)\Big[\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N}\|_{1,2}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N}\|_{1,2}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N}\|_{1,p^{\prime}}^{p^{\prime}}+\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\|v^{N}_{0}\|_{1,2}^{2}+\|l\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{-1,2}_{D}(\Omega))}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\|l_{t}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;W^{-1,2}_{D}(\Omega))}+h\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\Big(\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\Big)\Big].

Applying the discrete Gronwall inequality and equivalence of norms again, we maximise over the indices
1\leq m\leq M
to obtain the stated result.
∎

Due to the regularity of the functions in
V_{N}
, Lemma
2.1
gives a very strong bound on the solution sequence. Taking the limit in the time discretisation first allows us to maintain these nice regularity properties as long as possible. Without the compatibility condition (
2.4
), an
M
-independent bound is still possible as the above result shows. However, later
N
-independent bounds are not possible without the assumption, hence why it is included in later results concerning
N
-independent bounds.

The phase-field function experiences a nonlinearity in the elastodynamic equation, by which we mean the presence of the term
b(v^{\beta}_{m})
. Hence, to identify the resulting limit, we require a strong convergence result for
(v^{\beta}_{m})_{m=1}^{M}
. To this end, we look for a bound on the discrete time derivatives
(\delta v^{\beta}_{m})_{m=1}^{M}
. Due to the nonlinearity in the constitutive relation, we cannot argue as in the proof of Lemma 3.4 from
[
28
]
. In particular, we cannot ‘transfer’ the effects of the nonlinearity between
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u})
and its derivative in time.
Instead we make use of the embedding
V_{N}\subset W^{k,2}_{D}(\Omega)\subset C^{1}(\overline{\Omega})
, a consequence of the Sobolev embedding theorem for
k>\frac{d}{2}+1
, and using Corollary
2.1
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let the assumptions of Lemma
2.1
hold. There exists a constant
C=C(N)
independent of
M
such that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq m\leq M}\|\delta v^{\beta}_{m}\|_{1,2}\leq C.

Proof.

Using Proposition
2.2
and Corollary
2.1
, for every
1\leq m\leq M
, we have

(2.21)

0=\big[\partial_{v}\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m},v^{\beta}_{m})+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v^{\beta}_{m})\big](\delta v^{\beta}_{m}),

and

(2.22)

0\leq\big[\partial_{v}\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1},v^{\beta}_{m-1})+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v^{\beta}_{m-1})\big](\delta v^{\beta}_{m}).

Subtracting (
2.21
) from (
2.22
), we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\|\delta v^{\beta}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}+2\epsilon\|\nabla\delta v^{\beta}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{h}\int_{\Omega}\Big[\frac{b^{\prime}(v^{\beta}_{m-1})}{\alpha}\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1}))-\frac{b^{\prime}(v^{\beta}_{m})}{\alpha}\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}))\Big]\cdot\delta v^{\beta}_{m}\,\mathrm{d}x.

We add and subtract
\alpha^{-1}b(v^{\beta}_{m-1})\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}))
to the right-hand side of the above and use that
b^{\prime}(v)=2v
to deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\|\delta v^{\beta}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}+2\epsilon\|\nabla\delta v^{\beta}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{2}{\alpha}\int_{\Omega}|\delta v^{\beta}_{m}|^{2}\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}))\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}b^{\prime}(v^{\beta}_{m-1})\delta v^{\beta}_{m}F^{-1}(\alpha\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(s\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1}-\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m})\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\delta v^{\beta}_{m}\|_{2}\big[\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1})\|_{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m})\|_{2}\big]\cdot\big[\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1})\|_{\infty}^{p^{\prime}-1}+\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m})\|_{\infty}^{p^{\prime}-1}\big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|\delta v^{\beta}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}}{4\epsilon}+C(N)\Big[\max_{0\leq j\leq M}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{j}\|_{V_{N}}^{2p^{\prime}}+\max_{1\leq j\leq M}\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{j}\|_{V_{N}}^{2p^{\prime}}\Big],

using Hölder’s inequality, followed by Young’s inequality. Applying Lemma
2.1
, the result follows.
∎

In the elastodynamic equation for weak energy solutions of (
2.1
)–(
2.3
), we have the interial term
\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}
.
Hence, we should look for a bound on the sequence
(\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m})_{m=1}^{M}
. The finite-dimensional setting of
V_{N}
simplifies this matter significantly. Indeed, we need only use the weak elastodynamic equation (
2.9
), the equivalence of norms in finite-dimensional spaces and the previously determined bounds.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let the assumptions of Lemma
2.1
hold. There exists a constant
C=C(N)
independent of
M
such that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq m\leq M}\|\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}\|_{V_{N}}\leq C.

Proof.

By the uniform bound on
(v^{\beta}_{m})_{m=0}^{M}
, the elastodynamic equation (
2.10
) and Lemma
2.1
, it follows that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq m\leq M}\|\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}\|_{V_{N}^{*}}\leq C(N),

where
V_{N}^{*}
is the dual space. By equivalence of norms, the result follows.
∎

2.2.
The limit
M\rightarrow\infty

Now we define interpolants of the solution sequence for the time discrete problem. We derive bounds on these functions using the results of Lemmas
2.1
,
2.2
and
2.3
, and then take the limit as
M\rightarrow\infty
.
The limiting functions define a solution of a time continuous Galerkin approximation of (
2.1
)–(
2.3
) with respect to
V_{N}
.
We define the piecewise affine, backwards and forwards interpolants, respectively, of the sequence
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m})_{m=0}^{M}
by

\displaystyle\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\beta}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{t-t^{\beta}_{m-1}}{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}+\frac{t^{\beta}_{m}-t}{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1}

\displaystyle\text{ for }t\in[t^{\beta}_{m-1},t^{\beta}_{m}],\,1\leq m\leq M,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta,+}(t)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m}

\displaystyle\text{ for }t\in(t^{\beta}_{m-1},t^{\beta}_{m}],\,1\leq m\leq M,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta,-}(t)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m-1}

\displaystyle\text{ for }t\in[t^{\beta}_{m-1},t^{\beta}_{m}),\,1\leq m\leq M,

where we extend
\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta,+}
and
\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta,-}
continuously to
t=0
and
t=T
, respectively. Similarly, we denote by
\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\beta,\prime}
,
\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta,+,\prime}
,
\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta,-,\prime}
the interpolants of
(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m})_{m=0}^{M}
,
\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta,+,\prime\prime}
the backwards interpolant of
(\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta}_{m})_{m=1}^{M}
,
\overline{v}^{\beta}
,
v^{\beta,+}
,
v^{\beta,-}
the interpolants of
(v^{\beta}_{m})_{m=1}^{M}
, and
v^{\beta,+,\prime}
the backwards interpolant of
(\delta v^{\beta}_{m})_{m=1}^{M}
. We can identify

\displaystyle\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\beta}_{t}=\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta,+,\prime},\quad\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\beta,\prime}_{t}=\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta,+,\prime\prime},\quad\overline{v}^{\beta}_{t}=v^{\beta,+,\prime}.

By Lemmas
2.1
,
2.2
and
2.3
, we deduce that there exists a constant
C=C(N)
, independent of
M
, such that

\displaystyle\|\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\beta}\|_{W^{1,\infty}(V_{N})}+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta,\pm}\|_{L^{\infty}(V_{N})}+\|\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\beta,\prime}\|_{W^{1,\infty}(V_{N})}+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta,\pm,\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(V_{N})}+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta,+,\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(V_{N})}

\displaystyle\quad+\|\overline{v}^{\beta}\|_{W^{1,\infty}(H^{1})}+\|v^{\beta,\pm}\|_{W^{1,\infty}(H^{1})}+\|v^{\beta,+,\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(H^{1})}

\displaystyle\leq C.

With this bound and standard weak compactness results for Bochner spaces, we obtain the convergence result in Lemma
2.4
. Various strong convergence results follow from application of the Aubin–Lions lemma and the compact embedding
V_{N}\subset C^{1}(\overline{\Omega})^{d}
.
We include only the ones that are used in identifying the limiting couple as a solution of the problem of interest.
In particular, these strong convergence results are used for identification of the nonlinear terms in the limit. We collect the convergences in Lemma
2.4
.
We do not include the details of the proof as it follows standard procedures.
Then we prove various properties of the limiting couple, denoted
(\boldsymbol{u}^{N},v^{N})
. In Proposition
2.4
, we show that a weak elastodynamic equation holds with respect to the Galerkin approximation from
V_{N}
, and in Proposition
2.5
that an appropriate minimisation problem is satisfied at every point in
[0,T]
. Finally, in Proposition
2.6
, we prove that an energy-dissipation equality holds.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let the assumptions of Lemma
2.1
hold and denote
\beta=(N,M)
where
N
is fixed.
There exists a subsequence in
M
, independent of
N
, not relabelled, and a limiting couple
(\boldsymbol{u}^{N},v^{N})
such that
\boldsymbol{u}^{N}\in W^{2,\infty}(0,T;V_{N})
and
v^{N}\in W^{1,\infty}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega))
with
v^{N}(t)\in H^{1}_{D+1}(\Omega)
for every
t\in[0,T]
, and the following convergence results hold as
M\rightarrow\infty
:

•

\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{(N,M)},\,\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{(N,M),\prime}\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}\boldsymbol{u}^{N},\,\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}
weakly-* in
W^{1,\infty}(0,T;V_{N})
, respectively;

•

\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{(N,M)},\,\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{(N,M),\prime}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{u}^{N},\,\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}
strongly in
C([0,T];C^{1}(\overline{\Omega})^{d})
, respectively;

•

\boldsymbol{u}^{(N,M),\pm},\,\boldsymbol{u}^{(N,M),\pm,\prime}\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}\boldsymbol{u}^{N},\,\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}
weakly-* in
L^{\infty}(0,T;V_{N})
, respectively;

•

\overline{v}^{(N,M)}\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}v^{N}
weakly-* in
W^{1,\infty}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega))
;

•

v^{(N,M),\pm}\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}v^{N}
weakly-* in
L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega))
.

Furthermore,
v^{N}\in[0,1]
and
v^{N}_{t}\leq 0
a.e. in
Q
.

Proposition 2.4
.

Let the assumptions of Lemma
2.1
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{N},v^{N})
be the limiting couple constructed in Lemma
2.4
. For a.e.
t\in(0,T)
and every
\boldsymbol{w}\in V_{N}
, we have

(2.23)

\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{tt}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{w}+b(v^{N}(t))F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{w})\,\mathrm{d}x=\langle l(t),\boldsymbol{w}\rangle.

The initial conditions hold in the sense that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 0+}\Big[\|\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t)-\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N}\|_{V_{N}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}(t)-\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N}\|_{V_{N}}+\|v^{N}(t)-v^{N}_{0}\|_{1,2}\Big]=0.

Proof.

For (
2.23
), rewriting (
2.10
) in terms of the interpolants, for every
M\in\mathbb{N}
and a.e.
t\in(0,T)
, we first notice that we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\beta,+,\prime}_{t}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{w}+b(v^{\beta,-}(t))F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta,+,\prime}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta,+})(t)):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{w})\,\mathrm{d}x=\langle l^{\beta,+}(t),\boldsymbol{w}\rangle,

for every
\boldsymbol{w}\in V_{N}
. By the convergence results in Lemma
2.4
, we deduce that, for every
t\in(0,T)
,

(2.24)

\displaystyle\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\Big\{\int_{\Omega}b(v^{\beta,-}(t))F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta,+,\prime}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta,+})(t))):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{w})\,\mathrm{d}x-\langle l^{\beta,+}(t),\boldsymbol{w}\rangle\Big\}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}b(v^{N}(t))F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N})(t)):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{w})\,\mathrm{d}x-\langle l(t),\boldsymbol{w}\rangle,

and, for every
\psi\in C([0,T])
,

(2.25)

\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\int_{Q}\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\beta,+,\prime}_{t}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{w}\psi\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{Q}\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{tt}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{w}\psi\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t.

Using (
2.24
), (
2.25
) and Lebesgue’s differentiation theorem, we obtain (
2.23
