). Satisfaction of the initial conditions follows from standard arguments and regularity results for Bochner functions. (See
[
18
, Chapter 5]
, for example.)
∎

Proposition 2.5
.

Let the assumptions of Lemma
2.1
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{N},v^{N})
be the limiting couple constructed in Lemma
2.4
. For every
t\in[0,T]
,
v^{N}(t)
solves the variational problem

(2.26)

\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t),v^{N}(t))+\mathcal{H}(v^{N}(t))=\inf\Big\{\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t),v)+\mathcal{H}(v)\,:\,v\in H^{1}_{D+1}(\Omega),\,v\leq v^{N}(t)\Big\}.

Proof.

By the construction of
(v^{N}_{0})_{N}
, (
2.26
) holds at
t=0
.
For
t\in(0,T]
, it suffices to show that

(2.27)

0\leq\int_{\Omega}\frac{b^{\prime}(v^{N}(t))}{\alpha}\chi\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t)))+\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\big(v^{N}(t)-1\big)\chi+2\epsilon\nabla v^{N}(t)\cdot\nabla\chi\,\mathrm{d}x,

for every
\chi\in H^{1}_{D}(\Omega)
with
\chi\leq 0
. However, by Proposition
2.2
, for every
\psi\in C([0,T])
with
\psi\geq 0
, we have

(2.28)

0\leq\int_{Q}\frac{b^{\prime}(v^{\beta,+})}{\alpha}\chi\psi\varphi^{*}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\beta,+}))+\frac{1}{2\epsilon}(v^{\beta,+}-1)\chi\psi+2\epsilon\nabla v^{\beta,+}\cdot\nabla(\chi\psi)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t.

Letting
N\rightarrow\infty
in (
2.28
) and using the weak convergence results, an application of Lebesgue’s differentiation theorem gives (
2.27
) for a.e.
t\in(0,T]
. Noticing that the right-hand side of (
2.27
) is continuous as a function of
t
, we conclude that (
2.27
) holds for every
t\in[0,T]
.
∎

Proposition 2.6
.

Let the assumptions of Lemma
2.1
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{N},v^{N})
be the limiting couple constructed in Lemma
2.4
. For every
t\in[0,T]
, the following energy-dissipation equality holds:

(2.29)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(t;\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t),\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}(t),v^{N}(t))+\int_{0}^{t}\langle l_{t},\boldsymbol{u}^{N}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}b(v^{N})\big[F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}))-F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}))\big]:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(0;\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N},\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N},v^{N}_{0}).

Proof.

Trivially the result holds for
t=0
. Letting
M\rightarrow\infty
in the discrete energy-dissipation inequality and using weak lower semi-continuity, we deduce that (
2.29
) holds with “
=
” replaced by “
\leq
”, for a.e.
t\in(0,T]
.

For the opposite inequality, we first notice that

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}(t))-\langle l(t),\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t)\rangle+\int_{0}^{t}\langle l_{t},\boldsymbol{u}^{N}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}b(v^{N})F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad-\mathcal{K}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N})+\langle l(0),\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{tt}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s-\int_{0}^{t}\langle l,\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}b(v^{N})F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=0,

using (
2.23
). Thus, it suffices to show that

(2.30)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t),v^{N}(t))+\mathcal{H}(v^{N}(t))-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}b(v^{N})F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N},v^{N}_{0})+\mathcal{H}(v^{N}_{0}).

To prove that (
2.30
) holds, we mimic an argument from
[
28
]
. Fixing
t\in(0,T]
, for each
K\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
we define a time step
h_{K}=\frac{t}{K}
and the corresponding time-discrete approximate solution sequence

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}^{N,K}_{k}=\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(hk),\quad v^{N,K}_{k}=v^{N}(hk)\quad\text{ for }0\leq k\leq K.

Then we can write

(2.31)

\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t),v^{N}(t))-\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N},v^{N}_{0})=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\Big[\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N,K}_{k},v^{N,K}_{k})-\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N,K}_{k-1},v^{N,K}_{k-1})\Big].

Using the variational problem (
2.26
), we deduce that

(2.32)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N,K}_{k},v^{N,K}_{k})-\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N,K}_{k-1},v^{N,K}_{k-1})

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\Omega}\frac{b(v^{N,K}_{k})}{\alpha}\Big(\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N,K}_{k}))-\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N,K}_{k-1}))\Big)\,\mathrm{d}x+\mathcal{H}(v^{N,K}_{k-1})-\mathcal{H}(v^{N,K}_{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{t}{K}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{b(v^{N,K}_{k})}{\alpha}F^{-1}(\alpha\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(s\boldsymbol{u}^{N,K}_{k}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{u}^{N,K}_{k-1})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{N,K}_{k})\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle+\mathcal{H}(v^{N,K}_{k-1})-\mathcal{H}(v^{N,K}_{k}).

Using the previous notation for interpolants of discrete solution sequences, we insert (
2.32
) into (
2.31
) to obtain

(2.33)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t),v^{N}(t))-\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N},v^{N}_{0})-\mathcal{H}(v^{N}_{0})+\mathcal{H}(v^{N}(t))

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{b(v^{N,K,+})}{\alpha}F^{-1}(\alpha\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(s\boldsymbol{u}^{N,K,+}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{u}^{N,K,-})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{N,K}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

Using that
\boldsymbol{u}^{N}\in W^{2,\infty}(0,T;V_{N})
and
v^{N}\in W^{1,\infty}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega))\cap L^{\infty}(Q)
, we see that

•

\boldsymbol{u}^{N,K,\pm}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{u}^{N}
strongly in
C([0,T];V_{N})
;

•

\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{N,K}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{u}^{N}
strongly in
W^{1,\infty}(0,T;V_{N})
;

•

v^{N,K,+}\rightarrow v^{N}
strongly in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)^{d})
.

Letting
K\rightarrow\infty
in (
2.33
), it follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t),v^{N}(t))-\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N},v^{N}_{0})-\mathcal{H}(v^{N}_{0})+\mathcal{H}(v^{N}(t))

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\frac{b(v^{N})}{\alpha}F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s.

Hence the energy-dissipation equality holds for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
. Using the continuity of the solution couple, we conclude that the equality must hold for every
t\in[0,T]
.
∎

2.3.
N
-independent bounds

In this section, we derive
N
-independent bounds on the solution couple
(\boldsymbol{u}^{N},v^{N})
constructed in Lemma
2.4
. This allows us to take
N\rightarrow\infty
and obtain a weak energy solution of the original problem (
2.1
)–(
2.3
). We reintroduce the stress tensor at this stage to simplify the representation. Also, we use the Korn–Poincaré inequality (Theorem
1.1
) throughout this section.
The first two results provide bounds on the displacement
\boldsymbol{u}
and stress tensor
\mathbf{T}
. In Lemma
2.7
, we get a bound on the phase-field function
v
. Following this, we differentiate between the cases
p\in(1,2]
and
p\in(2,\infty)
. When
p\in(1,2]
we are able to improve the bounds on the phase-field function. Indeed, we obtain bounds on the time derivative in the spirit of of Lemma
2.2
. We are unable to obtain such a result when
p>2
for technical reasons. Instead, we make use of the monotonicity of the phase-field functions as a function of the time variable. These two cases are discussed in Section
2.4
and
2.5
, respectively.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let the assumptions of Lemma
2.4
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{N},\mathbf{T}^{N},v^{N})
be the solution triple constructed there, where we define
\mathbf{T}^{N}:=F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}))
. There exists a constant
C
, independent of
N
, such that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t)\|_{2}+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t)\|_{1,p^{\prime}}+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}(t)\|_{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\|\mathbf{T}^{N}(t)\|_{p}^{p}+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}(t)\|_{1,p^{\prime}}^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C.

Proof.

For the first, third and fourth bounds, we test against
\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}
in the elastodynamic equation (
2.23
). Using the lower bound for
b
, we can argue as in
[
9
]
and so do not include the details here. Using the constitutive relation and the resulting memory kernel property, for every
t\in[0,T]
, we have

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t))|\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N})|+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{\alpha(s-t)}|\mathbf{T}^{N}|^{p-1}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Taking the
L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)
norm of both sides, using the properties of the approximation of the initial data, and applying the Korn–Poincaré inequality, the
L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{1,p^{\prime}}(\Omega)^{d})
bound on
(\boldsymbol{u}^{N})_{N}
follows. Using the bound on
\mathbf{T}^{N}
with the constitutive relation, we see that
(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}))_{N}
is bounded in
L^{p^{\prime}}(Q)^{d\times d}
. Putting this together with the bound on
\boldsymbol{u}^{N}
and applying the Korn–Poincaré inequality, the final bound on
\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}
follows.
∎

Lemma 2.6
.

Let the assumptions of Lemma
2.4
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{N},\mathbf{T}^{N},v^{N})
be the solution triple constructed there. There exists a constant
C
independent of
N
such that

\displaystyle\int_{Q}|\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{tt}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\Big\{\|\mathbf{T}^{N}(t)\|_{p}+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}(t)\|_{1,p^{\prime}}\Big\}\leq C.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the claim for the first two terms, repeating the reasoning from the proof of Lemma
2.5
in order to obtain the bound on the final term. We test in the elastodynamic equation (
2.23
) against
\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{tt}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}
to get

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{tt}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big(\frac{\alpha}{2}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}\Big)+\int_{\Omega}b(v^{N})\mathbf{T}^{N}:F(\mathbf{T}^{N})_{t}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\langle l,\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{tt}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big(\langle l,\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}\rangle\Big)-\langle l_{t},\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}\rangle.

The term involving the nonlinearity can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}b(v^{N})\mathbf{T}^{N}:F(\mathbf{T}^{N})_{t}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big(\int_{\Omega}b(v^{N})\frac{p-1}{p}|\mathbf{T}^{N}|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)-\int_{\Omega}b(v^{N})v^{N}_{t}\Big[\frac{p-1}{p}|\mathbf{T}^{N}|^{p}\Big]\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big(\int_{\Omega}b(v^{N})\frac{p-1}{p}|\mathbf{T}^{N}|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big),

recalling the non-positivity of
v^{N}_{t}
. Integrating over
(0,t)
for an arbitrary
t\in(0,T]
, we deduce that

(2.34)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}|\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{tt}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{\Omega}b(v^{N}(t))\frac{p-1}{p}|\mathbf{T}^{N}(t)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha}{2}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N}\|_{2}^{2}+\int_{\Omega}b(v^{N}_{0})\frac{p-1}{p}|\mathbf{T}^{N}(0)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x+\langle l(t),(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N})(t)\rangle

\displaystyle-\langle l(0),\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N}\rangle-\int_{0}^{t}\langle l_{t},\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C\Big[\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N}\|_{2}^{2}+\int_{\Omega}|F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N}))|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x+\|l(t)\|_{-1,p}\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N})(t)\|_{p^{\prime}}

\displaystyle+\|l(0)\|_{-1,p}\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N})\|_{p^{\prime}}+\int_{0}^{t}\|l_{t}\|_{-1,p}\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N})\|_{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}s\Big]

\displaystyle\leq C\Big[\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N})\|_{p^{\prime}}^{p^{\prime}}+\|l\|_{W^{1,\infty}(W^{-1,p})}\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N})(t)\|_{p^{\prime}}

\displaystyle+\|l\|_{W^{1,\infty}(W^{-1,p})}\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N})\|_{p^{\prime}}+\int_{0}^{t}\|l_{t}\|_{-1,p}^{p}+\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N})\|_{p^{\prime}}^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}s\Big].

However, we have that

\displaystyle|\mathbf{T}^{N}(t)|^{p}=|F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N})(t))|^{p}=|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N})|^{p^{\prime}}.

Using this on the right-hand side of (
2.34
) and applying Young’s inequality, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}|\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{tt}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{\Omega}|\mathbf{T}^{N}(t)|^{p}+|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N})(t)|^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq C\Big[\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N})\|_{p^{\prime}}^{p^{\prime}}+\|l\|_{W^{1,\infty}(W^{-1,p})}^{p}+\int_{0}^{t}\|l_{t}\|_{-1,p}^{p}+\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\|_{p^{\prime}}^{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}s\Big]

Using Lemma
2.5
and the properties of the approximations of the initial data (in particular, the boundedness in both
L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
and
W^{1,p^{\prime}}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
irrespective of the value of
p
), the right-hand side can be bounded above independent of
N
so the required result follows.
∎

Lemma 2.7
.

Let the assumptions of Lemma
2.4
hold. Let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{N},\mathbf{T}^{N},v^{N})
be the solution triple constructed there. Suppose additionally that the compatibility condition (
2.4
) holds. There exists a constant
C
, independent of
N
, such that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|v^{N}(t)\|_{1,2}\leq C.

Proof.

By the energy-dissipation equality, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(v^{N}(t))

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{F}(t;\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t),\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}(t),v^{N}(t))+\langle l(t),\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}b(v^{N})\big[F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}))-F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}))\big]:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\langle l(t),\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t)\rangle-\int_{0}^{t}\langle l_{t},\boldsymbol{u}^{N}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s+\mathcal{F}(0;\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N},\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N},v^{N}_{0})

\displaystyle\leq C\Big[\|l(t)\|_{-1,p}\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t))\|_{p^{\prime}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N}\|_{2}^{2}+\|l(0)\|_{-1,p}\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N})\|_{p^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\Omega}b(v^{N}_{0})\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N})))\,\mathrm{d}x+\|v^{N}_{0}\|_{1,2}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\|l_{t}(s)\|_{-1,p}\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(s))\|_{p^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}s\Big].

Using the boundedness of
(v^{N}_{0})_{N}
in
H^{1}_{D}(\Omega)
and
(\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N})_{N}
,
(\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N})_{N}
in
L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}\cap W^{1,p^{\prime}}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
, alongside the bounds in Lemma
2.5
, the right-hand side can be bounded above independent of
N
and
t\in[0,T]
. Taking the supremum with respect to the time variable, we use the definition of the functional
\mathcal{H}
to yield the stated result.
∎

2.4.
Limit in the case
p\in(1,2]

To replicate the ideas of Lemma
2.2
, we first need to show that testing in the variational form of the minimisation problem against
v^{N}_{t}
causes the right-hand side of (
2.27
) to vanish. Namely, we need to prove the time continuous analogue of Corollary
2.1
.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let the assumptions of Lemma
2.4
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{N},\mathbf{T}^{N},v^{N})
be the solution triple constructed there. For a.e.
t\in(0,T)
, we have

(2.35)

\big[\partial_{v}\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t),v^{N}(t))+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v^{N}(t))\big](v^{N}_{t}(t))=0.

Proof.

Differentiating the energy-dissipation equality from Proposition
2.6
with respect to the time variable, we obtain

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}^{N}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{t}^{N}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2\epsilon}(v^{N}(t)-1)v^{N}_{t}(t)+2\epsilon\nabla v^{N}(t)\cdot\nabla v^{N}_{t}(t)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\Omega}\frac{b^{\prime}(v^{N})}{\alpha}v^{N}_{t}\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}))+b(v^{N})F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}x-\langle l_{t},\boldsymbol{u}^{N}\rangle-\langle l,\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\Omega}b(v^{N})\big[F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}))-F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}))\big]:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}x+\langle l_{t},\boldsymbol{u}^{N}\rangle

\displaystyle=\Big[\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}^{N}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{t}^{N}+F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}x-\langle l,\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}\rangle\Big]

\displaystyle\quad+\Big[\int_{\Omega}\frac{b^{\prime}(v^{N})}{\alpha}v^{N}_{t}\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}))+\frac{1}{2\epsilon}(v^{N}(t)-1)v^{N}_{t}(t)+2\epsilon\nabla v^{N}(t)\cdot\nabla v^{N}_{t}(t)\,\mathrm{d}x\Big].

The terms in the first set of square brackets on the right-hand side vanishes for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
, considering the elastodynamic equation with the test function
\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}
. Hence the terms in the second set of square brackets must also vanish. However, this is exactly the left-hand side of (
2.35
) and so we conclude the required result.
∎

Lemma 2.9
.

Let the assumptions of Lemma
2.4
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{N},\mathbf{T}^{N},v^{N})
be the solution triple constructed there. Suppose additionally that the compatibility condition (
2.4
) holds. There exists a constant
C
, independent of
N
, such that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|v^{N}_{t}(t)\|_{1,2}\leq C.

Proof.

Let
t\in(0,T]
and fix
h>0
sufficiently small such that
t-h>0
.
Subtracting (
2.35
), evaluated at time
t
, from the inequality

\displaystyle 0\leq\big[\partial_{v}\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t-h),v^{N}(t-h))+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v^{N}(t-h))\big](v^{N}_{t}(t)),

which is a consequence of the proof of Proposition
2.5
, we deduce that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega}\Big[\frac{b^{\prime}(v^{N}(t-h))}{\alpha}\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t-h)))-\frac{b^{\prime}(v^{N}(t))}{\alpha}\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t)))\Big]v^{N}_{t}(t)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\int_{\Omega}\big[(v^{N}(t-h)-1)-(v^{N}(t)-1)\big]v^{N}_{t}(t)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+2\epsilon\int_{\Omega}\nabla\big(v^{N}(t-h)-v^{N}(t)\big)\cdot\nabla v^{N}(t)\,\mathrm{d}x.

Using the fundamental theorem of calculus in the first integral on the right-hand side and dividing through by
h
, it follows that

(2.36)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\int_{\Omega}\frac{v^{N}(t)-v^{N}(t-h)}{h}v^{N}_{t}(t)\,\mathrm{d}x+2\epsilon\int_{\Omega}\nabla\Big(\frac{v^{N}(t)-v^{N}(t-h)}{h}\Big)\cdot\nabla v^{N}_{t}(t)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{\alpha}\int_{\Omega}\frac{v^{N}(t)-v^{N}(t-h)}{h}v^{N}_{t}(t)\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t-h)))\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}\Big\{\frac{b^{\prime}(v^{N}(t))}{\alpha}v^{N}_{t}(t)F^{-1}(\alpha\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(s\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t-h)+(1-s)\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t)))

\displaystyle:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t-h)-\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t)}{h}\Big)\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x.

Recalling that
\boldsymbol{u}^{N}\in C^{1}([0,T];V_{N})
and
v^{N}\in W^{1,2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega))
, taking the limit as
h\rightarrow 0+
in (
2.36
) yields the following, for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
:

(2.37)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\|v^{N}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+2\epsilon\|\nabla v^{N}_{t}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{2}{\alpha}\int_{\Omega}|v^{N}_{t}(t)|^{2}\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t)))\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega}\frac{b^{\prime}(1)}{\alpha}|v^{N}_{t}(t)||F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t)))||\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}(t))|\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\frac{b^{\prime}(1)}{\alpha}|v^{N}_{t}(t)||\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t))|^{p^{\prime}-1}|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}(t))|\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\frac{b^{\prime}(1)}{\alpha}\Big(|v^{N}_{t}(t)||\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t))|^{\frac{p^{\prime}}{2}}\Big)\cdot\Big(|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t))|^{\frac{p^{\prime}-2}{2}}|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}(t))|\Big)\,\mathrm{d}x

Applying Hölder’s inequality to the final line of (
2.37
) and absorbing one of the resulting terms into the left-hand side, we deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\|v^{N}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+2\epsilon\|\nabla v^{N}_{t}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\int_{\Omega}|v^{N}_{t}(t)|^{2}\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t)))\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\Omega}|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t))|^{p^{\prime}-2}|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}(t))|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x.

If
p=2
, we are done by the bound in Lemma
2.6
. Otherwise, applying Hölder’s inequality again but with parameters
q=\frac{p^{\prime}}{p^{\prime}-2}
and
q^{\prime}=\frac{p^{\prime}}{2}
, valid choices because
p^{\prime}>2
, we obtain

(2.38)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\|v^{N}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+2\epsilon\|\nabla v^{N}_{t}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\int_{\Omega}|v^{N}_{t}(t)|^{2}\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t)))\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq C\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t))\|_{p^{\prime}}^{p^{\prime}-2}\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}(t))\|_{p^{\prime}}^{2}.

By Lemma
2.6
, the right-hand side of (
2.38
) is uniformly bounded above, independent of
t
and
N
, for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
. Hence the stated result follows.
∎

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
p\in(1,2]
. Suppose that
l\in C^{1}([0,T];W^{-1,p}_{D}(\Omega)^{d})
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in W^{1,p^{\prime}}(\Omega)^{d}
and
v_{0}\in H^{1}_{D+1}(\Omega)
with
v_{0}\in[0,1]
a.e. in
\Omega
, such that the compatibility condition (
2.4
) holds.
There exists a triple
(\boldsymbol{u},\mathbf{T},v)
that is a weak energy solution of (
2.1
)–(
2.3
) in the sense of Theorem
2.1
. Furthermore, there exists a subsequence in
N
, not relabelled, such that if
(\boldsymbol{u}^{N},\mathbf{T}^{N},v^{N})
is the solution triple from Lemma
2.4
, we have

•

\boldsymbol{u}^{N}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{u}
weakly in
W^{2,2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)^{d})
and weakly-* in
W^{1,\infty}(0,T;W^{1,p^{\prime}}(\Omega)^{d})
,

•

\mathbf{T}^{N}\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}\mathbf{T}
weakly-* in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
, and

•

v^{N}\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}v
weakly-* in
W^{1,\infty}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega))
.

Proof.

The convergence results follow easily from Lemma
2.5
, Lemma
2.6
, Lemma
2.7
, Lemma
2.9
and standard compactness results for Bochner spaces. By the Aubin–Lions lemma, we see that
\boldsymbol{u}^{N}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{u}
strongly in
C^{1}([0,T];L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)^{d})
, and
v^{N}\rightarrow v
strongly in
C([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega))
, up to a possible further subsequence that we do not relabel.

From the elastodynamic equation (
2.23
), for every
\boldsymbol{w}\in C^{\infty}_{D}(\overline{\Omega})^{d}
and every
\psi\in C([0,T])
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{Q}\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}^{N}\cdot\big(\psi P_{N}\boldsymbol{w}\big)+b(v^{N})\mathbf{T}^{N}:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\psi P_{N}\boldsymbol{w})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{T}\langle l,\psi P_{N}\boldsymbol{w}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t.

Using the weak convergence results and the strong convergence of
(P_{N}\boldsymbol{w})_{N}
in in
W^{k,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
to
\boldsymbol{w}
as
N\rightarrow\infty
, it follows that

\displaystyle\int_{Q}\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\cdot\big(\psi\boldsymbol{w}\big)+b(v)\mathbf{T}:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\psi\boldsymbol{w})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{T}\psi\langle l,\boldsymbol{w}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t.

By Lebesgue’s differentiation theorem and a standard density argument, the required elastodynamic equation follows for any test function
\boldsymbol{w}\in W^{1,p^{\prime}}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
.
The satisfaction of the initial conditions follows from the usual arguments, using the approximation properties of the data
(\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N})_{N}
,
(\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N})_{N}
and
(v^{N}_{0})_{N}
.

To show that the constitutive relationship holds, we use a variant of Minty’s method. First, we notice that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{Q}b(v^{N})\mathbf{T}^{N}:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\Big\{\langle l,\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}\rangle-\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}^{N}\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N})\,\mathrm{d}x\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\Big\{\langle l,\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}\rangle-\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u})\,\mathrm{d}x\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{Q}b(v)\mathbf{T}:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t,

using the strong convergence results in the transition to the second line. For an arbitrary test function
\mathbf{S}\in L^{p}(Q)^{d\times d}
, it follows that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{Q}b(v^{N})(\mathbf{T}^{N}-\mathbf{S}):(F(\mathbf{T}^{N})-F(\mathbf{S}))\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{Q}b(v)(\mathbf{T}-\mathbf{S}):(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u})-F(\mathbf{S}))\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t.

Replacing
\mathbf{S}
with
\mathbf{T}\pm\gamma\mathbf{U}
where
\gamma>0
and
\mathbf{U}\in L^{\infty}(Q)^{d\times d}
, we get

\displaystyle 0\leq\mp\int_{Q}b(v)\mathbf{U}:(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u})-F(\mathbf{T}))\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t,

taking the limit as
\gamma\rightarrow 0+
.
Choosing
\mathbf{U}=\frac{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u})-F(\mathbf{T})}{1+|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u})-F(\mathbf{T})|}
and recalling that
b
is strictly positive, it follows that
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u})=F(\mathbf{T})
pointwise a.e. in
Q
. Also, we deduce that

(2.39)

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{Q}b(v^{N})|\mathbf{T}^{N}|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{Q}b(v)|\mathbf{T}|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t.

However, the sequence
(b(v^{N})^{\frac{1}{p}}\mathbf{T}^{N})_{N}
converges weakly in
L^{p}(Q)^{d\times d}
. The weak convergence with convergence of norms (
2.39
) implies that
(b(v^{N})^{\frac{1}{p}}\mathbf{T}^{N})_{N}
converges strongly in
L^{p}(Q)^{d\times d}
. Thus
\mathbf{T}^{N}\rightarrow\mathbf{T}
strongly in
L^{p}(Q)^{d\times d}
, which in turn implies that
(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}))_{N}
converges strongly in
L^{p^{\prime}}(Q)^{d\times d}
to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u})
. Reasoning as before, it follows that
(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}))_{N}
converges strongly to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u})
in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)^{d})
, and so, we deduce the pointwise convergence of
(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}))_{N}
a.e. in
Q
, up to a further subsequence in
N
. Pointwise convergence and strong convergence in
L^{p^{\prime}}(Q)
of
(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}))_{N}
to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t})
then follows. Applying Fatou’s lemma with the weak lower semi-continuity of norms to the energy-dissipation equality for
(\boldsymbol{u}^{N},\mathbf{T}^{N},v^{N})
, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(t;\boldsymbol{u}(t),\boldsymbol{u}_{t}(t),v(t))+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}b(v)\big[\mathbf{T}-F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}))\big]:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\langle l_{t}(s),\boldsymbol{u}(s)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{N\rightarrow\infty}\Big\{\mathcal{F}(t;\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(t),\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}(t),v^{N}(t))+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}b(v^{N})\big[\mathbf{T}^{N}-F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}))\big]:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}^{N})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\langle l_{t}(s),\boldsymbol{u}^{N}(s)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s\Big\}

\displaystyle=\liminf_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{F}(0;\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N},\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N},v^{N}_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(0;\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{1},v_{0}).

The final line follows from the choice of the approximations
(\boldsymbol{u}_{0,N})_{N}
and
(\boldsymbol{u}_{1,N})_{N}
, as well as Proposition
2.1
.
For the opposite inequality, we reason as in the proof of Proposition
2.6
. To do so, we only need to prove that the limiting triple satisfies the minimisation problem (
2.2
). For this, it suffices to show that

(2.40)

0\leq\big[\partial_{v}\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}(t),v(t))+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v(t))\big](\chi),

for every
\chi\in H^{1}_{D}(\Omega)
. Using the fact that
\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}))\rightarrow\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}))
strongly in
L^{1}(Q)
, by the strong convergence result for
(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{N}))_{N}
, we obtain (
2.40
) easily from taking the limit in (
2.27
). Hence we conclude that
(\boldsymbol{u},\mathbf{T},v)
is a solution in the required sense because we have the weak elastodynamic equation, the minimisation problem, the energy-dissipation equality and the satisfaction of the initial data.
∎

2.5.
Limit in the case
p\in(2,\infty)

In this case, we have
p^{\prime}\in(1,2)
. We cannot obtain a bound on
(v^{N}_{t})_{N}
by repeating the reasoning from Section
2.3
. In particular, the proof of Lemma
2.9
fails. Hence, at this stage
(v^{N})_{N}
is at best bounded in
L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega))
which implies that
(v^{N})_{N}
converges weakly-* in
L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega))
. However, we must find the limit of the nonlinear term
(b(v^{N}))_{N}
to take the limit in the elastodynamic equation (
2.23
). Hence we need at least a pointwise convergence result for the sequence
(v^{N})_{N}
. To obtain such a result, we make use of the monotonicity of these functions with respect to the time variable. Indeed the key is the following result from
[
12
]
.
We combine this result with the Rellich–Kondrachov compactness theorem in order to obtain a suitable strong convergence result as stated in Corollary
2.2
.
Next we prove a continuity result for the limiting function constructed in Corollary
2.2
. This is vital to prove one direction of the energy-dissipation inequality.
Then we prove the existence result for parameter values
p>2
.

Theorem 2.5
.

Let
[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}
and
v^{m}:[a,b]\rightarrow L^{2}(\Omega)
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, a sequence of functions such that

v^{m}(s)\leq v^{m}(t)
in
\Omega
for every
a\leq s\leq t\leq b
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Suppose also that there exists a constant
C
, independent of
m
, such that

\|v^{m}(t)\|_{2}\leq C
for every
t\in[a,b]
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
There exists a subsequence in
m
, not relabelled, and a function
v:[a,b]\rightarrow L^{2}(\Omega)
such that, for every
t\in[a,b]
, we have

v^{m}(t)\rightharpoonup v(t)
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
as
m\rightarrow\infty
.
Moreover,
\|v(t)\|_{2}\leq C
for every
t\in[0,T]
and

v(s)\leq v(t)
in
\Omega
, for every
a\leq s\leq t\leq b
.

Corollary 2.2
.

Let
(v^{m})_{m\geq 1}
be a sequence of functions defined on
[a,b]
with values in
H^{1}(\Omega)
such that

v^{m}(s)\leq v^{m}(t)
in
\Omega
for every
a\leq s\leq t\leq b
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Suppose also that there exists a constant
C
independent of
m
such that

\|v^{m}(t)\|_{1,2}\leq C
for every
t\in[a,b]
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
There exists a subsequence in
m
, not relabelled, and a function
v:[a,b]\rightarrow H^{1}(\Omega)
such that, for every
t\in[a,b]
,

v^{m}(t)\rightharpoonup v(t)
weakly in
H^{1}(\Omega)
and strongly in
L^{2}(\Omega)
.
Furthermore,
\|v(t)\|_{1,2}\leq C
for every
t\in[a,b]
and

v(s)\leq v(t)
in
\Omega
for every
a\leq s\leq t\leq b
.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
(v^{m})_{m}
be the subsequence constructed in Corollary
2.2
, with limiting function
v
. Then
v
is almost everywhere continuous as a map from
[a,b]
into
L^{2}(\Omega)
.

Proof.

Fix a time
t\in(a,b)
and, for
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
(\delta_{n,i})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence in
(0,\infty)
such that
t+\delta_{n,i}\in(a,b]
for every
n
and
\delta_{n,i}\rightarrow 0+
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. The sequence
(v(t+\delta_{n,i}))_{n}
is bounded in
H^{1}(\Omega)
. Hence, up to a subsequence that we do not relabel, we have that
(v(t+\delta_{n,i}))_{n}
converges weakly in
H^{1}(\Omega)
and strongly in
L^{2}(\Omega)
to a limit that we denote by
\tilde{w}_{i}
. Using the strong convergence result and the monotonicity assumption, for every
n
, we have

\displaystyle\|v(t+\delta_{n,1})\|_{2}\geq\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\|v(t+\delta_{m,2})\|_{2}=\|\tilde{w}_{2}\|_{2}.

Letting
n\rightarrow\infty
, we deduce that
\|\tilde{w}_{1}\|_{2}\geq\|\tilde{w}_{2}\|_{2}
. By symmetry, we deduce that
\|\tilde{w}_{1}\|_{2}=\|\tilde{w}_{2}\|_{2}
. We conclude that every subsequence of
(v(t+\delta))_{\delta>0}
converges to the same limit as
\delta\rightarrow 0+
. Hence the upper limit
v(t+)
exists for every
t\in(a,b)
. By analogous reasoning, the lower limit
v(t-)
also exists.

By monotonicity, the function
v
is discontinuous at the point
t
in the case that the upper and lower limits do not coincide. Indeed, we may write

\displaystyle\{t\in(a,b)\,:\,v\text{ is not continuous at }t\}

\displaystyle=\cup_{\epsilon>0}\{t\in(a,b)\,:\,\|v(t+)-v(t-)\|_{2}>\epsilon\}

\displaystyle=\cup_{n\geq 1}\Big\{t\in(a,b)\,:\,\|v(t+)-v(t-)\|_{2}>\frac{1}{n}\Big\}.

By monotonicity, we know that

\displaystyle\|v(a)\|_{2}\leq\|v(t-)\|_{2}\leq\|v(t+)\|_{2}\leq\|v(b)\|_{2}.

Hence, for every
n
, the set
\{t\in(a,b)\,:\,\|v(t+)-v(t-)\|_{2}>\frac{1}{n}\}
must be finite. Hence the set of discontinuity points is countable and consequently
v
is continuous almost everywhere on
(a,b)
as a map into
L^{2}(\Omega)
.
∎

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
p\in(2,\infty)
. Suppose
l\in C^{1}([0,T];W^{-1,p}_{D}(\Omega)^{d})
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in W^{1,p^{\prime}}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}\cap L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
and
v_{0}\in H^{1}_{D+1}(\Omega)
with
v_{0}\in[0,1]
a.e. in
\Omega
, such that the compatibility condition (
2.4
) holds.
There exists a weak energy solution
(\boldsymbol{u},\mathbf{T},v)
of (
2.1
)–(
2.3
) in the sense of Theorem
2.2
. Furthermore, there exists a subsequence in
N
, not relabelled, such that if
(\boldsymbol{u}^{N},\mathbf{T}^{N},v^{N})
is the solution triple from Lemma
2.4
, the following convergence results hold:

•

\boldsymbol{u}^{N}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{u}
weakly in
W^{2,2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)^{d})
and weakly-* in
W^{1,\infty}(0,T;W^{1,p^{\prime}}(\Omega)^{d})
;

•

\mathbf{T}^{N}\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}\mathbf{T}
weakly-* in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
;

•

v^{N}(t)\rightarrow v(t)
strongly in
L^{2}(\Omega)
and weakly in
H^{1}(\Omega)
for every
t\in[0,T]
.

Proof.

Using the bounds in Section
2.3
with Corollary
2.2
, the convergence results are immediate.
Considering the initial data for the approximate solution and the corresponding convergence results, the claim for the initial conditions follows.
Combining the strong convergence result from Corollary
2.2
with the uniform bound on
(v^{N})_{N}
and Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem, we deduce that
v^{N}\rightarrow v
strongly in
L^{q}(Q)^{d\times d}
, for every
q\in[1,\infty)
.
Hence, reasoning as before, the elastodynamic equation must be satisfied by the limiting triple.
Using Minty’s method, it follows that the constitutive relation holds. The strong convergence of
(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}))_{N}
in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
and of
(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{N}_{t}))_{N}
in
L^{p^{\prime}}(Q)^{d\times d}
follows. From this, we get the “
\leq
” direction of the energy-dissipation equality. Satisfaction of the minimisation problem can be deduced exactly as in the proof of Theorem
2.4
.

For the opposite direction “
\geq
” of the energy-dissipation equality, we can repeat the reasoning from the proof of Proposition
2.6
and use the continuity that follows from Proposition
2.7
. Indeed, fix a point
t\in(0,T]
such that it is a point of continuity of
v
. Let
\mathcal{N}
denote a set of null measure such that
v
is continuous on
[0,T]\setminus\mathcal{N}
.

Fix
K\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
and define a time step
h_{K}=\frac{t}{K}
. For each
0\leq k\leq K-1
, we fix a time point close to
hk
at which
v
is continuous. Namely, we pick a
t^{K}_{k}\in(hk-\frac{h_{K}}{4},hk+\frac{h_{K}}{4})\cap\big((0,T)\setminus\mathcal{N}\big)
such that
v
is continuous at
t^{K}_{k}
as a map into
L^{2}(\Omega)
. Next, we define a sequence of approximations

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k}:=\boldsymbol{u}(t^{K}_{k}),\quad\mathbf{T}^{K}_{k}:=\mathbf{T}(t^{K}_{k}),\quad v^{K}_{k}:=v(t^{K}_{k})\quad\text{ for }0\leq k\leq K.

Then we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}(t),v(t))-\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0},v_{0})=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k},v^{K}_{k})-\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k-1},v^{K}_{k-1}).

Using the minimisation problem, we see that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k},v^{K}_{k})-\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k-1},v^{K}_{k-1})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k},v^{K}_{k})-\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k-1},v_{k}^{K})+\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k-1},v_{k}^{K})-\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k-1},v^{K}_{k-1})

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\Omega}\frac{b(v^{K}_{k})}{\alpha}\Big(\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k}))-\varphi^{*}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k-1}))\Big)\,\mathrm{d}x+\mathcal{H}(v^{K}_{k-1})-\mathcal{H}(v^{K}_{k})

\displaystyle=h_{K}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{b(v^{K}_{k})}{\alpha}F^{-1}(\alpha\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(s\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k-1})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k})\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x+\mathcal{H}(v^{K}_{k-1})-\mathcal{H}(v^{K}_{k}).

As a result, we obtain the inequality

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}(t),v(t))-\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0},v_{0})

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{H}(v_{0})-\mathcal{H}(v(t))+h_{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{b(v^{K}_{k})}{\alpha}F^{-1}(\alpha\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(s\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k-1})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k})\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x.

For the sum on the right-hand side, we rewrite it in the following form:

\displaystyle h_{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{b(v^{K}_{k})}{\alpha}F^{-1}(\alpha\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(s\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k-1})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k})\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{b(v^{K,+})}{\alpha}F^{-1}(\alpha\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(s\boldsymbol{u}^{K,+}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{u}^{K,-})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{K}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=\int_{(0,t)\setminus\mathcal{N}}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{b(v^{K,+})}{\alpha}F^{-1}(\alpha\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(s\boldsymbol{u}^{K,+}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{u}^{K,-})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{K}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

Considering the limit as
K\rightarrow\infty
, we have

\displaystyle b(v^{K}_{+})\rightarrow b(v)\quad\text{ strongly in }L^{q}\big(\big((0,t)\setminus\mathcal{N}\big)\times\Omega\big)\text{ for every }q\in[1,\infty),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}F^{-1}(\alpha\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(s\boldsymbol{u}^{K,+}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{u}^{K,-}))\,\mathrm{d}s\rightarrow F^{-1}(\alpha\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}))\quad\text{ strongly in }L^{\infty}(0,t;L^{p}(\Omega)^{d\times d}).

By the uniform boundedness of
v
and the fact that
\mathcal{N}
is null, combining these convergences, we deduce that

\displaystyle b(v^{K}_{+})\int_{0}^{1}F^{-1}(\alpha\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(s\boldsymbol{u}^{K,+}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{u}^{K,-}))\,\mathrm{d}s\rightarrow b(v)F^{-1}(\alpha\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}))\,\text{ strongly in }L^{\infty}(0,t;L^{p}(\Omega)^{d\times d}).

Suppose that
(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{K}_{t}))_{K}
converges weakly in
L^{p}((0,t)\times\Omega)^{d\times d}
to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t})
. Then we conclude that

\displaystyle\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}\int_{(0,t)\setminus\mathcal{N}}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{b(v^{K,+})}{\alpha}F^{-1}(\alpha\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(s\boldsymbol{u}^{K,+}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{u}^{K,-})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{K}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}b(v)F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s.

It follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}(t),v(t))+\mathcal{H}(v(t))\geq\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0},v_{0})+\mathcal{H}(v_{0})+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\frac{b(v)}{\alpha}F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s.

Reasoning as in Proposition
2.6
, this is sufficient to show that the energy-dissipation equality holds.
It remains to prove the weak convergence result for
(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{K}_{t}))_{K}
. We have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h_{K}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k}-\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k-1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h_{K}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}(t_{k}^{K})-\boldsymbol{u}(t^{K}_{k-1}))

\displaystyle=\frac{t_{k}^{K}-t^{K}_{k-1}}{h_{K}}\fint_{t^{K}_{k-1}}^{t^{K}_{k}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}(s))\,\mathrm{d}s,

where
\fint
denotes the usual average integral operator. Noting that the difference
\frac{t_{k}^{K}-t^{K}_{k-1}}{h_{K}}
is uniformly bounded with respect to
K
and
k
, as a result of the construction of the points
(t_{k}^{K})_{k}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{K}_{k})\|_{p^{\prime}}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t})\|_{L^{\infty}(L^{p^{\prime}})}.

This implies that

\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{K}_{t})\|_{L^{\infty}(L^{p^{\prime}})}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t})\|_{L^{\infty}(L^{p^{\prime}})}.

By the weak-* compactness of bounded sets in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
, the required convergence result follows.
∎

3.
Strain-limiting fracture problems

In this section, we consider a strain-limiting dynamic fracture problem, by which we mean that the strain is bounded
a priori
. Here, we present the state of the art of this paper. Taking
p\rightarrow 1+
in the constitutive relation from Section
2
, we obtain the relationship

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u})=\frac{\mathbf{T}}{|\mathbf{T}|}=:F_{1}(\mathbf{T}).

However, the function
F_{1}
is not an injection, nor is it continuous at the origin. To avoid the issue at the origin, and to also have an injective function in the constitutive relation, we regularise the denominator. This motivates the study of the relationship (
1.3
) for a fixed parameter
a>0
, that is,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u})=\frac{\mathbf{T}}{(1+|\mathbf{T}|^{a})^{\frac{1}{a}}}=:F(\mathbf{T}).

The parameter
a
is fixed throughout this section so we do not indicate the dependence of
F
on
a
. We prove an existence result for a dynamic fracture problem with a phase-field approximation and a strain-limiting relation, independent of the value of
a
.

As discussed previously, the main issue that we need to overcome in the analysis is the lack of integrability of the stress tensor
\mathbf{T}
. Indeed, the approximations of the stress tensor are
a priori
bounded in the non-reflexive space
L^{1}(Q)^{d\times d}
and so are weakly-* compact only in the space of Radon measures on
\overline{Q}
, but not in any Lebesgue function space. We need to use careful arguments to prove that the sequence of approximations converge in some sense to a Lebesgue function. Then, we need to prove that the convergence is sufficiently strong so that we can show that the elastodynamic equation and the constitutive relation both hold in the limit.

To do this, we require additional regularity of the phase-field function
v
.
To get improved spatial regularity estimates on the approximations of
\mathbf{T}
, we need
\nabla v
and its approximations to be uniformly bounded on
Q
.
From the construction of the problem, we expect the gradients to have absolute value to the order of
\epsilon^{-1}
. Indeed, the parameter
\epsilon
gives some sense of the ‘thickness’ of the approximation of the crack set. However,
proving a uniform bound in practice does not seem possible. Hence, in the spirit of
[
12
]
, we introduce an extra term in the minimisation problem (
2.2
) in order to guarantee this
L^{\infty}(Q)^{d}
regularity of
\nabla v
. The new term imposes a dependence of the problem on the speed at which the crack grows. It is referred to as a rate-dependent term because of the presence of the time derivative
v_{t}
.

In the strain-limiting setting, we have an added issue in the case that the Neumann part of the boundary is non-empty. As in the steady problem without fracture
[
3
]
, we experience a penalisation on this part of the boundary in the weak elastodynamic equation due to a lack of global integrability of approximations of the stress tensor.
Indeed, we prove that the approximations of the stress tensor converge in
L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathcal{M}(\overline{\Omega})^{d\times d})
and pointwise a.e. on
Q
to limits
\overline{\mathbf{T}}
and
\mathbf{T}
, respectively. Due to the choice of test function, any singularities on the Dirichlet part of the boundary do not appear, because the test functions vanish on the whole of
\Gamma_{D}
. However, we cannot control any difference between
\overline{\mathbf{T}}
and
\mathbf{T}
on the Neumann part of the boundary, because the test functions do not vanish there. This is the reason for the presence of the penalty term on
\Gamma_{N}
.
This is also the reason for the existence result being stronger in the case of fully Dirichlet boundary conditions; it suffices to obtain local convergence with respect to the spatial domain
\Omega
in order to prove the required results.
As a result of the error term in the mixed boundary condition case, we not only have a penalisation in the elastodynamic equation, but we are unable to deduce an energy-dissipation equality. Even if we assume
a priori
that the error term vanishes, the convergence results are not sufficiently strong to show that equality holds.

The problem of interest in this section is the following. For a fixed
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we look for a triple
(\boldsymbol{u},\mathbf{T},v):Q\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\times\mathbb{R}
that solves

(3.1)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{div}\big(b(v)\mathbf{T}\big)+\boldsymbol{f}

\displaystyle\text{ in }Q,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u})

\displaystyle=F(\mathbf{T})=\frac{\mathbf{T}}{(1+|\mathbf{T}|^{a})^{\frac{1}{a}}}

\displaystyle\text{ in }Q,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}\big|_{\Gamma_{D}}

\displaystyle=0,\,v\big|_{\Gamma_{D}}=1

\displaystyle\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Gamma_{D},

\displaystyle b(v)\mathbf{T}\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{g}

\displaystyle\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Gamma_{N},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{t}(0,\cdot)=\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\,v(0,\cdot)=v_{0}

\displaystyle\text{ in }\Omega,

subject to the minimisation problem

(3.2)

\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}(t),v(t))+\mathcal{H}(v(t))+\mathcal{G}_{k}(v(t),v_{t}(t))=\inf_{v\in H^{k}_{D+1}(\Omega),\,v\leq v(t)}\Big\{\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}(t),v)+\mathcal{H}(v)+\mathcal{G}_{k}(v,v_{t}(t))\Big\},

with energy-dissipation equality

(3.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(t;\boldsymbol{u}(t),\boldsymbol{u}_{t}(t),v(t))+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}b(v)\big(\mathbf{T}-F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}))\big]:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t})+\boldsymbol{f}_{t}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma_{N}}\boldsymbol{g}_{t}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\|v_{t}(s)\|_{k,2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(0;\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{1},v_{0}),

for every
t\in[0,T]
and the crack non-healing property
v_{t}\leq 0
. All functionals are defined as in Section
2
but with
F
given by (
3.1
). The functional
\mathcal{G}_{k}
is defined on
H^{k}(\Omega)\times H^{k}(\Omega)
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{k}(v,w)=(v,w)_{k,2}:=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\int_{\Omega}\partial^{\alpha}v\cdot\partial^{\alpha}w\,\mathrm{d}x.

Due to the presence of the rate-dependent term, we cannot guarantee that
v\geq 0
.
For a discussion regarding the physical implications of the inclusion of this functional on the model, we refer to
[
12
]
.

To ensure that the nonlinearity
b(v)
is still uniformly bounded from above,
in this section we define
b
by

\displaystyle b(v)=\max\{0,v\}^{2}+\eta.

We later see that the phase-field function and its approximations are uniformly bounded in the function space
L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{k}(\Omega)^{d})
. Hence, if
k>\frac{d}{2}
, by the Sobolev embedding theorem, we deduce that the functions are uniformly bounded on
Q
. As a result, in this case, which is a standing assumption of the result proven here, the proof is actually valid for the choice of
b
that is used in Section
2
.

We do not require a compatibility condition of type (
2.4
) because
v_{t}(0)
is not defined
a priori
.
Indeed, the minimisation problem does not make sense at the initial time.
However, we require a compatibility condition between the Neumann boundary data and the initial data. We demand that

(3.4)

\boldsymbol{g}(0)=b(v_{0})F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))\mathbf{n}\quad\text{ on }\Gamma_{N}.

This is vital in order to bound approximations of
\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}(0)
which is needed to obtain higher regularity estimates in the temporal variable of the approximations of the stress tensor. For (
3.4
) to make sense for the given initial data, we also demand the safety strain condition, that is, we have

(3.5)

\max\big\{\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\|_{\infty},\,\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\|_{\infty}\big\}=C_{*}<1.

Such a condition is discussed in detail in
[
3
]
. To ensure that the total energy functional is finite at the initial time, we require a bound on
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
, as well as
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
in the safety strain condition. The bound on
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
is non-standard in the literature. However, the function
\varphi^{*}
is not necessarily finite outside the open unit ball, which is why we include the extra assumption.

As with the analysis of time-dependent, strain-limiting problems without a phase field function (see, for example, the analysis in
[
9
]
and
[
8
]
), we approximate (
3.1
)–(
3.3
) by a regularised problem. The function
F
is replaced by a function
F_{n}
that contains an elliptic term. As a result,
F_{n}
is a bijection from
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
to itself and so the problem can be written in terms of only
\boldsymbol{u}
and
v
.
We define
F_{n}
by

\displaystyle F_{n}(\mathbf{T})=\frac{\mathbf{T}}{(1+|\mathbf{T}|^{a})^{\frac{1}{a}}}+\frac{\mathbf{T}}{n}.

with
\varphi_{n}
,
\varphi^{*}_{n}
and
\mathcal{E}_{n}
defined analogously. We approximate the body forces
l
in the regularised problem to ensure that a suitable compatibility condition analogous to (
3.4
) holds in the setting of the regularised problem. We replace the function
\boldsymbol{g}
by an approximation
\boldsymbol{g}^{n}
that we define by

(3.6)

\boldsymbol{g}^{n}=F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))\mathbf{n}\cdot\psi(nt)+\boldsymbol{g}(t,x)\cdot(1-\psi(nt)),

where
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}([0,\infty))
is a non-increasing function with
\psi=1
on
[0,\frac{1}{2}]
and
\psi=0
on
[1,\infty)
.
The external force approximation is defined by
l^{n}:=\boldsymbol{f}+\boldsymbol{g}^{n}
.
We define the total energy
\mathcal{F}_{n}
by replacing
\mathcal{E}
with
\mathcal{E}_{n}
and
l
with
l^{n}
in the definition of the functional
\mathcal{F}
. Under the assumption that
l\in W^{2,1}([0,T];W^{-1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d})
, the sequence
(l^{n})_{n}
is bounded in this space independent of
n
and converges weakly to
l
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

Remark
.

As mentioned in Section
1
, the results in this section can be generalised to a more generalised class of functions
F
. Indeed, the required assumptions on
F
are as follows. There exist positive constants
c_{0}
,
c_{1}
and
a
such that, for every
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
,

\displaystyle(F(\mathbf{T})-F(\mathbf{S})):(\mathbf{T}-\mathbf{S})

\displaystyle\geq 0,

\displaystyle F(\mathbf{T})\cdot\mathbf{T}

\displaystyle\geq c_{0}|\mathbf{T}|-c_{1},

\displaystyle|F(\mathbf{T})|

\displaystyle\leq c_{1},

that is, monotonicity, coercivity and boundedness, respectively. Furthermore, we assume that there exists a strictly convex function
\varphi\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that
\varphi(0)=\varphi^{\prime}(0)=0
and
|\varphi^{\prime\prime}(s)|\leq c_{1}(1+s)^{-1}
for every
s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, with
\varphi
being related to
F
by the representation

F(\mathbf{T})=\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(|\mathbf{T}|)}{|\mathbf{T}|}\mathbf{T}.

Finally, if we define the inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{T})}
, for
\mathbf{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, by

\displaystyle(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{U})_{\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{T})}=\sum_{i,j,k,l=1}^{d}\frac{\partial F_{ij}(\mathbf{T})}{\partial\mathbf{T}_{kl}}\mathbf{S}_{ij}\mathbf{U}_{kl},

then we require the lower bound

\displaystyle(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{S})_{\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{T})}\geq\frac{c_{0}|\mathbf{S}|^{2}}{(1+|\mathbf{T}|)^{1+a}},

for every
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
.

The structure of this section is as follows. First, we focus on proving the existence of a weak energy solution to the regularised problem, using a discretisation in time similar to that which is used in Section
2
. Then we look for
n
-independent bounds on the sequence of solutions, particularly those which involve higher regularity estimates.
At best, we have a bound on
(\mathbf{T}^{n})_{n}
in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
, a function space that does not have nice compactness properties. Hence, to obtain a pointwise convergence result for
(\mathbf{T}^{n})_{n}
, we use weighted regularity estimates. Using this pointwise convergence, if
\Gamma_{N}=\emptyset
, we can then improve the convergence to strong convergence in
L^{1}(0,T;L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
, making use of the monotonicity of
F
. If
a\in(0,\frac{2}{d})
, this is further improved to strong convergence in
L^{1+\delta}(0,T;L^{1+\delta}_{loc}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
for every
\delta>0
such that
a+\delta<\frac{2}{d}
.

We remark that we do not use a Galerkin approximation in space to deduce the existence of solutions to the regularised problem. This is due to the choice of regularisation term, which ensures that we are in the Hilbert space setting. This setting ensures that appropriate bounds are available which allow us to use the Browder–Minty theorem to prove existence of solutions to the time discrete problem, without also applying a Galerkin approximation. This is not the case when the constitutive function has arbitrary
p
-growth, as in Section
2
.

Let us now introduce the time-discrete approximation of the regularised problem.
Although the existence of a solution to the regularised problem follows from the proof in Section
2
, we need the structure of the time discrete problem to obtain higher time regularity estimates. In particular, we need to overcome the fact that the second time derivative
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}
is not necessarily continuous in the time variable. We do this by working with the time discrete problem and ensuring that
\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{0}
is suitably defined.

Theorem 3.1
.

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in W^{1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
and
l\in C^{1}([0,T];W^{-1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d})
. Let
l_{n}
be defined by (
3.6
). Fix
M
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\gamma=(n,M)
and define a time step
h=\frac{T}{M}
. We define initialisations of the time discrete problem by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{0}=\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\quad\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{-1}=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-h\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\quad v^{\gamma}_{0}=v_{0}.

Furthermore, we denote
l^{\gamma}_{m}=l(t^{M}_{m})
where
t^{M}_{m}=mh
for
0\leq m\leq M
.
Defining the solution sequence recursively, for every
1\leq m\leq M
there exists a unique
\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\in W^{1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
such that

(3.7)

\int_{\Omega}\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}+b(v^{\gamma}_{m-1})F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{w})\,\mathrm{d}x=\langle l^{\gamma}_{m},\boldsymbol{w}\rangle,

for every
\boldsymbol{w}\in W^{1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
,
and a unique
v^{\gamma}_{m}\in H^{k}_{D+1}(\Omega)
such that

(3.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{n}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m},v^{\gamma}_{m})+\mathcal{H}(v^{\gamma}_{m})+\frac{1}{2h}\mathcal{G}_{k}(v^{\gamma}_{m}-v^{\gamma}_{m-1},v^{\gamma}_{m}-v^{\gamma}_{m-1})

\displaystyle=\inf\Big\{\mathcal{E}_{n}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m},v)+\mathcal{H}(v)+\frac{1}{2h}\mathcal{G}_{k}(v-v^{\gamma}_{m-1},v-v^{\gamma}_{m-1})\,:\,v\in H^{k}_{D+1}(\Omega),\,,v\leq v^{\gamma}_{m-1}\Big\}.

The proof follows identical reasoning to that of Theorem
2.3
so is not repeated here. Similarly, reasoning as with Proposition
2.2
, Corollary
2.1
and Proposition
2.3
, we deduce the following four results concerning the minimisation problem and energy-dissipation inequality.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
3.1
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m},v^{\gamma}_{m})_{m=1}^{M}
be the solution of the time discrete problem. For every
1\leq m\leq M
and every
\tilde{\chi}\in H^{k}_{D+1}(\Omega)
with
\tilde{\chi}\leq v^{\gamma}_{m-1}
, we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\big[\partial_{v}\mathcal{E}_{n}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m},v^{\gamma}_{m})+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v^{\gamma}_{m})+\frac{1}{2h}\partial_{v}\mathcal{G}_{k}(v^{\gamma}_{m}-v^{\gamma}_{m-1},v^{\gamma}_{m}-v^{\gamma}_{m-1})\big](\tilde{\chi}-v^{\gamma}_{m-1})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\Big\{\frac{b^{\prime}(v^{\gamma}_{m})}{\alpha}(\tilde{\chi}-v^{\gamma}_{m-1})\varphi^{*}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}))+\frac{1}{2\epsilon}(v^{\gamma}_{m}-1)(\tilde{\chi}-v^{\gamma}_{m-1})

\displaystyle\quad+2\epsilon\nabla v^{\gamma}_{m}\cdot\nabla(\tilde{\chi}-v^{\gamma}_{m-1})\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{h}\big(v^{\gamma}_{m}-v^{\gamma}_{m-1},\tilde{\chi}-v^{\gamma}_{m-1}\big)_{k,2}.

Corollary 3.1
.

For every
1\leq m\leq M
and every
\chi\in H^{k}_{D}(\Omega)
with
\chi\leq 0
, we have

\displaystyle 0\leq\big[\partial_{v}\mathcal{E}_{n}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m},v^{\gamma}_{m})+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v^{\gamma}_{m})+\frac{1}{2h}\partial_{v}\mathcal{G}_{k}(v^{\gamma}_{m}-v^{\gamma}_{m-1},v^{\gamma}_{m}-v^{\gamma}_{m-1})\big](\chi).

Corollary 3.2
.

For every
1\leq m\leq M
, we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\big[\partial_{v}\mathcal{E}_{n}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m},v^{\gamma}_{m})+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v^{\gamma}_{m})+\frac{1}{2h}\partial_{v}\mathcal{G}_{k}(v^{\gamma}_{m}-v^{\gamma}_{m-1},v^{\gamma}_{m}-v^{\gamma}_{m-1})\big](\delta v^{\gamma}_{h})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\Big\{\frac{b^{\prime}(v^{\gamma}_{m})}{\alpha}\delta v^{\gamma}_{m}\varphi^{*}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}))+\frac{1}{2\epsilon}(v^{\gamma}_{m}-1)\delta v^{\gamma}_{m}+2\epsilon\nabla v^{\gamma}_{m}\cdot\nabla\delta v^{\gamma}_{m}\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\|\delta v^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{k,2}^{2}.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
3.1
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m},v^{\gamma}_{m})_{m=1}^{M}
be the solution of the time discrete problem. For every
1\leq m\leq M
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{n}(t^{M}_{m};\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m},\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m},v^{\gamma}_{m})+h\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|\delta v^{\gamma}_{j}\|_{k,2}^{2}+h\sum_{j=1}^{m}\langle\delta l^{\gamma}_{m},\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+h\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int_{\Omega}b(v^{\gamma}_{j-1})\big[F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{n}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{j}))-F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{j}))\big]:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{j})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{F}_{n}(0;\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{1},v_{0}).

Now we concentrate on obtaining various
M
-independent bounds on the discrete solution sequence. Then we take the limit as
M\rightarrow\infty
to obtain a weak energy solution of the regularised problem. The time continuous problem is more amenable to work with when it comes to higher spatial regularity estimates, so we cannot derive these estimates until we have taken the limit as
M\rightarrow\infty
.
To this end, using the fact that

\displaystyle(F^{-1}_{n}(\mathbf{T})-F^{-1}_{n}(\mathbf{S})):(\mathbf{T}-\mathbf{S})\geq C(n)|\mathbf{T}-\mathbf{S}|^{2},

for every
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, where
C=C(n)
is a constant depending only on
n
, we immediately obtain an
M
-independent bound from the energy-dissipation inequality.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
3.1
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m},v^{\gamma}_{m})_{m=1}^{M}
be the solution of the time discrete problem. There exists a constant
C=C(n)
, independent of
M
, such that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq m\leq M}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{1,2}+\max_{1\leq m\leq M}\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{2}+\max_{1\leq m\leq M}\|v^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{k,2}+h\sum_{m=1}^{M}\Big(\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{1,2}^{2}+\|\delta v^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{k,2}^{2}\Big)\leq C.

The next bound in Lemma
3.3
gives us information on the second derivative
\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}
. As a result, we are able to improve the bound on
(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m})_{m=1}^{M}
also. In Lemma
3.4
, we improve the bounds on the phase-field function. This gives sufficient bounds on the corresponding interpolant functions, which we collect in Corollary
3.3
. Then we take the limit as
M\rightarrow\infty
in Proposition
3.2
and identify the limiting couple as a weak energy solution of the regularised problem.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
3.1
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m},v^{\gamma}_{m})_{m=1}^{M}
be the solution of the time discrete problem. There exists a constant
C=C(n)
independent of
M
such that

\displaystyle h\sum_{m=1}^{M}\|\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}+\max_{1\leq m\leq M}\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{1,2}\leq C.

Proof.

Testing in (
3.7
) against
h(\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m})
, we rewrite the term involving the nonlinearity in the following way:

(3.9)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}b(v^{\gamma}_{m-1})F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(h(\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}))\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}b(v^{\gamma}_{m-1})F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle-\int_{\Omega}b(v^{\gamma}_{m-1})F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle-\int_{\Omega}\Big\{b(v^{\gamma}_{m-1})\big[F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}))-F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}))\big]

\displaystyle:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1})\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}x.

We define the fourth order tensor
\mathcal{B}_{n}(\mathbf{T})
, where
\mathbf{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{sym}
, by

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{n}^{ijkl}(\mathbf{T})=\frac{\partial(F^{-1}_{n})_{ij}}{\partial T_{kl}}(\mathbf{T}).

This defines an inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{sym}
in the following way:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{U})_{\mathcal{B}_{n}(\mathbf{T})}=\mathcal{B}^{ijkl}_{n}(\mathbf{T})S_{ij}U_{kl}.

It follows that, for every
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{sym}
,

(3.10)

(F^{-1}_{n}(\mathbf{T})-F^{-1}_{n}(\mathbf{S})):\mathbf{T}=\int_{0}^{1}(\mathbf{T},\mathbf{T}-\mathbf{S})_{\mathcal{B}_{n}(s\mathbf{T}+(1-s)\mathbf{S})}\,\mathrm{d}s.

On the other hand, we also have

\displaystyle\varphi_{n}(F^{-1}_{n}(\mathbf{T}))-\varphi_{n}(F^{-1}_{n}(\mathbf{S}))

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)(\mathbf{T}-\mathbf{S},\mathbf{T}-\mathbf{S})_{\mathcal{B}_{n}(s\mathbf{T}+(1-s)\mathbf{S})}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{1}(\mathbf{T},\mathbf{T}-\mathbf{S})_{\mathcal{B}_{n}(s\mathbf{T}+(1-s)\mathbf{S})}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Substituting this into (
3.10
), it follows that

\displaystyle(F^{-1}_{n}(\mathbf{T})-F^{-1}_{n}(\mathbf{S})):\mathbf{T}

\displaystyle=\varphi_{n}(F^{-1}_{n}(\mathbf{T}))-\varphi_{n}(F^{-1}_{n}(\mathbf{S}))+\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)(\mathbf{T}-\mathbf{S},\mathbf{T}-\mathbf{S})_{\mathcal{B}_{n}(s\mathbf{T}+(1-s)\mathbf{S})}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\geq\varphi_{n}(F^{-1}_{n}(\mathbf{T}))-\varphi_{n}(F^{-1}_{n}(\mathbf{S})).

Returning to (
3.9
), we deduce that

(3.11)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}b(v^{\gamma}_{m-1})F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(h(\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}))\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\Omega}b(v^{\gamma}_{m-1})F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle-\int_{\Omega}b(v^{\gamma}_{m-1})F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle+\int_{\Omega}b(v^{\gamma}_{m-1})\big[\varphi_{n}(F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1})))-\varphi_{n}(F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m})))\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}b(v^{\gamma}_{m-1})\big[\varphi^{*}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}))-\varphi^{*}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}))\big]\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\Omega}b(v^{\gamma}_{m})\varphi^{*}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}))-b(v^{\gamma}_{m-1})\varphi^{*}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}))\,\mathrm{d}x.

We insert (
3.11
) into the elastodynamic equation and rewrite the terms involving
\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}
using (
2.16
) to deduce that

\displaystyle h\|\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\Big(\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}-\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}-\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}\|_{2}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\Omega}b(v^{\gamma}_{m})\varphi^{*}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}))-b(v^{\gamma}_{m-1})\varphi^{*}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}))\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\langle l^{\gamma}_{m},\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\rangle-\langle l^{\gamma}_{m-1},\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}\rangle+h\langle\delta l^{\gamma}_{m},\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}\rangle.

Using this inequality recursively and cancelling appropriate terms, we deduce that

\displaystyle h\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}+\int_{\Omega}b(v^{\gamma}_{m})\varphi^{*}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}))\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq C\Big[\|l^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{-1,2}\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m})\|_{2}+\|l^{\gamma}_{0}\|_{-1,2}\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\|_{2}+h\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|\delta l^{\gamma}_{j}\|_{-1,2}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+h\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{j}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{j})\|_{2}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}+\int_{\Omega}b(v_{0})\varphi^{*}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))\,\mathrm{d}x\Big].

Using Young’s inequality and the fact that
\varphi^{*}_{n}(\mathbf{T})\geq c(n)|\mathbf{T}|^{2}
to absorb the first term on the right-hand side into the left, applying the bound in Lemma
3.2
it follows that for a constant
C=C(n)
, independent of
h
and
m
, we have

\displaystyle h\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}+\int_{\Omega}b(v^{\gamma}_{m})\varphi^{*}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}))\,\mathrm{d}x\leq C.

Optimising with respect to
m
on the left-hand side, we obtain the required result.
∎

Lemma 3.4
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
3.1
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m},v^{\gamma}_{m})_{m=1}^{M}
be the solution of the time discrete problem. Suppose additionally that
k>\frac{d}{2}
. There exists a constant
C=C(n)
, independent of
M
, such that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq m\leq M}\|\delta v^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{k,2}\leq C.

Proof.

Using Corollary
3.2
, we have

\displaystyle\|\delta v^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{k,2}^{2}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega}\Big\{\frac{b^{\prime}(v^{\gamma}_{m})}{\alpha}\delta v^{\gamma}_{m}\varphi^{*}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}))+\frac{1}{2\epsilon}(v^{\gamma}_{m}-1)\delta v^{\gamma}_{m}+2\epsilon\nabla v^{\gamma}_{m}\cdot\nabla\delta v^{\gamma}_{h}\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\frac{b^{\prime}(1)}{\alpha}\|\delta v^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{\infty}\|\varphi^{*}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}))\|_{1}+\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\|v^{\gamma}_{m}-1\|_{2}\|\delta v^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{2}+2\epsilon\|\nabla v^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{2}\|\nabla\delta v^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big[\Big(\int_{\Omega}\varphi^{*}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}))\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{2}+\|v^{\gamma}_{m}-1\|_{2}^{2}+\|\nabla v^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}\Big]+\frac{1}{2}\|\delta v^{\gamma}\|_{k,2}^{2}.

We absorb the final term into the left-hand side and use Lemma
3.2
to bound the remaining terms.
∎

Corollary 3.3
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
3.1
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m},v^{\gamma}_{m})_{m=1}^{M}
be the solution of the time discrete problem. Suppose additionally that
k>\frac{d}{2}
. Using the notation of Section
2
, there exists a constant
C=C(n)
, independent of
M
, such that

\displaystyle\max_{t\in[0,T]}\|\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\gamma}(t)\|_{1,2}+\max_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma,\pm}(t)\|_{1,2}+\max_{t\in[0,T]}\|\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\gamma,\prime}(t)\|_{1,2}+\max_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma,\pm,\prime}(t)\|_{1,2}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma,+,\prime\prime}(t)\|_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\max_{t\in[0,T]}\|\overline{v}^{\gamma}(t)\|_{k,2}+\max_{t\in[0,T]}\|v^{\gamma,\pm}(t)\|_{k,2}+\max_{t\in[0,T]}\|v^{\gamma,+,\prime}(t)\|_{k,2}

\displaystyle\leq C.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
3.1
hold. Suppose additionally that
k>\frac{d}{2}
. There exists a weak energy solution
(\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\mathbf{T}^{n},v^{n})
of the regularised problem in the following sense. The weak elastodynamic equation holds, that is, for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
, we have

(3.12)

\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{w}+b(v^{n}(t))\mathbf{T}^{n}(t):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{w})\,\mathrm{d}x=\langle l^{n}(t),\boldsymbol{w}\rangle,

for every
\boldsymbol{w}\in W^{1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
, where the stress tensor
\mathbf{T}^{n}
is identified by the constitutive relation

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})=F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})\quad\text{ a.e. in }Q.

For a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, the following minimisation problem is satisfied:

(3.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{n}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(t),v^{n}(t))+\mathcal{H}(v^{n}(t))+\mathcal{G}_{k}(v^{n}(t),v^{n}_{t}(t))

\displaystyle=\inf\Big\{\mathcal{E}_{n}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(t),v)+\mathcal{H}(v)+\mathcal{G}_{k}(v,v^{n}_{t}(t))\,:\,v\in H^{k}_{D+1}(\Omega),\,v\leq v^{n}(t)\Big\},

Furthermore, for every
t\in[0,T]
, we have the energy-dissipation balance

(3.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{n}(t;\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(t),\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}(t),v^{n}(t))+\int_{0}^{t}\langle l^{n}_{t}(s),\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(s)\rangle+\|v^{n}_{t}(s)\|_{k,2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}b(v^{n})\big[F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}))-F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}))\big]:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{n}(0;\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{1},v_{0}),

and the non-healing property
v^{n}_{t}\leq 0
. The initial conditions hold in the sense that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 0+}\Big[\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{1,2}+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}(t)-\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\|_{2}+\|v^{n}(t)-v_{0}\|_{k,2}\Big]=0.

Furthermore, there exists a subsequence (not relabelled) in
M
, independent of
n
, such that the following convergence results hold:

•

\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{(n,M)}\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}
weakly-* in
W^{1,\infty}(0,T;W^{1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d})
;

•

\boldsymbol{u}^{(n,M),\pm}
,
\boldsymbol{u}^{(n,M),\pm,\prime}\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}
,
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}
weakly-* in
L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d})
, respectively;

•

\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{(n,M),\prime}\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}
weakly-* in
L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d})
and weakly in
W^{1,2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)^{d})
;

•

\overline{v}^{(n,M)}\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}v^{n}
weakly-* in
W^{1,\infty}(0,T;H^{k}(\Omega))
;

•

v^{(n,M),\pm}\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}v^{n}
weakly-* in
L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{k}_{D+1}(\Omega))
.

Proof.

The stated convergence results follow immediately from standard compactness results for Bochner spaces. By the Aubin–Lions lemma and the fact that
k>\frac{d}{2}\geq 1
, it follows that
(\overline{v}^{(n,M)})_{M}
converges strongly in
C([0,T];H^{1}(\Omega))
to
v^{n}
. As a result,
(v^{(n,M),\pm})_{M}
converges strongly in
L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega))
to the same limit. Satisfaction of the initial condition for
v^{n}
follows from the strong convergence in
C([0,T];H^{1}(\Omega))
. We argue similarly for
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(0)
and
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}(0)
, applying the Aubin–Lions lemma again.

Defining the approximate stress tensor
\mathbf{T}^{(n,M)}=F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\partial_{t}\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{(n,M)}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{(n,M),+}))
, we see from Lemmas
3.2
and
3.3
that
(\mathbf{T}^{(n,M)})_{M}
is bounded in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
. Hence it converges in this space to a limiting function
\mathbf{T}^{n}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
.

Rewriting the elastodynamic equation (
3.7
) in terms of the interpolants, multiplying by an arbitrary
\chi\in C([0,T])
and integrating over
[0,T]
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{Q}\partial_{t}\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\gamma,\prime}\cdot(\chi\boldsymbol{w})+b(v^{\gamma,-})\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\chi\boldsymbol{w})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{T}\langle l^{\gamma,+},\chi\boldsymbol{w}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t,

for every
\boldsymbol{w}\in W^{1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
. Letting
M\rightarrow\infty
and using Lebesgue’s differentiation theorem, it follows that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}^{n}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{w}+b(v^{n}(t))\mathbf{T}^{n}(t):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{w})\,\mathrm{d}x=\langle l^{n}(t),\boldsymbol{w}\rangle,

for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
and every
\boldsymbol{w}\in W^{1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
.

Next, noticing that
(\partial_{t}\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{(n,M),\prime}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{(n,M),+})_{M}
converges strongly in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)^{d})
, by the Aubin–Lions lemma, we deduce that

(3.15)
