\displaystyle\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\int_{Q}b(v^{(n,M),-})\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\partial_{t}\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{(n,M),\prime}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{(n,M),+})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\Big\{-\int_{Q}\partial_{t}\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{(n,M),\prime}\cdot(\partial_{t}\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{(n,M),\prime}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{(n,M),+})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\langle l^{(n,M),+},\partial_{t}\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{(n,M),\prime}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{(n,M),+}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t\Big\}

\displaystyle=-\int_{Q}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\langle l^{n},\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{Q}b(v^{n})\mathbf{T}^{n}:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t.

Applying Minty’s method, as in the proof of Theorem
2.6
, it follows from (
3.15
) that we have
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})=F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})
pointwise a.e. in
Q
.

To prove that the minimisation problem (
3.13
) is satisfied, we need a strong convergence result for
(\boldsymbol{u}^{(n,M),+})_{M}
and
({\boldsymbol{u}}^{(n,M),\prime,+})_{M}
in
L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega)^{d})
.
Such convergence results allow us to deal with the presence of nonlinearities in the minimisation problem and energy-dissipation balance. Adapting the reasoning in
[
28
]
(namely, Lemmas 3.9, 3.10 and 3.11) to the nonlinear problem, we deduce that this is the case. Indeed, we have that

(3.16)

\boldsymbol{u}^{(n,M),+}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\quad\boldsymbol{u}^{(n,M),\prime,+}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}\quad\text{ strongly in }L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega)^{d}).

As a result, we also have
\mathbf{T}^{(n,M)}\rightarrow\mathbf{T}^{n}
strongly in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
as
M\rightarrow\infty
.
Now, we know that the satisfaction of the minimisation problem (
3.13
) is equivalent to

(3.17)

0\leq\big[\partial_{v}\mathcal{E}_{n}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(t),v^{n}(t))+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v^{n}(t))+\partial_{v}\mathcal{G}_{k}(v^{n}(t),v^{n}_{t}(t))\big](\chi),

for every
\chi\in H^{k}_{D}(\Omega)
with
\chi\leq 0
, for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
.
However, (
3.17
) follows from taking
M\rightarrow\infty
in Corollary
3.1
, applying the weak convergence results for the approximations of the phase-field function and the strong convergence result (
3.16
).

It remains to show that the energy-dissipation equality (
3.14
) holds.
Using the convergence results, weak lower semi-continuity of norms and Fatou’s lemma, it follows that (
3.14
) holds with “
=
” replaced by “
\leq
”. For the opposite inequality, we argue as in the proof of Proposition
2.6
, adapting the reasoning slightly to take care of the extra rate-dependent term.
∎

Now that we have the existence of a solution to the regularised problem, the focus is to obtain appropriate
n
-independent bounds that allow us to take the limit as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Before formulating these bounds, we state a consequence of the energy-dissipation equality that is analogous to the result in Corollary
2.1
.
We differentiate the energy-dissipation equality, carefully apply the chain rule for weak derivatives and test in the elastodynamic equation against
\boldsymbol{u}_{t}^{n}
. The remaining terms must vanish and are exactly the left-hand side of (
3.18
). This result is vital to the proof of Lemma
3.7
. In turn, Lemma
3.7
is needed to obtain a bound on
(v^{n}_{t})_{n}
in
L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{k}(\Omega))
. Provided that
k>\frac{d}{2}+1
, by the Sobolev embedding theorem it follows that
(v^{n})_{n}
is uniformly bounded in
W^{1,\infty}(0,T;W^{1,\infty}(\Omega))
, which is required for the proof of higher regularity estimates for the sequence
(\mathbf{T}^{n})_{n}
.

Corollary 3.4
.

Let the assumptions of Proposition
3.2
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\mathbf{T}^{n},v^{n})
be the solution triple constructed there. For a.e.
t\in[0,T]
,

(3.18)

\big[\partial_{v}\mathcal{E}_{n}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(t),v^{n}(t))+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v^{n}(t))+\partial_{v}\mathcal{G}_{k}(v^{n}(t),v_{t}^{n}(t))\big](v^{n}_{t}(t))=0.

Now we derive
n
-independent bounds on the solution triple
(\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\mathbf{T}^{n},v^{n})
. Testing in the elastodynamic equation (
3.12
) against
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}
, we reason as in
[
9
]
to obtain Lemma
3.5
. The bound on the gradient of
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}
follows from using the bound on
\mathbf{T}^{n}
, the constitutive relation, the fundamental theorem of calculus and the Korn–Poincaré inequality. Indeed, we recall the memory kernel property

(3.19)

\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(t))=\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha(t-\tau)}F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n}(\tau))\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

Furthermore, we have
|F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})|\leq 1+n^{-1}|\mathbf{T}^{n}|
. The representation (
3.19
) is vital to obtaining improved bounds on the solution triple, including using bounds on the linear combination
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}
to yield bounds on the function
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let the assumptions of Proposition
3.2
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\mathbf{T}^{n},v^{n})
be the solution triple constructed there. There exists a constant
C
, independent of
n
, such that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(t)\|_{1,2}+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}(t)\|_{2}+\int_{Q}|\mathbf{T}^{n}|+\frac{|\mathbf{T}^{n}|^{2}}{n}+|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C.

Combining this with the energy-dissipation inequality immediately yields the improved bound in Lemma
3.6
. Indeed, from Lemma
3.5
, we use the bound on
(\boldsymbol{u}^{n})_{n}
in
L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega)^{d})
to bound the terms in the energy-dissipation equality that result from the presence of an external force.

At this point, we assume the safety strain condition (
3.5
) so that the sequence
(\varphi^{*}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0})))_{n}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{1}(\Omega)
. We recall that convex conjugation is order reversing so, for every
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, we have
\varphi^{*}_{n}(\mathbf{S})\leq\varphi^{*}(\mathbf{S})
. It follows that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\varphi^{*}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{\Omega}\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0})|}\frac{s}{(1-s^{a})^{\frac{1}{a}}}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\frac{|\Omega|C_{*}^{2}}{(1-C_{*}^{a})^{\frac{1}{a}}},

where
C_{*}
is the constant from the safety strain condition (
3.5
).

Lemma 3.6
.

Let the assumptions of Proposition
3.2
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\mathbf{T}^{n},v^{n})
be the solution triple constructed there. Suppose additionally that the safety strain condition (
3.5
) holds. There exists a constant
C
, independent of
n
, such that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\Big[\int_{\Omega}\varphi^{*}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(t)))\,\mathrm{d}x\Big]+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|v^{n}(t)\|_{k,2}+\int_{0}^{T}\|v^{n}_{t}(t)\|_{k,2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C.

Adapting the argument in Lemma
3.4
and making use of Corollary
3.4
, we can improve the bound on
(v^{n}_{t})_{n}
in
L^{2}(0,T;H^{k}(\Omega))
to a bound in
L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{k}(\Omega))
. As a result and recalling that
k>\frac{d}{2}
, it follows that
(v^{n}_{t})_{n}
is bounded in
L^{\infty}(Q)
, independent of
n
. This is used to improve the time regularity of the approximations
(\mathbf{T}^{n})_{n}
, which is the subject of Proposition
3.3
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let the assumptions of Proposition
3.2
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\mathbf{T}^{n},v^{n})
be the solution triple constructed there. Suppose additionally that the safety strain condition (
3.5
) holds. There exists a constant
C
independent of
n
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|v^{n}_{t}(t)\|_{k,2}\leq C.

With Lemmas
3.5
,
3.6
and
3.7
in mind, we work on improving the regularity of
(\mathbf{T}^{n})_{n}
. Indeed, we look for weighted estimates on the derivatives
(\mathbf{T}^{n}_{t})_{n}
and
(\nabla\mathbf{T}^{n})_{n}
which allow us to prove a pointwise convergence result on
Q
. For the pointwise result, we mimic some of the ideas from
[
3
]
. However, significant adaptations are made as we work in the time-dependent setting. Also, we have the added complication of the phase-field function. To deal with this, Lemma
3.7
is vital.

We begin with studying the regularity in time. The proof of Proposition
3.3
is one of the highlights of this work. As mentioned previously, we work directly with the time discrete approximation. If we were to work with the time continuous solution, we would require
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}
to be continuous at
t=0
. This is not necessarily true. However, to work with the time discrete solution, we must define an extra term
\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{-2}
in the solution sequence in order to define
\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{0}
. By our choice of
\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{-2}
, we show that
\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{0}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
, independent of
n
and
M
. We deduce from this that
(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt})_{n}
is bounded in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)^{d})
, independent of
n
. Furthermore, we see that we have
\mathbf{T}^{n}\in W^{1,2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
and obtain a weighted bound on the time derivative
\mathbf{T}^{n}_{t}
that is independent of
n
.

At this stage, we impose the compatibility condition (
3.4
). Under the compatibility condition, we know that the sequence of approximations
(l^{n})_{n}
is bounded in
W^{2,1}(0,T;W^{-1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d})
and converges weakly in this space to
l
. Furthermore, it becomes clear why the approximation choice (
3.6
) is made. Indeed, suppose that
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}
is continuous at
t=0
and sufficiently smooth so that the following reasoning is justified. Testing in the elastodynamic equation at
t=0
against
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(0)
, we get

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(0)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}-b(v^{n}(0))\mathbf{T}^{n}(0)\cdot\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(0))\,\mathrm{d}x+\langle l^{n}(0),\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(0)\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}-b(v_{0})F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))\cdot\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(0)+\boldsymbol{f}(0)\cdot\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(0)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\Gamma_{N}}F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))\mathbf{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(0)\,\mathrm{d}S

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\big(\mathrm{div}(b(v_{0})F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0})))+\boldsymbol{f}(0)\big)\cdot\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(0)\,\mathrm{d}x,

where the boundary integral vanishes due to integration by parts and the choice of
\boldsymbol{g}^{n}(0)
. It remains to show that
\mathrm{div}(b(v_{0})F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0})))
is bounded in
L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
, independent of
n
, to deduce that
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(0)
is also bounded in
L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
, independent of
n
. We use analogous reasoning in the proof of Proposition
3.3
to show that the discrete time derivative
\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{0}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
. Without the compatibility condition for
\boldsymbol{g}^{n}
, we get a boundary term involving
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(0)
, so we would need a bound on
(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(0))_{n}
in
W^{1,2}(\Omega)^{d}
. However, this does not appear to be possible, thus justifying our choice of approximation and the compatibility condition (
3.4
).

Proposition 3.3
.

Let the assumptions of Proposition
3.2
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\mathbf{T}^{n},v^{n})
be the solution triple constructed there. Suppose additionally that

l\in W^{2,1}(0,T;W^{-1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d})
,
the safety strain condition (
3.5
) holds, the compatibility condition (
3.4
) holds and that
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in W^{2,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
. Then
\mathbf{T}^{n}\in W^{1,2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
and there exists a constant
C
, independent of
n
, such that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(t)\|_{2}+\int_{Q}\frac{|\mathbf{T}^{n}_{t}|^{2}}{(1+|\mathbf{T}^{n}|)^{1+a}}+\frac{|\mathbf{T}^{n}_{t}|^{2}}{n}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C.

Proof.

We define
\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m}=F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}))
for
0\leq m\leq M
, where
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m},v^{\gamma}_{m})_{m=0}^{M}
is the solution sequence for the time discrete problem in Theorem
3.1
, with
\beta=(n,M)
.
We fix
2\leq m\leq M
, test in the elastodynamic equation (
3.7
) at time levels
m
and
m-1
against
\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}
and subtract the resulting equalities. Using (
2.16
), we rewrite the interial terms in the usual way. For the body force
l^{n}
, to avoid having terms involving spatial derivatives of
\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}
, which we cannot necessarily bound, we write

\displaystyle\langle l^{\gamma}_{m}-l^{\gamma}_{m-1},\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\delta l^{\gamma}_{m},\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\rangle-\langle\delta l^{\gamma}_{m-1},\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m-1}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+h\langle\delta^{2}l^{\gamma}_{m},\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{m-1}^{\gamma}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{m-1}^{\gamma}\rangle.

For the nonlinear terms, we write

(3.20)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\big[b(v^{\gamma}_{m-1})\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m}-b(v^{\gamma}_{m-2})\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m-1}\big]:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\Big\{b(v^{\gamma}_{m-1})\big(\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m}-\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m-1}\big):\frac{F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m})-F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m-1})}{h}

\displaystyle+\big(b(v^{\gamma}_{m-1})-b(v^{\gamma}_{m-2})\big)\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m-1}:\frac{F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m})-F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m-1})}{h}\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=h\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}b(v^{\gamma}_{m-1})\Big(\frac{\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m}-\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m-1}}{h},\frac{\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m}-\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m-1}}{h}\Big)_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m-1})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle+h\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}\Big(\frac{b(v^{\gamma}_{m-1})-b(v^{\gamma}_{m-2})}{h}\Big)\cdot\Big(\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m-1},\frac{\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m}-\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m-1}}{h}\Big)_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m-1})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq\frac{h}{2}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}b(v^{\gamma}_{m-1})\Big(\frac{\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m}-\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m-1}}{h},\frac{\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m}-\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m-1}}{h}\Big)_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m-1})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle-\frac{h}{2\eta}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}\Big(\frac{b(v^{\gamma}_{m-1})-b(v^{\gamma}_{m-2})}{h}\Big)^{2}\big(\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m-1},\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m-1}\big)_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m-1})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x,

where
\mathcal{A}_{n}(\mathbf{U})
is the fourth-order tensor defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{n}(\mathbf{U})_{ijkl}=\frac{\partial F_{n}(\mathbf{U})_{ij}}{\partial U_{kl}}

with corresponding bilinear form

\displaystyle(\mathbf{T},\mathbf{S})_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(\mathbf{U})}:=\sum_{i,j,k,l=1}^{d}\mathcal{A}_{n}(\mathbf{U})_{ijkl}T_{ij}S_{kl}.

We can easily check that
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(\mathbf{U})}
defines an inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, justifying the use of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality in (
3.20
). Summing the result over the indices
2\leq j\leq m
, we deduce that

(3.21)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Big(\|\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}-\|\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\Big)+\alpha(\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m},\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m})-\alpha(\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{1},\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{1})

\displaystyle+\frac{h}{2}\sum_{j=2}^{m}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}b(v^{\gamma}_{j-1})\Big(\delta\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j},\delta\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j}\Big)_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j-1})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle-\alpha h\sum_{j=2}^{m}(\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{j},\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{j-1})

\displaystyle\leq\langle\delta l^{\gamma}_{m},\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\rangle-\langle\delta l^{\gamma}_{1},\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{1}\rangle+h\sum_{j=2}^{m}\langle\delta^{2}l^{\gamma}_{j},\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{j-1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{j-1}\rangle

\displaystyle+\frac{h}{2\eta}\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}\Big(\frac{b(v^{\gamma}_{j})-b(v^{\gamma}_{j-1})}{h}\Big)^{2}(\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j},\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j})_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j+1}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x.

To bound the terms involving the index 1, in particular, the term
\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{1}
, we need to define
\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{0}
. Equivalently, we look for a
\boldsymbol{u}_{-2}^{\gamma}\in L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
such that, for every
\boldsymbol{w}\in W^{1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
,

(3.22)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{0}-2\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{-1}+\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{-2}}{h^{2}}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega}b(v_{0})F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{0}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{0})):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{w})\,\mathrm{d}x+\langle l^{n}(0),\boldsymbol{w}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\Big[\mathrm{div}\Big(b(v_{0})F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))\Big)+\boldsymbol{f}(0)\Big]:\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}x,

using integration by parts and the compatibility condition satisfied by the approximation
(l^{n})_{n}
. Under the regularity assumptions, namely,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in W^{2,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathrm{div}\Big(b(v_{0})F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))\Big)+\boldsymbol{f}(0)\Big\|_{2}\leq C,

where
C
is independent of
n
.
The bound on
\mathrm{div}(b(v_{0})F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0})))
in
L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
can be determined from the following reasoning. Performing the differentiation, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}\big(b(v_{0})F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))\big)_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\Big(b(v_{0})F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))_{ij}\Big)

\displaystyle=b^{\prime}(v_{0})\frac{\partial v_{0}}{\partial x_{j}}F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))_{ij}+b(v_{0})\mathcal{A}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))_{ijkl}\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0})_{kl}}{\partial x_{j}}.

Next, we notice that

\displaystyle|F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))|\leq|F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))|\leq\frac{C_{*}}{(1+C_{*}^{a})^{\frac{1}{a}}},

and from direct calculation, we have

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{A}_{n}(\mathbf{U})_{ijkl}\big|=\Big|\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}\Big(\frac{1}{(1+|\mathbf{U}|^{a})^{\frac{1}{a}}}+\frac{1}{n}\Big)-\frac{|\mathbf{U}|^{a-2}\mathbf{U}_{ij}\mathbf{U}_{kl}}{(1+|\mathbf{U}|^{a})^{1+\frac{1}{a}}}\Big|\leq 3.

Thus we have

\displaystyle\big|\mathrm{div}\big(b(v_{0})F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))\big)\big|\leq\frac{2C_{*}}{(1-C_{*}^{a})^{\frac{1}{a}}}\Big|\frac{\partial v_{0}}{\partial x_{j}}\Big|+6\Big|\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0})_{kl}}{\partial x_{j}}\Big|.

It follows that
\mathrm{div}(b(v_{0})F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0})))
is an element of
L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
with bound indepndent of
n
, since
v_{0}\in H^{1}(\Omega)
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in W^{2,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
.
Returning to (
3.22
), we deduce that there exists a unique
\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{-2}\in L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
solving (
3.22
).
As a result, the difference quotient
\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\gamma}
is well-defined and is an element of
L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
.
Testing in (
3.22
) against
\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{0}
yields

\displaystyle\|\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{0}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\Big\|\mathrm{div}\Big(b(v_{0})F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))\Big)+\boldsymbol{f}(0)\Big\|_{2}\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{0}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{0}\|_{2}.

Hence
\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{0}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
, independent of
n
and
M
.
Subtracting the equality

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}+b(v^{\gamma}_{0})\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{0}:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{w})\,\mathrm{d}x=\langle l^{\gamma}_{0},\boldsymbol{w}\rangle,

from the elastodynamic equation (
3.7
) at time level
m=1
, and choosing the test function
\boldsymbol{w}=\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{1}+\alpha\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{1}
,
we can manipulate the resulting equation as in the case
m\geq 2
.
We add the corresponding result to (
3.21
) and deduce that

(3.23)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Big(\|\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}-\|\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{0}\|_{2}^{2}\Big)+\alpha(\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m},\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m})-\alpha(\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{0},\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{1})

\displaystyle+\frac{h}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}b(v^{\gamma}_{j-1})\Big(\delta\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j},\delta\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j}\Big)_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j-1})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle-\alpha h\sum_{j=2}^{m}(\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{j},\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{j-1})

\displaystyle\leq\langle\delta l^{\gamma}_{m},\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\rangle-\langle\delta l^{\gamma}_{1},\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\rangle+h\sum_{j=2}^{m}\langle\delta^{2}l^{\gamma}_{j},\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{j-1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{j-1}\rangle

\displaystyle+\frac{h}{2\eta}\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}\Big(\frac{b(v^{\gamma}_{j})-b(v^{\gamma}_{j-1})}{h}\Big)^{2}(\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j},\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j})_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j+1}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x.

We have

(3.24)

\alpha h\sum_{j=2}^{m}(\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{j},\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{j-1})\leq C(\alpha)h\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\|\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{\|\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}}{8},

and

(3.25)

\alpha(\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m},\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m})\leq\frac{\|\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}}{8}+2\alpha^{2}\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}.

Substituting (
3.24
) and (
3.25
) into (
3.23
) and applying the discrete Gronwall inequality, it follows that, for every
1\leq m\leq M
,

(3.26)

\displaystyle\|\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}+h\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}b(v^{\gamma}_{j-1})\Big(\delta\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j},\delta\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j}\Big)_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j-1})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq C(\delta)\Big[\|\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{0}\|_{2}^{2}+\max_{0\leq j\leq m}\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}+\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\|\delta l^{\gamma}_{j}\|_{-1,2}^{2}+\Big(h\sum_{j=2}^{m}\|\delta^{2}l^{\gamma}_{j}\|_{-1,2}\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle+h\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}\Big(\frac{b(v^{\gamma}_{j})-b(v^{\gamma}_{j-1})}{h}\Big)^{2}(\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j},\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j})_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j+1}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x+1\Big]

\displaystyle+\delta\max_{0\leq j\leq m}\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{j}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{j})\|_{2}^{2},

where
C=C(\delta)
is a constant that is independent of
M
and
n
, but does depend on
\delta
, which is fixed sufficiently small so that the final term on the right can be absorbed into the left-hand side.
To prove that
\delta
can be chosen such that this is possible, we first notice that

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m}=h\sum_{j=1}^{m}\delta\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j}+\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{0}=h\sum_{j=1}^{m}\delta\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j}+F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0})).

It follows that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\frac{\|\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}}{n}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(h\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{\|\delta\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}}{n}+\frac{\|F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))\|_{2}^{2}}{n}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(h\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}b(v^{\gamma}_{j-1})\Big(\delta\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j},\delta\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j}\Big)_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j-1})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x+1\Big).

By definition, we have

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m})|\leq 1+\frac{|\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m}|}{n}.

This yields

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{j}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{j})\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(1+h\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}b(v^{\gamma}_{j-1})(\delta\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j},\delta\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j})_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{j-1})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x\Big).

Thus we see that
\delta
can be chosen sufficiently small so that the final term on the right-hand side of (
3.26
) can be absorbed into the left. Optimising over the indices
1\leq m\leq M
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq m\leq M}\|\delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}+h\sum_{m=1}^{M}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}b(v^{\gamma}_{m})\big(\delta\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m},\delta\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m}\big)_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m-1})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq C\Big[1+\max_{1\leq m\leq M}\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{2}^{2}+\max_{1\leq m\leq M}\|\delta l^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{-1,2}^{2}+\Big(h\sum_{m=2}^{M}\|\delta^{2}l^{\gamma}_{m}\|_{-1,2}\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+h\sum_{m=1}^{M-1}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}\Big(\frac{b(v^{\gamma}_{m})-b(v^{\gamma}_{m-1})}{h}\Big)^{2}(\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m},\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m})_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m+1}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{\gamma}_{m})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x\Big].

Rewriting this in terms of interpolants of the discrete solution sequence, we obtain

(3.27)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\gamma,\prime}_{t}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\gamma}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma,+})\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+\int_{Q}\int_{0}^{1}b(v^{\gamma,-})\big(\overline{\mathbf{T}}^{\gamma}_{t},\overline{\mathbf{T}}^{\gamma}_{t}\big)_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\mathbf{T}^{\gamma,+}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{\gamma,-})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\Big[1+\max_{t\in[0,T]}\|\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\gamma}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\max_{t\in[0,T]}\|\overline{l}^{\gamma}_{t}(t)\|_{-1,2}^{2}+\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\|l^{\gamma,+,\prime\prime}(t)\|_{-1,2}\,\mathrm{d}t\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle+h\int_{Q}\int_{0}^{1}\Big(\frac{b(v^{\gamma,+})-b(v^{\gamma,-})}{h}\Big)^{2}\cdot(\mathbf{T}^{\gamma,+},\mathbf{T}^{\gamma,+})_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\tau^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{\gamma,+}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{n\gamma,+})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\Big],

where
C
is independent of
M
and
n
and
\tau^{h}_{t}
denotes the translation operator in the time variable of length
h
.

Now we show that
\mathbf{T}^{n}\in W^{1,2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
.
To bound the right-hand side of (
3.27
) independent of
M
, we notice that

\displaystyle\frac{b(v^{\gamma,+})-b(v^{\gamma,-})}{h}\leq\|\overline{v}^{\gamma}_{t}\|_{\infty}\|b^{\prime}(\overline{v}^{\gamma})\|_{\infty}\leq C(n).

Furthermore, for every
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{|\mathbf{T}|^{2}}{n}\leq(\mathbf{T},\mathbf{T})_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(\mathbf{S})}\leq 3|\mathbf{T}|^{2}.

Using these two statements with the previously determined
M
-independent bounds and the fact that
(l^{n})_{n}
is bounded in
W^{2,1}(0,T;W^{-1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d})
, it follows from (
3.27
) that

(3.28)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big(\|\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\gamma,\prime}_{t}(t)\|_{2}^{2}\big)+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big(\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\gamma}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\gamma,+})\|_{2}^{2}\big)+\int_{Q}\frac{|\overline{\mathbf{T}}^{\gamma}_{t}|^{2}}{n}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\Big\{1+\|l^{\gamma}_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}(W^{-1,2})}+\|l^{\gamma}_{tt}\|_{L^{1}(W^{-1,2})}^{2}+\int_{Q}|\mathbf{T}^{\gamma,+}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq C,

where
C=C(n)
is independent of
M
. Hence
(\overline{\mathbf{T}}^{(n,M)})_{M}
is bounded in
W^{1,2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
independent of
M
and so converges weakly in this space to
\mathbf{T}^{n}=F_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}))
.
However, since
(\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{(n,M)}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{(n,M),+})_{M}
converges strongly in
L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d})
by (
3.16
), it follows from the constitutive relation that
(\mathbf{T}^{(n,M),+})_{M}
converges strongly in
L^{2}(Q)^{d\times d}
. Using Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem, we get

(3.29)

\displaystyle\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\int_{Q}\int_{0}^{1}(\mathbf{T}^{(n,M),+},\mathbf{T}^{(n,M),+})_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\tau^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{(n,M),+}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{(n,M),+})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{Q}(\mathbf{T}^{n},\mathbf{T}^{n})_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t.

Making further use of the previously determined strong convergence results for the interpolants, we see that

(3.30)

\displaystyle\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\Big[\max_{t\in[0,T]}\|\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{(n,M)}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\max_{t\in[0,T]}\|\overline{l}^{(n,M)}_{t}(t)\|_{-1,2}^{2}+\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\|l^{(n,M),+,\prime\prime}(t)\|_{-1,2}\,\mathrm{d}t\Big)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|l^{n}_{t}(t)\|_{-1,2}^{2}+\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\|l^{n}_{tt}(t)\|_{-1,2}\,\mathrm{d}t\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C

where
C
is a constant that is independent of
n
.
Ignoring the final term on the left-hand side of (
3.27
), and using (
3.29
) and (
3.30
), it follows that

\displaystyle\limsup_{M\rightarrow\infty}\Big[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{(n,M),\prime}_{t}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\overline{\boldsymbol{u}}^{(n,M)}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{(n,M),+})(t)\|_{2}^{2}\Big]\leq C,

where
C
is independent of
n
. By weak lower semi-continuity, we have

(3.31)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(t)\|_{2}+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})(t)\|_{2}\leq C.

Next we use (
3.31
) with the extra regularity of
\mathbf{T}^{n}
in order to obtain the bound on
\mathbf{T}^{n}_{t}
in the statement of the proposition.
Using that
\mathbf{T}^{n}\in W^{1,2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
, it follows that we have
F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})\in W^{1,2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
, with weak derivative given by the chain rule, that is,
\mathcal{A}_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})\mathbf{T}^{n}_{t}
. By standard properties of differentiable Bochner functions, the following convergence results hold for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
with respect to strong convergence in
L^{2}(\Omega)^{d\times d}
:

(3.32)

\lim_{h\rightarrow 0+}\frac{\mathbf{T}^{n}(t+h)-\mathbf{T}^{n}(t)}{h}=\mathbf{T}^{n}_{t},\quad\lim_{h\rightarrow 0+}\frac{F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n}(t+h))-F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n}(t))}{h}=\mathcal{A}_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})\mathbf{T}^{n}_{t}.

Let
\Delta^{h}_{t}
denote the undivided difference quotient of length
h
in the time variable and let
t\in(0,T)
. Considering the elastodynamic equation at times
t
and
t+h
, for a
h>0
sufficiently small such that
t+h<T
, choosing
\Delta^{h}_{t}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})(t)
as the test function we see that

(3.33)

\displaystyle\langle\Delta^{h}_{t}l^{n}(t),\Delta^{h}_{t}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\Delta^{h}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(t)\cdot\Delta^{h}_{t}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})(t)+\Delta^{h}_{t}\Big(b(v^{n})\mathbf{T}^{n}\Big)(t):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\big(\Delta^{h}_{t}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\big)(t)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big(\frac{\|\Delta^{h}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}}{2}+\alpha\int_{\Omega}\Delta^{h}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}\cdot\Delta^{h}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)-\alpha\int_{\Omega}|\Delta^{h}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{t}^{n}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle+\int_{\Omega}\Delta^{h}_{t}b(v^{n})\tau^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}:\Delta^{h}_{t}F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})+b(v^{n})\Delta^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}:\Delta^{h}_{t}F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})\,\mathrm{d}x.

From Lemmas
3.6
and
3.7
, we have

\displaystyle|\Delta^{h}_{t}b(v^{n})|=\Big|\int_{0}^{h}b^{\prime}(v^{n}(t+s))v^{n}_{t}(t+s)\,\mathrm{d}s\Big|\leq h\|b^{\prime}(v^{n})\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}\|v^{n}_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}\leq Ch,

where
C
is independent of
n
. Using this and recalling that
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(\mathbf{U})}
is an inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
for every
\mathbf{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, it follows that

(3.34)

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\Omega}\Delta^{h}_{t}b(v^{n})\tau^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}:\Delta^{h}_{t}F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})\,\mathrm{d}x\Big|

\displaystyle\leq Ch\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}\big|\big(\tau^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n},\Delta^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}\big)_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\tau^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{n})}\big|\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\frac{h^{2}}{2\eta}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}(\tau^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n},\tau^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n})_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\tau^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{n})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle+\frac{\eta}{2}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}\big(\Delta^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n},\Delta^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}\big)_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\tau^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{n})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x.

Now we notice that there exists a constant
C
, independent of
n
, such that

\displaystyle|F_{n}(\mathbf{T})-F_{n}(\mathbf{S})|^{2}\leq C(\mathbf{T}-\mathbf{S}):(F_{n}(\mathbf{T})-F_{n}(\mathbf{S})).

Thus we have
|\Delta^{h}_{t}F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})|^{2}\leq C\Delta^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}:\Delta^{h}_{t}F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})
. As a result, we can bound the term involving the external force in the following way:

(3.35)

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta^{h}_{t}l^{n},\Delta^{h}_{t}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq C\|\Delta^{h}_{t}l^{n}\|_{-1,2}\|\Delta^{h}_{t}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\Delta^{h}_{t}l^{n}\|_{-1,2}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{4}\int_{\Omega}\Delta^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}:\Delta^{h}_{t}F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})\,\mathrm{d}x,

where
C
is independent of
n
.
Substituting (
3.34
) and (
3.35
) into (
3.33
) and integrating over
(t_{1},t_{2})\subset[0,T]
, we see that

\displaystyle{\|\Delta^{h}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}(t_{2})\|_{2}^{2}}+\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}{b(v^{n})}\Delta^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}:\Delta^{h}_{t}F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\Big[\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\|\Delta^{h}_{t}l^{n}\|_{-1,2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+h^{2}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}(\tau^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n},\tau^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n})_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\tau^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{n})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\|\Delta^{h}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}(t)\|_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\|\Delta^{h}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}(t_{1})\|_{2}^{2}+\|\Delta^{h}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(t_{1})\|_{2}^{2}\Big].

Dividing through by
h^{2}
and using standard properties of difference quotients, we get

(3.36)

\displaystyle\frac{\|\Delta^{h}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}(t_{2})\|_{2}^{2}}{h^{2}}+\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}b(v^{n})\frac{\Delta^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}}{h}:\frac{\Delta^{h}_{t}F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})}{h}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\|l^{n}_{t}\|_{-1,2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}\big(\tau^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n},\tau^{n}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}\big)_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\tau^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{n})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}(t)\|_{2}^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq C\Big[1+\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}\big(\tau^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n},\tau^{n}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}\big)_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\tau^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{n})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\Big],

where
C
is a constant, independent of
n
and
h
. Next, we take the limit as
h\rightarrow 0+
. The strong convergence result
\tau^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbf{T}^{n}
in
L^{2}((t_{1},t_{2})\times\Omega)^{d\times d}
and the dominated convergence theorem yield

(3.37)

\displaystyle\lim_{h\rightarrow 0+}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}\big(\tau^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n},\tau^{n}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}\big)_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(s\tau^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}+(1-s)\mathbf{T}^{n})}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}\big(\mathbf{T}^{n},\mathbf{T}^{n})_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}|\mathbf{T}^{n}|+\frac{|\mathbf{T}^{n}|^{2}}{n}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C.

Using (
3.32
), Fatou’s lemma and the monotonicity of
F_{n}
, we have

(3.38)

\displaystyle\liminf_{h\rightarrow 0+}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}b(v^{n})\frac{\Delta^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}}{h}:\frac{\Delta^{h}_{t}F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})}{h}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}b(v)\mathbf{T}^{n}_{t}:F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})_{t}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}\eta\big(\mathbf{T}^{n}_{t},\mathbf{T}^{n}_{t})_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\eta\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\mathbf{T}^{n}_{t}|^{2}}{(1+|\mathbf{T}^{n}|^{a})^{1+\frac{1}{a}}}+\frac{|\mathbf{T}^{n}_{t}|^{2}}{n}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t.

Substituting (
3.37
) and (
3.38
) into (
3.36
) gives the required result.
∎

Noticing that we have
|\Delta^{h}_{t}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})|\leq C\Delta^{h}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}:\Delta^{h}_{t}F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})
, as well as the fact that
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t})\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
, we immediately obtain the bound in Corollary
3.5
. Then in Lemma
3.8
, we use the new bounds to improve the integrability of
(\mathbf{T}^{n})_{n}
to
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
.

Corollary 3.5
.

There exists a constant
C
independent of
n
such that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(t)\|_{1,2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let the assumptions of Proposition
3.2
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\mathbf{T}^{n},v^{n})
be the solution triple constructed there. Suppose additionally that

l\in W^{2,1}(0,T;W^{-1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d})
,
the safety strain condition (
3.5
) holds, the compatibility condition (
3.4
) holds, and that
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in W^{2,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
. There exists a constant
C
independent of
n
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{\Omega}|\mathbf{T}^{n}(t)|\,\mathrm{d}x\leq C.

Proof.

Let
t\in(0,T)
and pick
h>0
sufficiently small such that
t+h<T
. We test in (
3.12
) at times
t
and
t+h
against
(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})(t)
and subtract the resulting equations. Dividing through by
h
, integrating over
(t_{1},t_{2})\subset[0,T]
and letting
h\rightarrow 0+
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\langle l^{n}_{t},\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(t_{2})\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})(t_{2})-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(t_{1})\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})(t_{1})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}b^{\prime}(v^{n})v^{n}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}:F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})+b(v^{n})\mathbf{T}^{n}_{t}:F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t.

From the chain rule and product rule for weak derivatives, we have
\varphi_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})\in W^{1,1}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))
with weak derivative given by
\mathbf{T}^{n}_{t}:F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})
. Furthermore, since
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}\in L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega)^{d})
, we must have
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\in C([0,T];W^{1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d})
and so
F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
. By continuity of
F^{-1}_{n}
, it follows that
\mathbf{T}^{n}\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
with
\mathbf{T}^{n}(0)=F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))
. Putting together these facts, we get

(3.39)

\displaystyle\lim_{t_{1}\rightarrow 0+}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}b(v^{n})\mathbf{T}^{n}_{t}:F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\lim_{t_{1}\rightarrow 0+}\Big\{\Big[\int_{\Omega}b(v^{n}(t))\varphi_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n}(t))\,\mathrm{d}x\Big]_{t=t_{1}}^{t=t_{2}}-\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}b^{\prime}(v^{n})v^{n}_{t}\varphi_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\Big\}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\big(b(v^{n})\varphi_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})\big)(t_{2})-b(v_{0})\varphi_{n}(F_{n}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0})))\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{0}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}b^{\prime}(v^{n})v^{n}_{t}\varphi_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t.

Furthermore, we have

(3.40)

\displaystyle\Big[\int_{\Omega}b(v^{n}(t))\varphi_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n}(t))\,\mathrm{d}x\Big]_{t=t_{1}}^{t=t_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\Big[\int_{\Omega}b(v^{n}(t))\varphi_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n}(t))\,\mathrm{d}x\Big]_{t=t_{1}}^{t=t_{2}}-\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}b^{\prime}(v^{n})v^{n}_{t}\varphi_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\langle l^{n}_{t},\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(t_{2})\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})(t_{2})-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(t_{1})\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})(t_{1})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle-\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}b^{\prime}(v^{n})v^{n}_{t}\mathbf{T}^{n}:F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\|l^{n}_{t}\|_{-1,2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})(t)\|_{1,2}^{2}+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}(t)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+\|v^{n}_{t}\|_{\infty}\int_{Q}\Big[\frac{|\mathbf{T}^{n}|^{2}}{n}+|\mathbf{T}^{n}|\Big]\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C,

where
C
is a constant that is independent of
n
,
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.
Letting
t_{1}\rightarrow 0+
in (
3.40
) and using (
3.39
) again, it follows that

(3.41)

\int_{\Omega}\varphi_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n}(t_{2}))\,\mathrm{d}x\leq C\Big(1+\int_{\Omega}b(v_{0})\varphi_{n}(F^{-1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0})))\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)\leq C.

The second inequality follows from the safety strain condition (
3.5
). Using the definition of
\varphi_{n}
, there exists positive constants
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
, independent of
n
, such that
c_{1}|\mathbf{T}|-c_{2}\leq\varphi_{n}(\mathbf{T})
, for every
\mathbf{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. Substituting this into (
3.41
), the asserted bound follows.
∎

Next, we improve the spatial regularity of the stress tensor. We use arguments mimicking those in
[
3
]
and
[
8
]
. However, now we have the extra issue of the phase-field function being present. It is here that we require
\nabla v^{n}
to be uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}(Q)
, independent of
n
. Thus we assume that
k>\frac{d}{2}+1
to ensure that we have
H^{k}(\Omega)\subset C^{1}(\overline{\Omega})
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let the assumptions of Proposition
3.2
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\mathbf{T}^{n},v^{n})
be the solution triple constructed there. Suppose also that

l\in W^{2,1}(0,T;W^{-1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d})
,
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}_{loc}(\Omega)^{d})
,
the safety strain condition (
3.5
) holds, the compatibility condition (
3.4
) holds and
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in W^{2,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
with
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in W^{2,2}_{loc}(\Omega)^{d}
. Furthermore, assume
k>\frac{d}{2}+1
. For every compact subset
\Omega_{0}\subset\Omega
, there exists a constant
C=C(\Omega_{0})
, independent of
n
, such that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{0})}+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{0}}\frac{|\nabla\mathbf{T}^{n}|^{2}}{(1+|\mathbf{T}^{n}|)^{1+a}}+\frac{|\nabla\mathbf{T}^{n}|^{2}}{n}+|\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}(t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C.

Proof.

Let
\zeta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
be a cut-off function between
\Omega
and
\Omega_{0}
. We denote by
\Omega_{1}
, a compact subset of
\Omega
that contains
\mathrm{supp}(\zeta)
. Suppose that
h>0
is sufficiently small so that
\zeta\Delta^{h}_{i}\boldsymbol{w}
is well-defined on
\Omega
for an arbitrary
\boldsymbol{w}\in L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
, where
\Delta^{h}_{i}
is the undivided difference quotient of length
h
in the
i
-th spatial co-ordinate direction. For a.e.
t\in(0,T)
, noticing that the Neumann boundary term vanishes due to the compact support of
\zeta
, using Proposition
3.3
we get

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\zeta^{2}\Delta^{h}_{i}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}\cdot\Delta^{h}_{i}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})+\Delta^{h}_{i}\big(b(v^{n})\mathbf{T}^{n}\big):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\big(\zeta^{2}\Delta^{h}_{i}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\big)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\zeta^{2}\Delta^{h}_{i}\boldsymbol{f}(t)\cdot\Delta^{h}_{i}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq C(\zeta)h^{2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{1})}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{2}g(t),

where
g\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}_{+})
with bound in this space independent of
n
but depending on
\Omega_{1}
.
Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\zeta^{2}\Delta^{h}_{i}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}\cdot\Delta^{h}_{i}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq C(\zeta)h^{2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{1})}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq C(\zeta)h^{2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq C(\zeta)h^{2}g(t),

making
g
larger if necessary.
The right-hand side is in
L^{2}(0,T)
by Corollary
3.5
, with bound independent of
n
. For the nonlinear terms, we expand as follows:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\Delta^{h}_{i}\big(b(v^{n})\mathbf{T}^{n}\big):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\big(\zeta^{2}\Delta^{h}_{i}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\big)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\Big(\Delta^{h}_{i}b(v^{n})\tau^{h}_{i}\mathbf{T}^{n}+b(v^{n})\Delta^{h}_{i}\mathbf{T}^{n}\Big):\Big(2\zeta\nabla\zeta\otimes\Delta^{h}_{i}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})+\zeta^{2}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\Delta^{h}_{i}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}))\Big)\,\mathrm{d}x,

where
\tau^{h}_{i}
is a translation of length
h
in the
i
-th spatial co-ordinate direction.
Integration over
(0,T)
yields

\displaystyle\int_{Q}\eta\zeta^{2}\frac{|\Delta^{h}_{i}\mathbf{T}^{n}|^{2}}{n}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{Q}\zeta^{2}b(v^{n})\Delta^{h}_{i}\mathbf{T}^{n}:\Delta^{h}_{i}F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{2}\Big[1+\int_{0}^{T}g(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\Big]+\int_{Q}\Big|2\zeta\Delta^{h}_{i}b(v^{n})\tau^{h}_{i}\mathbf{T}^{n}:\big(\nabla\zeta\otimes\Delta^{h}_{i}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\big)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{Q}\Big|\zeta^{2}\Delta^{h}_{i}b(v^{n})\tau^{h}_{i}\mathbf{T}^{n}:\Delta^{h}_{i}F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})\Big|\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{Q}\Big|2\zeta b(v^{n})\Delta^{h}_{i}\mathbf{T}^{n}:\big(\nabla\zeta\otimes\Delta^{h}_{i}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\big)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t.

Now we use this to show that
\mathbf{T}^{n}\in L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}_{loc}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
with bound that does depend on
n
. Using Young’s inequality and the fact that, for every
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
,
|F_{n}(\mathbf{T})-F_{n}(\mathbf{S})|\leq 3|\mathbf{T}-\mathbf{S}|
, we see that

\displaystyle\frac{\eta}{2}\int_{Q}\zeta^{2}\frac{|\Delta^{h}_{i}\mathbf{T}^{n}|^{2}}{n}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C(n,\zeta)\Big[h^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\Big\{\||\nabla\zeta|\Delta^{h}_{i}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\|_{2}^{2}+h^{2}\|\nabla v^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{1})}^{2}\|\zeta\tau^{h}_{i}\mathbf{T}^{n}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+h\|\nabla v^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{1})}\|\zeta\tau^{h}_{i}\mathbf{T}^{n}\|_{2}\||\nabla\zeta|\Delta^{h}_{i}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\|_{2}\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}t\Big]

\displaystyle\leq C(n,\zeta)h^{2},

using the
L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{1,\infty}(\Omega))
bound on
v^{n}
, which is a consequence of the Sobolev embedding
H^{k}(\Omega)\subset W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)
. We divide through by
h^{2}
and let
h\rightarrow 0+
in order to deduce that
\mathbf{T}^{n}
is an element of
L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}_{loc}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
.
As a result, we have
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\in L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}_{loc}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
, from which we deduce that
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\in L^{2}(0,T;W^{2,2}_{loc}(\Omega)^{d})
, using the Korn–Poincaré inequality.

From the elastodynamic equation (
3.12
) and this extra regularity, it follows that

(3.42)

\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}=\mathrm{div}\big(b(v^{n})\mathbf{T}^{n}\big)+\boldsymbol{f}\quad\text{ pointwise a.e. in }Q,

with each term an element of
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}_{loc}(\Omega)^{d})
. We take the scalar product of (
3.42
) with the function
\zeta^{2}\nabla\cdot\nabla(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})
and integrate over
\Omega
to obtain

(3.43)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\zeta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}\cdot\big(\nabla\cdot\nabla(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\big)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\zeta^{2}\mathrm{div}\big(b(v^{n})\mathbf{T}^{n}\big)\cdot\big(\nabla\cdot\nabla(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\big)+\zeta^{2}\boldsymbol{f}\cdot\big(\nabla\cdot\nabla(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\big)\,\mathrm{d}x.

First we focus on the term containing the nonlinearity
\mathbf{T}^{n}
. We have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\zeta^{2}\mathrm{div}\big(b(v^{n})\mathbf{T}^{n}\big)\cdot\big(\nabla\cdot\nabla(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\big)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\zeta^{2}b(v^{n})\frac{\partial T^{n}_{ij}}{\partial x_{j}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{k}^{2}}\big(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\big)_{i}+\zeta^{2}b^{\prime}(v^{n})T^{n}_{ij}\frac{\partial v^{n}}{\partial x_{j}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{k}^{2}}\big(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\big)_{i}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Using an integration by parts argument, we write

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\zeta^{2}b(v^{n})\frac{\partial T^{n}_{ij}}{\partial x_{j}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{k}^{2}}\big(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\big)_{i}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\Big\{\zeta^{2}b(v^{n})\frac{\partial T^{n}_{ij}}{\partial x_{k}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})_{ij}-T^{n}_{ij}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{k}^{2}}\big(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\big)_{i}\Big(b^{\prime}(v^{n})\frac{\partial v^{n}}{\partial x_{j}}\zeta^{2}+2\zeta\frac{\partial\zeta}{\partial x_{j}}b(v^{n})\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+T^{n}_{ij}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})_{ij}\Big(b^{\prime}(v^{n})\frac{\partial v^{n}}{\partial x_{k}}\zeta^{2}+2\zeta\frac{\partial\zeta}{\partial x_{k}}b(v^{n})\Big)\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}x.

This yields

(3.44)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\zeta^{2}\mathrm{div}\big(b(v^{n})\mathbf{T}^{n}\big)\cdot\big(\nabla\cdot(\nabla(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}))\big)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\zeta^{2}b(v^{n})\nabla\mathbf{T}^{n}\vdots\nabla F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})+T^{n}_{ij}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{k}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})_{i}\Big(\zeta^{2}b^{\prime}(v^{n})\frac{\partial v^{n}}{\partial x_{j}}\Big)

\displaystyle-T^{n}_{ij}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{k}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})_{i}\Big(b^{\prime}(v^{n})\frac{\partial v^{n}}{\partial x_{j}}\zeta^{2}+2\zeta\frac{\partial\zeta}{\partial x_{j}}b(v^{n})\Big)

\displaystyle+T^{n}_{ij}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})_{ij}\Big(b^{\prime}(v^{n})\frac{\partial v^{n}}{\partial x_{k}}\zeta^{2}+2\zeta\frac{\partial\zeta}{\partial x_{k}}b(v^{n})\Big)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\zeta^{2}b(v^{n})\nabla\mathbf{T}^{n}\vdots\nabla F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})+\zeta\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})_{ij}B^{n,k}_{ij}\,\mathrm{d}x,

where
\mathbf{B}^{n,k}=(B^{n,k}_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{d}
is defined, for
1\leq k\leq d
, by

\displaystyle B^{n,k}_{ij}

\displaystyle=T^{n}_{ij}\big(2b(v^{n})\partial_{k}\zeta+\zeta b^{\prime}(v^{n})\partial_{k}v^{n}\Big)-2\delta_{jk}T^{n}_{im}\Big(2b(v^{n})\partial_{m}\zeta+\tau b^{\prime}(v^{n})\partial_{m}v^{n}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\delta_{ij}T^{n}_{km}\Big(2b(v^{n})\partial_{m}\zeta+\tau b^{\prime}(v^{n})\partial_{m}v^{n}\Big)+2\delta_{jk}T^{n}_{im}\zeta b^{\prime}(v^{n})\partial_{m}v^{n}-\delta_{ij}T^{n}_{km}\zeta b^{\prime}(v^{n})\partial_{m}v^{n},

making use of the identity

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{k}^{2}}\boldsymbol{w}_{i}=2\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{w})_{ik}-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{w})_{kk}.

Using the bound on
(v^{n})_{n}
in
L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{1,\infty}(\Omega))
and the definition of
\mathbf{B}^{n,k}
, there exists a constant
C
, independent of
n
, such that
|\mathbf{B}^{n,k}|\leq C|\mathbf{T}^{n}|
, for every
1\leq k\leq d
.
Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\Omega}\zeta\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}):\mathbf{B}^{n,k}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\int_{\Omega}\zeta\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n}):\mathbf{B}^{n,k}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\int_{\Omega}\zeta\big(\partial_{k}\mathbf{T}^{n},\mathbf{B}^{n,k}\big)_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega}\frac{\zeta^{2}\eta}{2}\big(\partial_{k}\mathbf{T}^{n},\partial_{k}\mathbf{T}^{n}\big)_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})}+C(\eta)\mathbb{I}_{\mathrm{supp}(\zeta)}\big(\mathbf{B}^{n,k},\mathbf{B}^{n,k}\big)_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega}\frac{\zeta^{2}\eta}{2}\big(\partial_{k}\mathbf{T}^{n},\partial_{k}\mathbf{T}^{n}\big)_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})}+C\Big(\frac{|\mathbf{B}^{n,k}|^{2}}{n}+\frac{|\mathbf{B}^{n,k}|^{2}}{1+|\mathbf{T}^{n}|}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega}\frac{\zeta^{2}\eta}{2}\nabla\mathbf{T}^{n}\vdots\nabla F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})+C\Big(\frac{|\mathbf{T}^{n}|^{2}}{n}+|\mathbf{T}^{n}|\Big)\,\mathrm{d}x.

Using this in (
3.44
) and substituting the result into (
3.43
), we obtain

(3.45)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\zeta^{2}\Big(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}-\boldsymbol{f}\Big)\cdot\Big(\nabla\cdot\big(\nabla(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\big)\Big)\,\mathrm{d}x+C\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\mathbf{T}^{n}|^{2}}{n}+|\mathbf{T}^{n}|\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\Omega}\frac{\zeta^{2}b(v^{n})}{2}\nabla\mathbf{T}^{n}\vdots\nabla F_{n}(\mathbf{T}^{n})\,\mathrm{d}x.

For the first term on the right-hand side, we integrate by parts to deduce that

(3.46)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\zeta^{2}\Big(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}-\boldsymbol{f}\Big)\cdot\Big(\nabla\cdot\big(\nabla(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\big)\Big)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\zeta^{2}\nabla\big(\boldsymbol{f}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}\big):\nabla\big(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\big)+2\zeta\big[\nabla\zeta\otimes\big(\boldsymbol{f}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}\big)\big]:\nabla\big(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\big)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big(\frac{\|\zeta\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}}{2}+\alpha\int_{\Omega}\zeta^{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)+\alpha\int_{\Omega}\zeta^{2}|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle+\|\zeta\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\|_{2}\|\zeta\nabla(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\|_{2}+C(\zeta)\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{2}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\|_{2}

\displaystyle+C(\zeta)\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{tt}\|_{2}\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\|_{2}.

Integrating in time, using the continuity of
\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n}
and
\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}
as maps from
[0,T]
to
L^{2}(\Omega)^{d\times d}
, with the assumptions on the initial data, and applying Gronwall’s inequality, it follows from (
3.45
) and (
3.46
) that

(3.47)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\zeta\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\int_{Q}\zeta^{2}\Big(\frac{|\nabla\mathbf{T}^{n}|^{2}}{(1+|\mathbf{T}^{n}|)^{1+a}}+\frac{|\nabla\mathbf{T}^{n}|^{2}}{n}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C(\zeta).

Recalling that for every
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
we have

\displaystyle|F_{n}(\mathbf{T})-F_{n}(\mathbf{S})|^{2}\leq C(\mathbf{T}-\mathbf{S}):(F_{n}(\mathbf{T})-F_{n}(\mathbf{S})),

where
C
is a constant independent of
n
, it follows from (
3.47
) that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{0}}|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\partial_{i}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}))|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{0}}|\nabla(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}))|^{2}\leq C(\Omega_{0}),

for every
1\leq i\leq d
. Applying Korn’s inequality for functions in the space
W^{1,2}(\Omega_{0})^{2}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\|\partial_{i}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\|_{W^{1,2}(\Omega_{0})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\|\partial_{i}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{0})}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\partial_{i}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}))\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\nabla(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}))\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{0})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C(\Omega_{0}).

Hence
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\in L^{2}(0,T;W^{2,2}_{loc}(\Omega)^{d})
with bound independent of
n
. It remains to prove that
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{2,2}_{loc}(\Omega)^{d})
. From the memory kernel property, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{\alpha(s-t)}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\,\mathrm{d}s,

with equality meant in
L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
. Each term on the right-hand side is an element of
W^{2,2}_{loc}(\Omega)^{d}
. Hence, for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
, we must have
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(t)\in W^{2,2}_{loc}(\Omega)^{d}
. Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{\alpha(s-t)}\nabla^{2}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\,\mathrm{d}s,

with equality in
L^{2}_{loc}(\Omega)^{d\times d}
.
Applying the
L^{2}(\Omega_{0})
norm to this and using the previously determined result for
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}
, we conclude the stated result.
∎

Now we have sufficient
n
-independent estimates to take the limit as
n\rightarrow\infty
. In Theorem
3.2
, we focus on proving a pointwise convergence result for
(\mathbf{T}^{n})_{n}
and show that the limiting triple satisfies the weak elastodynamic equation, up to the Neumann boundary condition. We also prove the satisfaction of the initial conditions. We delay the proof of the minimisation problem and the energy-dissipation equality until Propositions
3.5
and
3.6
, respectively, for simplicity of the presentation.

Theorem 3.2
.

Assume that
k>\frac{d}{2}+1
with data
v_{0}\in H^{k}_{D+1}(\Omega)
, and
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in W^{1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
such that
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in W^{2,2}_{loc}(\Omega)^{d}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in W^{2,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d}
and the safety strain condition (
3.5
) holds. Furthermore, assume that
l\in W^{2,1}(0,T;W^{-1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d})
such that
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}_{loc}(\Omega)^{d})
and the compatibility condition (
3.4
) holds. Let
((\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\mathbf{T}^{n},v^{n}))_{n}
be the sequence of solutions to the regularised problem constructed in Proposition
3.2
. There exists a subsequence in
n
, not relabelled, and a limiting triple
(\boldsymbol{u},\mathbf{T},v)
such that

•

\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}\boldsymbol{u}
weakly-* in
W^{2,\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)^{d})\cap W^{1,\infty}(0,T;W^{1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d})
,

•

\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{u}
weakly in
W^{2,2}(0,T;W^{1,2}_{D}(\Omega)^{d})\cap W^{1,2}(0,T;W^{2,2}_{loc}(\Omega)^{d})
,

•

\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u})
pointwise a.e. in
Q
,

•

v^{n}\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}v
weakly-* in
W^{1,\infty}(0,T;H^{k}(\Omega))
with
v(t)\in H^{k}_{D+1}(\Omega)
for every
t\in[0,T]
,

•

\mathbf{T}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbf{T}
pointwise a.e. in
Q
where
\mathbf{T}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
.

Furthermore, there exists an error term
\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}}\in L^{\infty}_{w^{*}}(0,T;(C^{1}_{0}(\Gamma_{N})^{d})^{*})
such that

(3.48)

\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{w}+b(v(t))\mathbf{T}(t):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{w})\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{f}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}x+\langle\boldsymbol{g}(t)-\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}}(t),\boldsymbol{w}\rangle_{C_{0}^{1}(\Gamma_{N})},

for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
and every
\boldsymbol{w}\in C^{1}_{D}(\overline{\Omega})^{d}
, where the stress tensor is identified by the relation
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u})=F(\mathbf{T})
, pointwise a.e. in
Q
. Furthermore, the initial conditions hold in the sense that

(3.49)

\lim_{t\rightarrow 0+}\Big[\|\boldsymbol{u}(t)-\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{1,2}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{t}(t)-\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\|_{1,2}+\|v(t)-v_{0}\|_{k,2}\Big]=0.

Proof.

The first, second and fourth convergence results follow immediately from the
a priori
bounds in Lemma
3.5
, Lemma
3.6
, Lemma
3.7
, Proposition
3.3
and Corollary
3.5
. Using Propositions
3.3
and
3.4
, applying the reasoning in
[
9
]
yields the pointwise convergence result
\mathbf{T}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbf{T}
a.e. in
Q
. In particular, we consider sequences

\mathbf{S}^{n}=\frac{\mathbf{T}^{n}}{(1+|\mathbf{T}^{n}|)^{1+a}},\quad s^{n}=\frac{1}{(1+|\mathbf{T}^{n}|)^{1+a}}.

By a careful application of the chain rule for weak derivatives, it follows that
(\mathbf{S}^{n})_{n}
is bounded in
W^{1,2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
and
L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}_{loc}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
, independent of
n
. An analogous result holds for
(s^{n})_{n}
. By the Aubin–Lions lemma, it follows that
\mathbf{S}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbf{S}
and
s^{n}\rightarrow s
strongly in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}_{loc}(\Omega))
and hence pointwise a.e. in
Q
, up to a subsequence that we do not relabel. By Fatou’s lemma and the bound on
(\mathbf{T}^{n})_{n}
in
L^{1}(Q)^{d\times d}
, we deduce that
s^{-\frac{1}{1+a}}\in L^{1}(Q)
. Hence
s>0
a.e. in
Q
and so
\mathbf{T}^{n}=(s^{n})^{-1}\mathbf{S}^{n}
converges pointwise a.e. in
Q
to a limit which we denote by
\mathbf{T}
.
By Fatou’s lemma and Lemma
3.8
,
\mathbf{T}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
. The fifth bullet point now follows.

By Lemma
3.5
, we have that
n^{-1}\mathbf{T}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbf{0}
strongly in
L^{2}(Q)^{d\times d}
. With the constitutive relation for the regularised problem and the pointwise convergence of
(\mathbf{T}^{n})_{n}
, this implies the pointwise convergence result for
(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}))_{n}
, noting that weak limits in
L^{2}(Q)
and pointwise limits coincide. Hence all of the stated convergence results hold.
Furthermore, the strain-limiting constitutive relation between
\mathbf{T}
and
\boldsymbol{u}
must hold by uniqueness of pointwise limits.

The attainment of the initial conditions (
3.49
) follows from standard arguments, using the aforementioned regularity, application of the Aubin–Lions lemma and the initial conditions satisfied by the sequence of approximate solutions.

Adapting the reasoning in
[
8
]
, noticing that the Neumann boundary term vanishes when considering compactly supported test functions, and using the fact that
(b(v^{n}))_{n}
is uniformly bounded on
Q
and converges strongly in
L^{p}(Q)
to
b(v)
for every
p\in[1,\infty)
, we see that, for every
\boldsymbol{w}\in W^{1,2}_{0}(\Omega)^{d}
and a.e.
t\in(0,T)
,

(3.50)

\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{w}+b(v(t))\mathbf{T}(t):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{w})\,\mathrm{d}x=\langle l(t),\boldsymbol{w}\rangle=\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{f}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Next, we want to be able to test against functions that only vanish on the Dirichlet part of the boundary, and so obtain some information regarding the satisfaction of the Neumann boundary condition.
First we notice that
(\mathbf{T}^{n})_{n}
is bounded in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega)^{d\times d})
and hence in
L^{\infty}_{w^{*}}(0,T;\mathcal{M}(\overline{\Omega})^{d\times d})
, the dual space of
L^{1}(0,T;C(\overline{\Omega})^{d\times d})
. Up to a further subsequence that we do not relabel, it follows from the Banach–Alaoglu theorem that we have
\mathbf{T}^{n}\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}\mathbf{T}
weakly-* in
L^{\infty}_{w^{*}}(0,T;\mathcal{M}(\overline{\Omega})^{d\times d})
. Furthermore, this limit satisfies

(3.51)

\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}x+\langle\overline{\mathbf{T}}(t),\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{w})\rangle_{(\mathcal{M}(\overline{\Omega}),C(\overline{\Omega}))}=\langle l(t),\boldsymbol{w}\rangle,

for every
\boldsymbol{w}\in C^{1}_{D}(\overline{\Omega})^{d}
and a.e.
t\in(0,T)
, where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{(\mathcal{M}(\overline{\Omega}),C(\overline{\Omega}))}
denotes the duality pairing between
\mathcal{M}(\overline{\Omega})
and
C(\overline{\Omega})
. In the spirit of
[
3
]
, to define the error on
\Gamma_{N}
, we define the normal component the stresses
\mathbf{T}(t)
and
\overline{\mathbf{T}}(t)
on the Neumann boundary, respectively, by

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{T}(t)\mathbf{n},\boldsymbol{w}\rangle|_{C^{1}_{0}(\Gamma_{N})}=\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}(t)\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}+\mathbf{T}(t):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}})\,\mathrm{d}x,

and

\displaystyle\langle\overline{\mathbf{T}}(t)\mathbf{n},\boldsymbol{w}\rangle_{C^{1}_{0}(\Gamma_{N})}=\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}(t)\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}\,\mathrm{d}x+\langle\overline{\mathbf{T}}(t),\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}\rangle_{(\mathcal{M}(\overline{\Omega}),C(\overline{\Omega}))},

for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
and every
\boldsymbol{w}\in C^{1}_{0}(\Gamma_{N})^{d}
, where
\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}\in C^{1}_{D}(\overline{\Omega})^{d}
is an extension of
\boldsymbol{w}
to
\overline{\Omega}
such that it vanishes on
\Gamma_{D}
.
Such an extension exists by the definition of
C^{1}_{0}(\Gamma_{N})^{d}
.
Using (
3.50
) and (
3.51
), the definitions of
\mathbf{T}(t)\mathbf{n}
and
\overline{\mathbf{T}}(t)\mathbf{n}
as above are independent of the choice of extension
\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}
.
Furthermore, by the discussion in Section
1.1
, we can choose the extension such that
\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{w}}\|_{1,\infty}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{w}\|_{C^{1}_{0}(\Gamma_{N})}
, where
C
depends only on
\Omega
. Hence,
\mathbf{T}(t)\mathbf{n}
,
\overline{\mathbf{T}}(t)\mathbf{n}
are well-defined elements of the dual space
(C^{1}_{0}(\Gamma_{N})^{d})^{*}
.
Moreover, by the stated regularity results, we have
\mathbf{T}(t)\mathbf{n}
,
\overline{\mathbf{T}}(t)\mathbf{n}\in L^{\infty}_{w^{*}}(0,T;(C^{1}_{0}(\Gamma_{N})^{d})^{*})
. Then we define the penalisation
\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}}
on
(0,T)\times\Gamma_{N}
by

(3.52)

\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}}(t),\boldsymbol{w}\rangle=\langle\overline{\mathbf{T}}(t)\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{T}(t)\mathbf{n},\boldsymbol{w}\rangle,

for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
, for every
\boldsymbol{w}\in C^{1}_{0}(\Gamma_{N})^{d}
.
Using (
3.50
), (
3.51
) and (
3.52
), it follows that (
3.48
) holds.
Hence we conclude the existence of a solution to the elastodynamic equation with strain-limiting constitutive relation, up to the Neumann boundary condition.
∎

Proposition 3.5
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
3.2
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{u},\mathbf{T},v,\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}})
be the solution quadruple constructed there. For a.e.
t\in(0,T)
,
v(t)
is the unique solution of the minimisation problem

(3.53)

\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}(t),v(t))+\mathcal{H}(v(t))+\mathcal{G}_{k}(v(t),v_{t}(t))=\inf_{v\in H^{k}_{D+1}(\Omega),\,v\leq v(t)}\Big\{\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}(t),v)+\mathcal{H}(v)+\mathcal{G}_{k}(v,v_{t}(t))\Big\}.

Furthermore,
v_{t}\leq 0
a.e. in
Q
.

Proof.

Since
v^{n}_{t}\leq 0
in
Q
and weak convergence preserves ordering, it is immediate that
v_{t}\leq 0
a.e. in
Q
. To prove (
3.53
), it is enough to show, for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
and every
\chi\in H^{k}_{D}(\Omega)
such that
\chi\leq 0
, that the following holds:

(3.54)

0\leq\big[\partial_{v}\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}(t),v(t))+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v(t))+\partial_{v}\mathcal{G}_{k}(v(t),v_{t}(t))\big](\chi).

For every
n
and every test function
\chi
as above, we know that the solution of the regularised problem satisfies

\displaystyle 0\leq\big[\partial_{v}\mathcal{E}_{n}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(t),v^{n}(t))+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v^{n}(t))+\partial_{v}\mathcal{G}_{k}(v^{n}(t),v_{t}^{n}(t))\big](\chi).

Using the convergence results of Theorem
3.2
, we immediately deduce that

(3.55)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\psi\big[\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v^{n}(t))+\partial_{v}\mathcal{G}_{k}(v^{n}(t),v_{t}^{n}(t))\big](\chi)\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{T}\psi\big[\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v(t))+\partial_{v}\mathcal{G}_{k}(v(t),v_{t}(t))\big](\chi)\,\mathrm{d}t,

for a fixed but arbitrary
\psi\in C([0,T])
such that
\psi\geq 0
.

For the nonlinear term, first we claim that
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u})
pointwise a.e. in
Q
. Recalling that
(\boldsymbol{u}^{n})_{n}
converges weakly in
W^{1,2}(0,T;W^{2,2}_{loc}(\Omega)^{d})
and applying the Aubin–Lions lemma, it follows that
\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{u}
strongly in
C([0,T];W^{1,2}_{loc}(\Omega)^{d})
. Considering an exhaustion of
\Omega
yields the required pointwise convergence result.

Next, we claim that
(\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n})))_{n}
converges pointwise a.e. on
Q
to
\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}))
.
Using that
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u})=F(\mathbf{T})
with the memory kernel property and the safety strain condition (
3.5
), we have

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}(t))|

\displaystyle=\Big|\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{\alpha(s-t)}F(\mathbf{T}(s))|\,\mathrm{d}s\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}}{\alpha}C_{*}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{\alpha(s-t)}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha}\Big(1+\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha T}(C_{*}-1)\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{**}}{\alpha},

where
C_{**}\in(0,1)
. In particular,
\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u})\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}\leq C_{**}<1
. For a.e.
(t,x)\in Q
, for
n
sufficiently large (depending on
(t,x)
) we have that
|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(t,x))|
is bounded away from
1
as a consequence of the pointwise convergence result. The function
\varphi^{*}
is well-defined and finite on the open unit ball. Also
\varphi^{*}_{n}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow\varphi^{*}(\mathbf{T})
as
n\rightarrow\infty
for every
|\mathbf{T}|<1
. Thus, we deduce that

\displaystyle\varphi^{*}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}))\rightarrow\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}))\quad\text{ pointwise a.e. in }Q.

Combining this with the pointwise convergence of
(v^{n})_{n}
, we get that

\psi\frac{b^{\prime}(v^{n})}{\alpha}\chi\varphi^{*}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}))\rightarrow\psi\frac{b^{\prime}(v)}{\alpha}\chi\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}))\quad\text{ pointwise a.e. in }Q.

The sequence on the left is non-positive a.e. in
Q
, using the non-positivity of
\chi
. Hence, by the reverse Fatou lemma, we deduce that

(3.56)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\psi\partial_{v}\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}(t),v(t))(\chi)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{Q}\psi\frac{b^{\prime}(v)}{\alpha}\chi\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}))\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{Q}\psi\frac{b^{\prime}(v^{n})}{\alpha}\chi\varphi^{*}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{n}))\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t.

Combining (
3.56
) with (
3.55
) and recalling that
\psi
is arbitrary, we see that (
3.54
) holds and so we conclude the stated result.
∎

Using the pointwise convergence results, Fatou’s lemma and the weak lower semi-continuity of norms, taking the limit as
n\rightarrow\infty
in the energy-dissipation equality for the regularised solution immediately yields the following result.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
3.2
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{u},\mathbf{T},v,\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}})
be the solution quadruple constructed there. For every
t\in[0,T]
, the following energy-dissipation inequality holds:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(t;\boldsymbol{u}(t),\boldsymbol{u}_{t}(t),v(t))+\int_{0}^{t}\|v_{t}(s)\|_{k,2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\langle l_{t}(s),\boldsymbol{u}(s)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}b(v)\big[F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}))-F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}))\big]:\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{F}(0;\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{1},v_{0}).

Before trying to obtain the opposite inequality, we discuss equivalent formulations of the energy-dissipation equality to motivate our proof in the case of fully Dirichlet boundary conditions, as well as to see why it fails in the case of mixed boundary conditions. We note that we cannot adapt the proof of Proposition
2.6
to this setting due to the lack of satisfaction of the elastodynamic equation without a penalisation term.

Differentiating and carefully applying the chain rule for weak derivatives, an equivalent formulation of the energy-dissipation equality is

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\Big\{\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{t}(t)+b(v(t))\mathbf{T}(t):\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u}_{t}(t))\,\mathrm{d}x-\langle l(t),\boldsymbol{u}_{t}(t)\rangle\Big\}

\displaystyle\quad+\Big[\int_{\Omega}\frac{b^{\prime}(v(t))v_{t}(t)}{\alpha}\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}(t)))+\frac{1}{2\epsilon}(v(t)-1)v_{t}(t)+2\epsilon\nabla v(t)\cdot\nabla v_{t}(t)\,\mathrm{d}x+\|v_{t}(t)\|_{k,2}^{2}\Big].

The terms in the curly brackets correspond to the elastodynamic equation, and the second set of brackets is exactly

\big[\partial_{v}\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{u}(t),v(t))+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v(t))+\partial_{v}\mathcal{G}_{k}(v(t),v_{t}(t))\big](v_{t}(t))

The latter collection of terms in bounded below by zero. Hence, to show that the sum of terms in the square brackets vanishes, we must prove that

(3.57)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{b^{\prime}(v(t))v_{t}(t)}{\alpha}\varphi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\alpha\boldsymbol{u}(t)))+\frac{1}{2\epsilon}(v(t)-1)v_{t}(t)+2\epsilon\nabla v(t)\cdot\nabla v_{t}(t)\,\mathrm{d}x+\|v_{t}(t)\|_{k,2}^{2}\leq 0.

If (
3.57
) holds, the energy-dissipation equality reduces to showing that

(3.58)
